3-16

Let
U,V\sim{\cal{U}}(-1,1)
are independent random variables distributed jointly on
(-1,1)\times(-1,1)
(shown by a square in Figure
3.2
).

Figure 3.2:
Components of random vector
(U,V)^{\top}
come independently form
{\cal{U}}(-1,1)
.

It may bee seen that the probability of falling a point inside the circle in Figure
3.2
is
\pi/4
. Hence, the constant
\pi
is approximately as

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}=4\times\frac{{\text{number of pairs lie inside the circle}}}{{\text{number of pairs do not lie inside the circle}}}.

(3.20)

Therefore, the constant
\pi
can be estimated by simulating a sufficiently large (
n
, say) numbers of point distributed uniformly on
(-1,1)\times(-1,1)
and then computing the ratio
3.20
. To this end, we define

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{{\cal{C}}}(u_{i},v_{i})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle 1,\penalty\ \mathrm{{if}}\ i{\text{th pair}}\penalty\ (u_{i},v_{i})^{\top}\penalty\ {\text{lies inside the circle}},\\
\displaystyle 0,\penalty\ \mathrm{if}\ i{\text{th pair}}\penalty\ (u_{i},v_{i})^{\top}\penalty\ {\text{lies outside the circle}},\end{array}\right.

and then, based on the law of large numbers, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}=4\times\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}_{{\cal{C}}}(u_{i},v_{i})}{n}.

(3.24)

It is easy to check that
E(\hat{\pi})=\pi
and
var(\hat{\pi})=4\pi(1-\pi/4)/n=2.69676/n
. Here, we interested in improving estimator
\hat{\pi}
by conditioning on marginal variable
U
as follows.

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E\bigl[\mathbb{I}_{{\cal{C}}}(U_{i},V_{i})\big|U_{i}=u_{i}\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}P\bigl[U^{2}_{i}+V^{2}_{i}\leq 1\big|U_{i}=u_{i}\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}P\bigl[V^{2}_{i}\leq 1-u^{2}_{i}\big|U_{i}=u_{i}\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}P\bigl[V^{2}_{i}\leq 1-u^{2}_{i}\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{1-u^{2}_{i}},

where
U_{i}\sim{\cal{U}}(-1,1)
. This yields

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{1-U^{2}_{i}}.

Therefore,
\hat{\pi}_{1}
is obtained, first by generating
u_{1},\cdots,u_{n}\sim{\cal{U}}(-1,1)
, and then computing
\hat{\pi}_{1}=4/n\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{1-u^{2}_{i}}
. It is easy to check that

\begin{split}E\bigl(\hat{\pi}_{1}\bigr)&=\frac{4}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E\Bigl(\sqrt{1-U^{2}_{i}}\Bigr)\\
&=\frac{4}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{-1}^{1}\sqrt{1-y^{2}}\frac{1}{2}dy\\
&=\frac{4}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E\Bigl(\sqrt{1-Z^{2}_{i}}\Bigr)\\
&=\pi,\end{split}\begin{split}var\bigl(\hat{\pi}_{1}\bigr)&=\frac{16}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Bigl[E\bigl(1-Z_{i}^{2}\bigr)-\Bigl(\frac{\pi}{4}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigr],\\
&=\frac{16}{n}\Bigl[\frac{2}{3}-\Bigl(\frac{\pi}{4}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigr]\\
&=\frac{0.79706}{n}.\\
\\
\\
\end{split}

where
Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{n}\sim{\cal{U}}(0,1)
. As it is seen,
\hat{\pi}_{1}
yields around 70.44% reduction in variance for estimating
\pi
compared to
\hat{\pi}
. Yet the performance of estimator
\hat{\pi}_{1}
can be improved using the method introduced in Section
3.3
. It follows from above that quantity

\displaystyle\frac{4}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{1-Z^{2}_{i}}

with variance
{0.79706/n}
. We know that
Z\sim 1-Z\sim{\cal{U}}(0,1)
and so the quantity

\displaystyle\frac{4}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{1-(1-Z)^{2}_{i}}

is also unbiased estimator for
\pi
with the same variance. But, evidently
Z
and
1-Z
are correlated negatively and hence

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}_{2}=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{1-Z^{2}_{i}}+\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{1-(1-Z_{i})^{2}},

is an unbiased estimator for
\pi
whose variance is smaller than that of
\hat{\pi}_{1}
.

Figure 3.3:
The bias (a) and rmse (b) of
\hat{\pi}
,
\hat{\pi}_{1}
, and
\hat{\pi}_{2}
computed based on
N=1000
independent samples of size
n
.

As it may seen from Figure
3.3
, the
\hat{\pi}_{2}
outperforms
\hat{\pi}_{1}
and
\hat{\pi}
, in terms of both bias and rmse criteria. The pertinent
R

\hat{\pi}
,
\hat{\pi}_{1}
, and
\hat{\pi}_{2}

3.4
Computing the CDF of Gaussian distribution

In this section, we proceed to provide the compute the Monte Carlo approximation for the CDF of the Gaussian distribution.

3.4.1
Computing the CDF of univariate Gaussian distribution

Indeed computing the CDF of a Gaussian distribution is an importance sampling problem. Following example gives more detailed information.

3-17

Let
Z\sim{\cal{N}}(0,1)
and we are interested in computing
P(Z<2)
. Evidently the given probability can be represented as

\displaystyle P(Z<2)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{2}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{z^{2}}{2}\Bigr\}dz

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathbb{I}_{(-\infty,2)}(z)\times\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{z^{2}}{2}\Bigr\}dz

\displaystyle=E\Bigl(\mathbb{I}_{(-\infty,2)}(Z)\Bigr),

(3.25)

where
\mathbb{I}_{{\cal{S}}}(x)
is the well-known indicator function defined as

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{{\cal{S}}}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle 1,\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{{if}}\ x\in{\cal{S}},\\
\displaystyle 0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{if}\ x\notin{\cal{S}}.\end{array}\right.

Hence, the quantity can be readily approximated by considering the standard univariate Gaussian as the instrumental distribution. It follows, based on the law of large numbers, that

\displaystyle P(Z\leq 2)

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{I}_{(-\infty,2)}\bigl(z_{i}\bigr)=\frac{{\text{number of $z_{i}$s less or equal 2 in a sample of size $N$}}}{N},

(3.29)

where
z_{1},\cdots,z_{N}
independently follow
{\cal{N}}(0,1)
and
N
is a sufficiently large integer value. This means that the Monte Carlo approximation of
P(Z<2)
given in (
3.29
) is the average of samples that are less than two. The Central limit theorem guarantees that estimator (
3.29
) is unbiased for
P(Z<2)
with variance

\displaystyle\frac{\Phi(2|0,1)\bigl[1-\Phi(2|0,1)\bigr]}{N}.

Of course, the command
integrate(f,lower,upper,...)
(when
f
is the PDF of Gaussian distribution) or
pnorm(q,mean=0,sd=1,...)
can be used in
R
environment for computing the CDF of Gaussian distribution. However, one could use the well known
Gaussian quadrature
rules discussed in next Chapter.

3.5
Computing the CDF of multivariate Gaussian distribution

Due to significant importance of the multivariate Gaussian distribution in a wide range of study fields, computing its CDF has a long history. Several efforts have been made for computing the CDF of multivariate Gaussian distribution. For more detailed information, we refer the reader to
Botev (2017)
and references therein. One of the pioneers in this context was
Genz (1992)
who suggested the separation of variables (SOV) method. Let
\bm{X}\sim{\cal{N}}_{p}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma)
and
L
denote the Cholesky decomposition of
\Sigma
, that is
\Sigma=LL^{\top}
in which
L
is a
p\times p
lower triangular matrix. For computing
{\cal{P}}=P(\bm{a}<\bm{X}<\bm{b})
, in which
\bm{a}
and
\bm{b}
are vectors of real constants, the SOV method can be described as follows. Consider transformation
\bm{x}=L\bm{y}
, we have

\displaystyle\bm{x}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bm{x}=\bm{y}^{\top}L^{\top}L^{-\top}L^{-1}L\bm{y}=\bm{y}^{\top}\bm{y}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}y^{2}_{i},

and
d\bm{x}=|L|d\bm{y}=\sqrt{|\Sigma|}d\bm{y}
. Hence,
\bm{a}<L\bm{y}<\bm{b}
or equivalently

\displaystyle\frac{a_{1}}{L_{1,1}}\leq

\displaystyle{y}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{b_{1}}{L_{1,1}}

\displaystyle\frac{a_{2}-L_{2,1}y_{1}}{L_{2,2}}\leq

\displaystyle{y}_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{b_{2}-L_{2,1}y_{1}}{L_{2,2}}

\displaystyle\frac{a_{p}-\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}L_{p,i}y_{i}}{L_{p,p}}\leq

\displaystyle{y}_{p}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{b_{p}-\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}L_{p,i}y_{i}}{L_{p,p}}.

This type of change of variable reduces the problem of computing
{\cal{P}}
to
p
univariate integration as follows.

\displaystyle{\cal{P}}=(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\int_{a_{1}^{\prime}}^{b_{1}^{\prime}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{y_{1}^{2}}{2}\Bigr\}\int_{a_{2}^{\prime}}^{b_{2}^{\prime}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{y_{2}^{2}}{2}\Bigr\}\cdots\int_{a_{p}^{\prime}}^{b_{p}^{\prime}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{y_{m}^{2}}{2}\Bigr\}d\bm{y},

(3.30)

where
a^{\prime}_{i}=\bigl(a_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}L_{i,j}y_{j}\bigr)/L_{i,i}
and
b^{\prime}_{i}=\bigl(b_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}L_{i,j}y_{j}\bigr)/L_{i,i}
. By applying another transformation of the form
z_{i}=\Phi\bigl(y_{i}\bigr)
(for
i=1,\cdots,p
), we obtain

\displaystyle{\cal{P}}=\int_{d_{1}}^{e_{1}}\int_{d_{2}}^{e_{2}}\cdots\int_{d_{p}}^{e_{p}}d\bm{z},

(3.31)

where

\displaystyle d_{i}=

\displaystyle\Phi\biggl(\frac{a_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}L_{i,j}\Phi^{-1}(z_{j})}{L_{i,i}}\biggr),

\displaystyle e_{i}=

\displaystyle\Phi\biggl(\frac{b_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}L_{i,j}\Phi^{-1}(z_{j})}{L_{i,i}}\biggr).

Although the integrand in (
3.31
) has a simpler form than (
3.30
), but its integral region is complicated. As a solution for this issue, we apply the transformation
w_{i}=(z_{i}-d_{i})/(e_{i}-d_{i})
to see that

\displaystyle{\cal{P}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(e^{\prime}_{1}-d^{\prime}_{1}\bigr)d{w_{1}}\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(e^{\prime}_{2}-d^{\prime}_{2}\bigr)d{w_{2}}\cdots\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(e^{\prime}_{p-1}-d^{\prime}_{p-1}\bigr)d{w_{p-1}}\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(e^{\prime}_{p}-d^{\prime}_{p}\bigr)d{w_{p}},

\displaystyle=\bigl(e^{\prime}_{1}-d^{\prime}_{1}\bigr)\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(e^{\prime}_{2}-d^{\prime}_{2}\bigr)d{w_{1}}\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(e^{\prime}_{3}-d^{\prime}_{3}\bigr)d{w_{2}}\cdots\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(e^{\prime}_{p}-d^{\prime}_{p}\bigr)d{w_{p-1}}\int_{0}^{1}d{w_{p}},

\displaystyle=\bigl(e^{\prime}_{1}-d^{\prime}_{1}\bigr)\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(e^{\prime}_{2}-d^{\prime}_{2}\bigr)d{w_{1}}\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(e^{\prime}_{3}-d^{\prime}_{3}\bigr)d{w_{2}}\cdots\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(e^{\prime}_{p}-d^{\prime}_{p}\bigr)d{w_{p-1}},

(3.32)

where

\displaystyle d^{\prime}_{i}=

\displaystyle\Phi\biggl(\frac{a_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}L_{i,j}\Phi^{-1}\bigl(d_{j}+w_{j}(e_{j}-d_{j})\bigr)}{L_{i,i}}\biggr),

\displaystyle e^{\prime}_{i}=

\displaystyle\Phi\biggl(\frac{b_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}L_{i,j}\Phi^{-1}\bigl(d_{j}+w_{j}(e_{j}-d_{j})\bigr)}{L_{i,i}}\biggr).

It is worth to note that in the RHS of (
3.32
) the integrand within
i
th integral do not depend on
w_{i}
and so moves back as an integrand to
(i-1)
th integral. Henceforth, the number of integrals is
p-1
. Furthermore, the quantity
e^{\prime}_{1}-d^{\prime}_{1}
is independent of
w
.

For computing
{\cal{P}}
in (
3.32
), the method proposed by
Genz (1992)
approximates
{\cal{P}}
through the Monte Carlo method in which
w_{i}
(for
i=1,\cdots,p-1
) is drawn from
{\cal{U}}(0,1)
as the instrumental distribution. This method as originally developed for zero-mean Gaussian distribution. In general, when
\bm{\mu}
of, the constants
\bm{a}
and
\bm{b}
are replaced with
\bm{a}-\bm{\mu}
and
\bm{b}-\bm{\mu}
, respectively. The following algorithm describes the method of
Genz (1992)
in more details.

Algorithm 10

Computing CDF of the multivariate Gaussian distribution

1:

Read
\bm{a}
,
\bm{b}
,
\bm{\mu}
,
\Sigma
,
\epsilon
, and
{\text{N}}_{max}
;

2:

Compute the Cholesky decomposition
L
of
\Sigma
;

3:

Set
{\text{Sum}}_{int}=0
,
{\text{Var}}_{int}=0
, and
\alpha=2.5
;

4:

Set
\bm{b}=\bm{b}-\bm{\mu}
and
\bm{a}=\bm{a}-\bm{\mu}
;

5:

Set
e_{1}=\Phi\bigl(b_{1}/L_{1,1}\bigr)
,
d_{1}=\Phi\bigl(a_{1}/L_{1,1}\bigr)
, and
f_{1}=e_{1}-d_{1}
;

6:

Set
N=0
and
{\text{Error}}=10
;

7:

while

{\text{Error}}\geq\epsilon
and
N<{\text{N}}_{max}

do

8:

Generate realizations
w_{1},w_{2},\cdots,w_{p-1}
from
{\cal{U}}(0,1)
;

9:

Set
i=2
;

10:

while

i\leq p

do

11:

Set
j=i-1
;

12:

y_{j}=\Phi^{-1}\Bigl(d_{j}+w_{j}\bigl(e_{j}-d_{j}\bigr)\Bigr)
;

13:

L_{y}=\sum_{k=1}^{j}L_{i,k}\times y_{k}
;

14:

d_{i}=\Phi\Bigl(\frac{a_{i}-L_{y}}{L_{i,i}}\Bigr)
;

15:

e_{i}=\Phi\Bigl(\frac{b_{i}-L_{y}}{L_{i,i}}\Bigr)
;

16:

f_{i}=\bigl(e_{i}-d_{i}\bigr)f_{j}
;

17:

Set
i=i+1
;

18:

end

19:

N=N+1
;

20:

\delta=\frac{f_{p}-{\text{Sum}}_{int}}{N}
;

21:

{\text{Sum}}_{int}={\text{Sum}}_{int}+\delta
;

22:

{\text{Var}}_{int}=(N-2)\frac{{\text{Var}}_{int}}{N}+\delta^{2}
;

23:

{\text{Error}}=\alpha\sqrt{{\text{Var}}_{int}}
;

24:

end

25:

Return
{\text{Sum}}_{int}
as an estimation of CDF of multivariate Gaussian distribution.

3.6
Univariate Gaussian quadrature

The
quadrature rules
involve methods for approximating an integral

\displaystyle I=\int_{a}^{b}f(x)dx\approx{\cal{Q}}(f)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\omega_{i}f\bigl(x_{i}\bigr)

(3.33)

where quantities
\omega_{i}
and
x_{i}
are the
i
th
weight
and
node
of the quadrature rule, respectively. The idea behind a quadrature rule is to approximate the integrand
f(x)
through a simple function. Reasonably, an appropriate choice is a polynomial
P(x)
, of degree not greater than
k-1
that interpolate
f(x)
at
x_{i}
(for
i=1,\cdot,k
). Suppose, for a given function
f(x)
, we are interested in approximating the integral of the form
f(x)
over interval
(a,b)
using a two-point (
k=2
) quadrature rule. We have

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}f(x)dx\approx\omega_{1}f\bigl(x_{1}\bigr)+\omega_{2}f\bigl(x_{2}\bigr),

(3.34)

where the value of above integral is known when all four unknown constants including
\omega_{1}
(first weight),
\omega_{2}
(second weight),
x_{1}
(first node), and
x_{2}
(second node) are determined. To this end, we assume
f(x)
is a polynomial of degree three represented as
f(x)=a_{0}+a_{1}x+a_{2}x^{2}+a_{3}x^{3}
. Although we can let for larger values of
m
, but we set
m=2
for simplicity. So, substituting the third-order polynomial
f(x)=a_{0}+a_{1}x+a_{2}x^{2}+a_{3}x^{3}
into the LHS of (
3.34
), it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}f(x)\ dx

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{b}a_{0}+a_{1}x+a_{2}x^{2}+a_{3}x^{3}dx

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}a_{0}x+a_{1}\frac{x^{2}}{2}+a_{2}\frac{x^{3}}{3}+a_{3}\frac{x^{4}}{4}\end{bmatrix}_{a}^{b}

\displaystyle=a_{0}(b-a)+a_{1}\Bigl(\frac{b^{2}-a^{2}}{2}\Bigr)+a_{2}\Bigl(\frac{b^{3}-a^{3}}{3}\Bigr)+a_{3}\Bigl(\frac{b^{4}-a^{4}}{4}\Bigr).

(3.35)

Representing the RHS of (
3.34
) in terms of third-order polynomial yields

\displaystyle\omega_{1}f(x_{1})+\omega_{2}f(x_{2})=\omega_{1}\bigl(a_{0}+a_{1}x_{1}+a_{2}x_{1}^{2}+a_{3}x_{1}^{3}\bigr)+\omega_{2}\bigl(a_{0}+a_{1}x_{2}+a_{2}x_{2}^{2}+a_{3}x_{2}^{3}\bigr).

(3.36)

Equating the RHSs of (
3.35
) and (
3.36
) yields a set of four equations as follows.

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}b-a=\omega_{1}+\omega_{2},\\
\frac{b^{2}-a^{2}}{2}=\omega_{1}x_{1}+\omega_{2}x_{2},\\
\frac{b^{3}-a^{3}}{3}=\omega_{1}{x_{1}}^{2}+\omega_{2}{x_{2}}^{2},\\
\frac{b^{4}-a^{4}}{4}=\omega_{1}{x_{1}}^{3}+\omega_{2}{x_{2}}^{3}.\end{array}\right.

Solving the set of four equations given in (
3.6
), we have

\displaystyle\omega_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{b-a}{2},

\displaystyle\omega_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{b-a}{2},

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\Bigl(\frac{b-a}{2}\Bigr)+\frac{b+a}{2},

\displaystyle x_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\Bigl(\frac{b-a}{2}\Bigr)+\frac{b+a}{2}.

(3.41)

Substitute the RHS of (
3.41
) into the RHS of (
3.34
) to see that

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}f(x)dx

\displaystyle\approx\omega_{1}f\bigl(x_{1}\bigr)+\omega_{2}f\left(x_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{b-a}{2}f\Bigl(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{b-a}{2}+\frac{b+a}{2}\Bigr)+\frac{b-a}{2}f\Bigl(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{b-a}{2}+\frac{b+a}{2}\Bigr).

(3.42)

Following example shows how much a quadrature can be reliable.

3-18

Let
f(x)=x\exp\{-x\}
and we would like to approximate

\displaystyle I=\int_{0}^{3}x\exp\{-x\}dx,

(3.43)

based on Gaussian quadrature of
m=2
points. We compute two weights and nodes through (
3.6
). It is easy to check that

\displaystyle I=\approx 1.5\times f(0.63397)+1.5\times f(2.36602)=0.83755,

(3.44)

where the exact value of above integral is
0.80085
. Figure
3.4
displays the integrand and the corresponding nodes.

Figure 3.4:
Plot of integrand
f(x)=x\exp\{-x\}
and nodes
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. The area under the integrand is shown by shaded area.

The above reasoning can be extended for
k>2
that yields more accurate approximation of integral (
3.34
). In general, using the quadrature as described above, one can approximate the given definite integral using a quadrature rule with an arbitrary
k
numbers of nodes and weights. In general, we can write

\displaystyle I=\int_{a}^{b}f(x)dx=\int_{a}^{b}P(x)dx=\int_{a}^{b}\sum_{i=1}^{k}L_{i}(x)f\bigl(x_{i}\bigr)dx=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\omega_{i}f\bigl(x_{i}\bigr),

where

\displaystyle\omega_{i}=\int_{a}^{b}L_{i}(x)dx,

in which
L_{i}(x)
is the well-known Lagrange interpolating polynomial of order
k-1
. defined as

\displaystyle L_{i}(x)=\prod_{j=1,j\neq i}^{n}\frac{x-x_{i}}{x_{j}-x_{i}}.

In what follows, among several quadrature rules introduced in the literature, we briefly describe the
Gaussian quadrature
rules for computing a definite integral.

3.6.1
Gaussian quadrature rules

The quadrature rule presented in the previous sub-section works based on a general polynomial. Herein, we describe the well-known
Gaussian quadrature
that works based on a special class of polynomials known as the
orthogonal polynomials
and can be applied for approximating accurately a wide range of definite integrals
(
Dahlquist and Björck, 2003
)
. For presentation and theoretical purposes, the following definitions are useful.

Definition 3.6.1
.

(
Foupouagnigni and Koepf, 2018
)
The function
w(\cdot):(a,b)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
that satisfies the following three conditions is said to be a weight function.

i.

w(x)
is measurable.

ii.

The
k
th moment of
w(x)
must be finite, that is
\int_{a}^{b}|x|^{k}w(x)dx<\infty
for
k=0,1,2,\cdots
.

iii.

For a given polynomial such as
g(x)
if
\int_{a}^{b}g(x)w(x)dx=0
then we must have
g(x)=0
.

Definition 3.6.2
.

(
Foupouagnigni and Koepf, 2018
)
The sequence of polynomials
\{f_{0}(x)
,
f_{1}(x)
,
f_{2}(x)
,
\cdots\}
constitutes a sequence of orthogonal polynomials with respect to weight function
w(\cdot):(a,b)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
if we have

\displaystyle\langle f_{n},f_{m}\rangle=\int_{a}^{b}f_{n}(x)f_{m}(x)w(x)dx={\text{C}}_{n}\times{\cal{D}}_{m,n},

where
\langle.,.\rangle
represents the inner product operator,
{\text{C}}_{n}
is a constant, and
{\cal{D}}_{m,n}
is the Kronecker delta defined as

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\cal{D}}_{m,n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle 1,\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{{if}}\ m=n,\\
\displaystyle 0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{if}\ m\neq n.\end{array}\right.

There are several types of the orthogonal polynomials such as
Chebyshev of first kind
(
T_{1,n}
),
Chebyshev of second kind
(
T_{2,n}
),
generalized Laguerre
(
{\cal{GL}}_{n}^{\alpha}
),
Hermite
(
H_{n}
),
Jacobi
(
J_{n}^{\alpha,\beta}
),
Laguerre
(
{\cal{L}}_{n}
), and
Legendre
(
L_{n}
), to name a few. In general, the orthogonal polynomials are solution of differential equation and obtained through a recurrence formula. In what follows, we describe briefly the
Hermite
and
Laguerre
classes of orthogonal polynomials with widespread applications in statistics.

•

Hermite polynomial
: the Hermite polynomial of degree
n
, shown by
H_{n}(x)
,
is orthogonal with respect to weight function
w(x)=\exp\{-x^{2}\}
. This means that

\displaystyle\langle H_{n},H_{m}\rangle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}H_{n}(x)H_{m}(x)\exp\bigl\{-{x^{2}}{}\bigr\}dx=2^{n}\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma(n+1)\times{\cal{D}}_{m,n}.

(3.46)

The expansion representation for
H_{n}(x)
is

\displaystyle H_{n}(x)=\Gamma(n+1)\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\frac{(-1)^{i}(2x)^{n-2i}}{\Gamma(i+1)\Gamma(n-2i+1)},

(3.47)

where
\lfloor n/2\rfloor
denotes the greatest integer less or equal
n/2
(for
n=0,1,2,\cdots
). This class of orthogonal polynomials satisfies with the recurrence relations

\displaystyle\frac{dH_{n}(x)}{dx}-2xH_{n}(x)+H_{n+1}(x)=0,

for
n\geq 0
and

\displaystyle H_{n+1}(x)-2xH_{n}(x)+2nH_{n-1}(x)=0,

(3.48)

for
n\geq 1
.
Using expression (
3.48
),
the first six members of Hermite polynomials are as follows
(
Arfken et al., 2011
)
.

\displaystyle H_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle H_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=2x,

\displaystyle H_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=4x^{2}-2,

\displaystyle H_{3}(x)

\displaystyle=8x^{3}-12x,

\displaystyle H_{4}(x)

\displaystyle=16x^{4}-48x^{2}+12,

\displaystyle H_{5}(x)

\displaystyle=32x^{5}-160x^{3}+120x.

Figure 3.5:
Graph of (a): Hermite and (b): Legendre polynomials of orders
n=2
(solid orange line),
n=3
(dashed black line),
n=4
(dotted red line), and
n=5
(dotted-dashed blue line).

Figure
3.5
displays the graph of Hermite and Legendre polynomials of orders
n=2,3,4,5
.

•

Laguerre polynomial:
The Laguerre polynomial of degree
n
, shown by
{\cal{L}}_{n}(x)
, is orthogonal with respect to weight function
w(x)=\exp\{-x\}
. This means that

\displaystyle\langle{\cal{L}}_{n},{\cal{L}}_{m}\rangle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\cal{L}}_{n}(x){\cal{L}}_{m}(x)\exp\{-x\}dx={\cal{D}}_{m,n}.

(3.49)

This class of orthogonal polynomials is solution of the differential equation

\displaystyle x\frac{d^{2}{\cal{L}}_{n}(x)}{dx^{2}}+(1-x)\frac{d{\cal{L}}_{n}(x)}{dx}+n{\cal{L}}_{n}(x)=0,

(3.50)

whose solution becomes

\displaystyle{\cal{L}}_{n}(x)=\Gamma(n+1)\sum_{i=0}^{n}\frac{(-1)^{i}x^{i}}{\Gamma(n-i+1)\bigl[\Gamma(i+1)]^{2}}.

(3.51)

Using expression (
3.51
), the first six members of Laguerre polynomials are as follows.

\displaystyle{\cal{L}}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle{\cal{L}}_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=1-x,

\displaystyle{\cal{L}}_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{2}-4x+2}{2},

\displaystyle{\cal{L}}_{3}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{x^{3}-9x^{2}+18x-6}{6},

\displaystyle{\cal{L}}_{4}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{4}-16x^{3}+72x^{2}-96x+24}{24},

\displaystyle{\cal{L}}_{5}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{-x^{5}+25x^{4}-200x^{3}+600x^{2}-600x+120}{120}.

Table
3.3
shows a list of most commonly used orthogonal polynomials in statistics.

1

There are different types of the Gaussian quadrature rules have been developed in the literature for approximating the integral (
3.52
). Particular Gauss quadrature rule arises from particular choice of the interval and the form of the weight function
w(x)
. For example, the Gauss-Legendre quadrature rule is applied when

[a,b]=[-1,1]
and
w(x)=1
, and so is employed for approximating integral of the form

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}f(x)dx.

Moreover, for approximating integral of the form

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\{-x\}f(x)dx,

we may use the Gauss-Laguerre quadrature rule since as it is seen the range of integral is
[a,b]=[0,\infty)
and
w(x)=\exp\{-x\}
, and finally, a
k
-point Gauss-Chebyshev rule is useful when
[a,b]=[-1,1]
. The bounds of integral (
3.52
) can be changed from
(a,b)
to
[-1,1]
through a simple transformation when
a\neq-1
and
b\neq 1
. For integrand of the form
w(x)f(x)
, in which
w(x)
is given in Table
3.3
, an orthogonal polynomial up to degree
k+1
is fitted to integrand
w(x)f(x)
that intercepts integrand
w(x)f(x)
at
k
nodes
\{x_{n}\}_{n=1}^{k}
. Once we have found
k
pairs of nodes and associated weights, shown by
\{\bigl(x_{n},\omega_{n}\bigr)\}_{n=1}^{k}
, the integral can be approximated based on a
k
-point Gaussian quadrature rule as

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}w(x)f(x)dx\approx\sum_{n=1}^{k}\omega_{n}f\bigl(x_{n}\bigr).

(3.52)

The nodes and weighs for Gauss-Chebyshev quadrature are determined simply. The class
{{T}}_{1,n}(\cdot)
and
{{T}}_{2,n}(\cdot)
of orthogonal polynomials are given by

\displaystyle\begin{cases}{{T}}_{1,n}(x)=\cos\bigl(n\theta\bigr),&{\text{for first kind,}}\\
{{T}}_{2,n}(x)=\frac{\sin\bigl((n+1)\theta\bigr)}{\sin(\theta)},&{\text{for second kind,}}\end{cases}

(3.53)

where
x=\cos(\theta)
for
\theta\in[0,\pi]

(
Rivlin, 2020
)
. These two classes of orthogonal polynomials admit the recurrence relation

\displaystyle{{T}}_{1,n+1}(x)=2x{{T}}_{1,n}(x)-{{T}}_{1,n-1}(x),\penalty\ \mathrm{for}\penalty\ n\geq 1,

(3.54)

where
{T}_{1,0}(x)=1
and
{T}_{1,1}(x)=x
. The nodes are given by

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x_{n}=\cos\Bigl(\frac{2n-1}{2k}\pi\Bigr),&{\text{for first kind,}}\\
x_{n}=\cos\Bigl(\frac{n}{k+1}\pi\Bigr),&{\text{for second kind,}}\end{cases}

(3.55)

where
n=1,\cdots,k
. The pertaining weights for the first and second kinds are the same and distributed uniformly, that is
\omega_{n}=\pi/k
, for
n=1,\cdots,k
.
The Gaussian quadrature rules based on the other orthogonal polynomials
are determined through the Golub-Welsch algorithm
(
Golub and Welsch, 1969
)
as follows.

3.6.2
Golub-Welsch algorithm

The fundamental Gaussian quadrature theorem states that the nodes
x_{i}
s are the roots of the orthogonal polynomial. It can be shown that each orthogonal polynomial of degree
k
has distinct real roots on interval
[a,b]

(
Shen et al., 2011
, pp. 53)
. There are two simple methods for computing the roots of orthogonal polynomial
(
Shen et al., 2011
, pp. 55)
. Except for the Chebyshev case, the roots of other orthogonal polynomials may be obtained trough the
eigenvalue
or
iterative
method. In what follows, we describe briefly the
eigenvalue method
or
Golub-Welsch algorithm
, see
(
Shen et al., 2011
, pp. 55)
.

•

nodes
: Let
P_{n}(x)
denote the orthogonal polynomial. It can be seen that the orthogonal polynomials given on Table
3.3
admits the recurrence formula as
(
Shen et al., 2011
, Theorem 3.1)
:

\displaystyle xP_{n}(x)=a_{n}P_{n+1}(x)+b_{n}P_{n}(x)+c_{n}P_{n-1}(x),

(3.56)

for
c_{0}P_{-1}(x)=0
. The zeros of the orthogonal polynomial
P_{n}(x)
are eigenvalues of symmetric threediagonal matrix
A
given by
(
Gil et al., 2007
)
:

\displaystyle A=\left(\begin{matrix}b_{0}&\sqrt{a_{0}c_{1}}&&&\\
\sqrt{a_{0}c_{1}}&b_{1}&\sqrt{a_{1}c_{2}}&&\\
&\sqrt{a_{1}c_{2}}&b_{2}&&\\
&&&\ddots&\sqrt{a_{k-2}c_{k-1}}\\
&&&\sqrt{a_{k-2}c_{k-1}}\ &b_{k-1}\end{matrix}\right),

(3.57)

where

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}P_{-1}(x)=0,fori\geq 0.

The eigenvalues
{e}_{1},\cdots,{e}_{k}
of matrix
A
are also nodes of the Gaussian quadrature rule, that is
e_{n}=x_{n}
, for
n=1,\cdots,k
.

•

weight
: Once we have computed the eigenvalues of
A
in (
3.57
), the
n
th eight
\omega_{n}
is obtained as

\displaystyle\omega_{n}=\mu_{0}\frac{\bigl(\bm{v}_{n}[1]\bigr)^{2}}{\lVert\bm{v}_{n}\rVert},

(3.58)

in which

\displaystyle\mu_{0}=\int_{a}^{b}w(x)dx,

\lVert\cdot\rVert
denotes the Euclidean norm,
\{\bm{v}_{1},\cdots,\bm{v}_{k}\}
are the eigenvectors correspond to
\{{e}_{1},\cdots,{e}_{k}\}
, and
\bm{v}_{n}[1]
is the first element of eigenvector
\bm{v}_{n}
.

In what follows, we compute the Gaussian quadrature rule for orthogonal polynomials presented in Table
3.3
.

•

nodes and weighs for generalized Laguerre polynomial
: The class
{\cal{L}}^{\alpha}_{n}(\cdot)
of orthogonal polynomials admits the recurrence relation

\displaystyle(n+1){\cal{L}}^{\alpha}_{n+1}(x)=(2n+\alpha+1-x){\cal{L}}^{\alpha}_{n}(x)-(n+\alpha){\cal{L}}^{\alpha}_{n-1}(x),

(3.59)

where
{\cal{L}}^{\alpha}_{0}(x)=1
. Comparing the recurrence relations given in (
3.56
) and (
3.59
), we obtain
b_{n}=0
, for
n=0,1,2,\cdots
, and

\displaystyle\begin{cases}a_{n}=-(n+1)\penalty\ {\text{and}}\penalty\ b_{n}=(2n+\alpha+1),&{\text{if}}\penalty\ n\geq 0,\\
c_{n}=-(n+\alpha),&{\text{if}}\penalty\ n\geq 1.\end{cases}

(3.60)

In this case, matrix
A
in (
3.57
) can be reconstructed by using the constants
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
, and
c_{n}
provided in (
3.60
) for computing eigenvalues of the reconstructed matrix
A
as the nodes. For computing weights, it follows from Table
3.3
that

\displaystyle\mu_{0}=\int_{a}^{b}w(x)dx=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{\alpha}\exp\{-x\}dx=\Gamma(\alpha+1).

Hence the weights
\omega_{n}
(for
n=1,\cdots,k
) are obtained by considering
\mu_{0}=\Gamma(\alpha+1)
in the RHS of (
3.58
) in which
\bm{v}_{n}
(for
n=1,\cdots,k
) is the eigenvector corresponds to
i
th eigenvalue of matrix
A
constructed based on information given by (
3.60
).

•

nodes and weighs for Hermite polynomial
: The class
{{H}}^{\alpha}_{n}(\cdot)
of orthogonal polynomials admits the recurrence relation given by (
3.48
) for
n\geq 1
.
Comparing the recurrence relations given in (
3.56
) and (
3.48
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{cases}a_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\penalty\ {\text{and}}\penalty\ b_{n}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ &{\text{if}}\ n\geq 0,\\
c_{n}=n,\penalty\ \penalty\ &{\text{if}}\ n\geq 1.\end{cases}

(3.61)

In this case, matrix
A
in (
3.57
) can be reconstructed by using the constants
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
, and
c_{n}
provided in (
3.61
) for computing eigenvalues of the reconstructed matrix
A
as the nodes. For computing weights, it follows from Table
3.3
that

\displaystyle\mu_{0}=\int_{a}^{b}w(x)dx=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\{-{x^{2}}{}\}dx=\sqrt{2\pi}.

Hence the weights
\omega_{n}
(for
n=1,\cdots,k
) are obtained by considering
\mu_{0}=\sqrt{\pi}
in the RHS of (
3.58
) in which
\bm{v}_{n}
(for
n=1,\cdots,k
) is the eigenvector corresponds to
i
th eigenvalue of matrix
A
constructed based on information given by (
3.61
).

•

nodes and weighs for Jacobi polynomial
: The class
J^{\alpha,\beta}_{i}(\cdot)
of orthogonal polynomials admits the recurrence relation

\displaystyle J^{\alpha,\beta}_{n+1}(x)=\bigl(xa^{\alpha,\beta}_{n}-b^{\alpha,\beta}_{n}\bigr)J^{\alpha,\beta}_{n}(x)-c^{\alpha,\beta}_{n}J^{\alpha,\beta}_{n-1}(x),

(3.62)

with
J^{\alpha,\beta}_{0}(x)=1
and
J^{\alpha,\beta}_{1}(x)=x(\alpha+\beta+2)/2+(\alpha-\beta)/2
, for
n=1,2,\cdots
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle a^{\alpha,\beta}_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2n+\alpha+\beta+1)(2n+\alpha+\beta+2)}{2(n+1)(n+\alpha+\beta+1)},

\displaystyle b^{\alpha,\beta}_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2})(2n+\alpha+\beta+1)}{2(n+1)(n+\alpha+\beta+1)(2n+\alpha+\beta)},

\displaystyle c^{\alpha,\beta}_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{(n+\alpha)(n+\beta)(2n+\alpha+\beta+2)}{(n+1)(n+\alpha+\beta+1)(2n+\alpha+\beta)}.

(3.63)

Rearranging the RHS of (
3.62
) and comparing it with the recurrence relation given in (
3.56
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{cases}a_{0}=\frac{2}{\alpha+\beta+2}\penalty\ {\text{and}}\penalty\ b_{0}=\frac{\beta-\alpha}{\alpha+\beta+2},&{\text{if}}\penalty\ \ n=0,\\
a_{n}=\frac{2(n+1)(n+\alpha+\beta+1)}{(2n+\alpha+\beta+1)(2n+\alpha+\beta+2)},&{\text{if}}\penalty\ \ n\geq 1,\\
b_{n}=\frac{\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2}}{(2n+\alpha+\beta)(2n+\alpha+\beta+2)},&{\text{if}}\penalty\ \ n\geq 1,\\
c_{n}=\frac{2(n+\alpha)(n+\beta)}{(2n+\alpha+\beta)(2n+\alpha+\beta+1)},&{\text{if}}\penalty\ \ n\geq 1.\end{cases}

(3.64)

In this case, matrix
A
in (
3.57
) can be reconstructed by using the constants
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
, and
c_{n}
provided in (
3.64
). As before, the nodes are eigenvalues of the reconstructed matrix
A
and for computing weights, it follows from Table
3.3
that

\displaystyle\mu_{0}=\int_{a}^{b}w(x)dx=\int_{-1}^{1}(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}dx=\frac{2^{\alpha+\beta+1}\Gamma(\alpha+1)\Gamma(\beta+1)}{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta+1)}.

Hence the weights
\omega_{n}
(for
n=1,\cdots,k
) are obtained by considering
\mu_{0}=2
in the RHS of (
3.58
) in which
\bm{v}_{n}
(for
n=1,\cdots,k
) is the eigenvector corresponds to
n
th eigenvalue of matrix
A
constructed based on information given by (
3.64
).

•

nodes and weighs for Legendre polynomial
: The class
L_{n}(\cdot)
of orthogonal polynomials admits the recurrence relation

\displaystyle xL_{n}(x)=\frac{n+1}{2n+1}L_{n+1}(x)+\frac{n}{2n+1}L_{n-1}(x),

(3.65)

with
L_{0}(x)=1
and
L_{1}(x)=x
, for
n=1,2,\cdots
. Comparing the recurrence relations given in (
3.56
) and (
3.65
), we obtain
b_{n}=0
, for
n=0,1,2,\cdots
, and

\displaystyle\begin{cases}a_{n}=\frac{n+1}{2n+1},\penalty\ &{\text{if}}\ n\geq 0,\\
c_{n}=\frac{n}{2n+1},\penalty\ &{\text{if}}\ n\geq 1.\end{cases}

(3.66)

In this case, matrix
A
in (
3.57
) can be reconstructed by setting
\sqrt{a_{n-1}c_{n}}=n/\sqrt{4n^{2}-1}
(for
n=1,\cdots,k
) in the RHS of (
3.57
). The nodes are eigenvalues of the reconstructed matrix
A
. For computing weights, it follows from Table
3.3
that

\displaystyle\mu_{0}=\int_{a}^{b}w(x)dx=\int_{-1}^{1}dx=2.

Hence the weights
\omega_{n}
(for
n=1,\cdots,k
) are obtained by considering
\mu_{0}=2
in the RHS of (
3.58
) in which
\bm{v}_{n}
(for
n=1,\cdots,k
) is the eigenvector corresponds to
n
th eigenvalue of matrix
A
constructed based on information given by (
3.66
).

It should be noted that the accuracy of a Gaussian quadrature depends on the structure of the orthogonal polynomial. In general, if
f(x)
in (
3.52
) has continuous derivatives of order
2k
, then the error in approximating integral (
3.52
) becomes
(
Kahaner et al., 1989
)
:

\displaystyle{\text{Error}}=\int_{a}^{b}w(x)f(x)dx-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\omega_{i}f(x_{i})=\frac{f^{(2k)}(\eta)}{\Gamma(2k+1)}\int_{a}^{b}w(x)P^{2}_{k}(x)dx,

where
f^{(2k)}(\eta)
denotes the
(2k)
th derivative of
f(x)
for some
a<\eta<b
. In special case, when
w(x)=1
, we have

\displaystyle{\text{Error}}=\frac{(b-a)^{2k+1}\bigl[\Gamma(k+1)\bigr]^{4}}{(2k+1)\bigl[\Gamma(2k+1)\bigr]^{3}}f^{(2k)}(\eta).

As a useful criterion, the goodness of approximation
\hat{I}
for integral
I
is reflected by absolute relative error (ARE) criteria defined as

\displaystyle{\text{ARE}}=\Bigl|\frac{I-\hat{I}}{I}\Bigr|.

(3.67)

The following

function

is provided to compute the nodes and weights for implementing the Gaussian quadrature rules discussed earlier. We note that the argument

(for Hermite),

(for generalized Laguerre),

(for Jacobi), and

(for Legendre). Furthermore, arguments

and

are parameters of Gaussian quadrature based on generalized Laguerre or Jacobi orthogonal polynomials.

3-19

Let’s to approximate integral (
3.43
) of Example
3-18
through the Gaussian-Legendre rule. To this end, first, we need to convert the range of integration from
[0,3]
to
[-1,1]
. Using a change of variable
z=2u/3-1
it turns out that

\displaystyle I=\int_{0}^{3}x\exp\{-x\}dx=

\displaystyle\frac{9}{4}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{3}{2}\Bigr\}\int_{-1}^{1}(z+1)\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{3}{2}z\Bigr\}dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{9}{4}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{3}{2}\Bigr\}\int_{-1}^{1}w(z)f(z)dz,

(3.68)

where
w(z)=1
and
f(z)=(z+1)\exp\bigl\{-3z/2\bigr\}
. Hence, the sequence of nodes and weights can be computed using function
quad_rule
, when
type=
“
LE
”. Computing the sequence
\{x_{i},\omega_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
, we can write

\displaystyle I\approx\frac{9}{4}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{3}{2}\Bigr\}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\omega_{i}\times\bigl(x_{i}+1\bigr)\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{3}{2}x_{i}\Bigr\}.

(3.69)

The pertaining AREs for
k=2,3,5
and 10 are 4.58e-02, 1.50e-03 1.75e-07, and 3.18e-15, respectively. We remind that the general quadrature rule applied in Example
3-18
yields 0.83755 with
{\text{ARE}}=0.045
. Evidently, he Gaussian quadrature shows superior performance with
{\text{ARE}}=0.045
just by adding one more node.

3-20

Suppose we are interested in approximating

\displaystyle I(p)=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{p}\log x\exp\bigl\{-x\bigr\}dx,

(3.70)

where
p>0
. Since the range of integrand
I
is
(0,\infty)
and involves the term
\exp\{-x\}
, using class
{\cal{L}}^{p}_{k}
of orthogonal polynomials, a Gaussian quadrature rule is applied for approximating
I(p)
for different settings of
k
and
p
. To this end, we compute the sequence of nodes and weights using function
quad_rule
, when
type=
“
GL
” and
alpha=
p
. Computing sequence
\{x_{i},\omega_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
, we approximate integral
I(p)
as follows.

\displaystyle I(p)\approx\sum_{i=1}^{k}\omega_{i}\times\bigl(x_{i}\bigr)^{p}\log x_{i}.

(3.71)

Table
3.4
shows the ARE results for computing integral
I(p)
in (
3.70
). It turns out from Table
3.4
that for larger
p
, smaller
k
provides the accuracy at desired level.

3-21

Suppose we are interested in approximating

\displaystyle I(z,\alpha)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\bigl\{-u^{\alpha}\bigr\}\cos(zu)du,

(3.72)

where
0<\alpha\leq 2
. In fact, integral
I(z,\alpha)
is the PDF of symmetric standard
\alpha
-stable distribution at point
z

(
Nolan, 2020
)
. For
\alpha=2
, each standard symmetric
\alpha
-stable distribution turns into a zero-mean Gaussian distribution with scale
1/\sqrt{2}
. Hence, we have
I(z,2)={\cal{N}}(z|0,1/2)
. Herein, using a Gauss-Hermite rule, we proceed to approximate integral
I(z,\alpha)
. Table
3.5
shows the ARE for different settings of
k
and
z
. As it is seen, the ARE for large
z
decreases and a larger
k
needs to provide the accuracy of desired level.

3.7
Multivariate Gaussian quadrature

Each univariate
k
-point Gaussian quadrature rule represented as (
3.52
) can be straightforwardly extended to the
p
-dimensional case with
k^{p}
points. Let
f(\bm{x})
denote real function with domain
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and we are willing to approximate the multiple integral of the form

\displaystyle\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}w(\bm{x})f(\bm{x})d\bm{x}=\int_{a_{1}}^{b_{1}}\cdots\int_{a_{p}}^{b_{p}}w\bigl(x_{1},\cdots,x_{p}\bigr)f\bigl(x_{1},\cdots,x_{p}\bigr)dx_{1}\cdots dx_{p},

(3.73)

in which elements of either
\bm{a}=(a_{1},\cdots,a_{p})^{\top}
or
\bm{b}=(b_{1},\cdots,b_{p})^{\top}
may be finite or infinite. It is assumed that the weight function is decomposed as the product of
p
marginal weight functions as
w(\bm{x})=w_{1}(x_{1})w_{2}(x_{2})\cdots w_{p}(x_{p})
. Hence, the integral (
3.73
) can be represented as

\displaystyle\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}w(\bm{x})f(\bm{x})d\bm{x}=\int_{a_{1}}^{b_{1}}\cdots\int_{a_{p}}^{b_{p}}w_{1}\bigl(x_{1}\bigr)w_{2}\bigl(x_{2}\bigr)\cdots w_{p}\bigl(x_{p}\bigr)f\bigl(x_{1},\cdots,x_{p}\bigr)dx_{1}\cdots dx_{p}.

The Gaussian quadrature in multivariate case allows to approximate the integral (
3.73
) as

\displaystyle\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}w(\bm{x})f(\bm{x})d\bm{x}\approx\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{i_{p}=1}^{k_{p}}\omega_{1i_{1}}\omega_{2i_{2}}\cdots\omega_{pi_{p}}f\bigl(x_{1i_{1}},x_{2i_{2}},\cdots,x_{pi_{p}}\bigr),

(3.74)

where
\{\bigl({x}_{ij},{\omega}_{ij}\bigr)\}_{j=1}^{k_{i}}
is the set of
k_{i}
nodes and weights associated with the
i
th coordinate under one of seven Gauss quadrature rules given by Table
3.3
, for
i=1,\cdots,p
. It is worthwhile to note that the Gauss quadrature rule applied to two distinct coordinates may be different. For example, for approximating a double integral, we may compute
k_{1}
pairs of nodes and weights for the first coordinate using the Gauss-Laguerre rule while the set of
k_{2}
nodes and weights for the second coordinate are obtained using the Gauss-Legendre rule.

3-22

Let the PDF of random vector
\bm{X}=\bigl(X_{1},X_{2}\bigr)^{\top}
is given by

\displaystyle g(x_{1},x_{2}|\bm{\theta})=\frac{\beta^{\alpha}x_{1}^{\alpha+\lambda-1}x_{2}^{\lambda-1}}{\Gamma(\lambda)\Gamma{(\alpha)}}\exp\bigl\{-\beta x_{1}-x_{1}x_{2}\bigr\},

(3.75)

where
x_{1}>0
,
x_{2}>0
, and
\bm{\theta}=\bigl(\alpha>0,\beta>0,\lambda>0\bigr)^{\top}
. For computing the associated CDF that can be expressed in terms of integral representation as

\displaystyle G(q_{1},q_{2}|\bm{\theta})=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta^{\alpha}}{\Gamma(\lambda)\Gamma(\alpha)}\int_{0}^{q_{1}}\int_{0}^{q_{2}}x_{1}^{\alpha+\lambda-1}x_{2}^{\lambda-1}\exp\bigl\{-\beta x_{1}-x_{1}x_{2}\bigr\}dx_{2}dx_{1},

(3.76)

where
q_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
q_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, we would like to use the Gaussian quadrature rule. To this end, we apply the Gauss-Legendre rule to both coordinates of integral in the RHS of (
3.76
). Hence, using a simple change of variable, we have

\displaystyle G(q_{1},q_{2}|\bm{\theta})=

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\beta^{\alpha}}{\Gamma(\lambda)\Gamma(\alpha)}\Bigl[\frac{x_{1}+1}{2}q_{1}\Bigr]^{\alpha+\lambda-1}\Bigl[\frac{x_{2}+1}{2}q_{2}\Bigr]^{\lambda-1}

\displaystyle\times\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{x_{1}+1}{2}q_{1}\Bigl[\beta+\frac{x_{2}+1}{2}q_{2}\Bigr]\Bigr\}dx_{2}dx_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{q_{1}q_{2}}{4}\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}w(\bm{x})f(x_{1},x_{2})dx_{2}dx_{1},

(3.77)

in which

\displaystyle w(\bm{x})=

\displaystyle w(x_{1})w(x_{2})=1\times 1=1,

(3.78)

\displaystyle f(u,v)=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta^{\alpha}}{\Gamma(\lambda)\Gamma(\alpha)}\Bigl[\frac{u+1}{2}q_{1}\Bigr]^{\alpha+\lambda-1}\Bigl[\frac{v+1}{2}q_{2}\Bigr]^{\lambda-1}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{u+1}{2}q_{1}\Bigl[\beta+\frac{v+1}{2}q_{2}\Bigr]\Bigr\}.

(3.79)

It follows form (
3.74
) that

\displaystyle G(q_{1},q_{2}|\bm{\theta})\approx

\displaystyle\frac{q_{1}q_{2}}{4}\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}}\omega_{1i}\omega_{2j}f(x_{1i},x_{2j}),

(3.80)

where
\omega_{pi}
and
x_{pi}
denote, accordingly, the
i
th weight and node associated with the
p
th coordinate, for
p=1,2
, and
f(\cdot,\cdot)
is given by (
3.80
). Furthermore, constants
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
are the number of nodes (or weights) corresponds to quadrature rule considered for the first and second coordinates, respectively. Evidently, herein, we should use the Gauss-Legendre rule. In what follows, we give
R
code for approximating
G(q_{1},q_{2}|\bm{\theta})
.

3-23

Recall the univariate BS distribution whose CDF is given by (
2.44
).
We write
\bm{T}\sim{\cal{BS}}(\bm{\theta})
to denote random vector
\bm{T}=\bigl(T_{1},T_{2}\bigr)^{\top}
follows a bivariate BS distribution with PDF given by
(
Kundu et al., 2010
)
:

\displaystyle f\bigl(t_{1},t_{2}\big|\bm{\theta}\bigr)=\bm{\phi}_{2}\bigl(\bm{z}\big|\bm{0},\Sigma\bigr)\prod_{i=1}^{2}\frac{1}{2\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}}\Big[\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{i}}{t_{i}}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{i}}{t_{i}}\Bigr)^{\frac{3}{2}}\Bigr],

(3.81)

where
\bm{\theta}=\bigl(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta_{2},\rho\bigr)^{\top}
is the family parameter vector,
\Sigma=[(1,\rho)^{\top},(\rho,1)^{\top}]
, and
\bm{z}=\bigl(z_{1},z_{2}\bigr)^{\top}
with

\displaystyle z_{i}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}\bigg[\sqrt{\frac{t_{i}}{\beta_{i}}}-\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{i}}{t_{i}}}\biggr],\penalty\ i=1,2.

(3.82)

We further note that

\displaystyle P\bigl(T_{1}\leq t_{1},T_{2}\leq t_{2}|\bm{\theta}\bigr)=\bm{\Phi}_{2}\bigl(\bm{z}\big|\bm{0},\Sigma\bigr).

(3.83)

where
z_{i}
is given by (
3.82
).
Let
\xi_{i}=\xi_{i}\bigl(\alpha_{i},\rho\bigr)=2(1-\rho^{2})\alpha_{i}^{2}
for
i=1,2
. Then more algebra shows

\displaystyle E\bigl(T^{m}_{1}T^{n}_{2}\bigr)=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{m}_{1}t^{n}_{2}f\bigl(t_{1},t_{2}\big|\bm{\theta}\bigr)dt_{1}dt_{2}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\int_{L_{1}}^{U_{1}}\int_{L_{2}}^{U_{2}}t^{m}_{1}t^{n}_{2}\bm{\phi}_{2}\bigl(\bm{z}\big|\bm{0},\Sigma\bigr)\prod_{i=1}^{2}\frac{1}{2\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}}\Big[\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{i}}{t_{i}}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{i}}{t_{i}}\Bigr)^{\frac{3}{2}}\Bigr]dt_{i},

(3.84)

where the quantities
L_{i}
and
U_{i}
are chosen in such a way as to constitute on a region beyond which integrand in (
3.84
) is sufficiently small. To this end, we set

\displaystyle L_{i}=

\displaystyle\bigg\{t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigg|\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}\bigg[\sqrt{\frac{t_{i}}{\beta_{i}}}-\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{i}}{t_{i}}}\biggr]=-\eta\bigg\},

(3.85)

\displaystyle U_{i}=

\displaystyle\bigg\{t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigg|\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}\bigg[\sqrt{\frac{t_{i}}{\beta_{i}}}-\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{i}}{t_{i}}}\biggr]=\eta\bigg\},

(3.86)

where
\eta
is a positive constant. Since
z_{i}
is an increasing function of
t_{i}
, then by solving non-linear equations in (
3.85
)-(
3.86
), we obtain

\displaystyle L_{i}=

\displaystyle-\eta\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}\Bigl[\frac{\eta}{2}\alpha_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\eta^{2}\alpha_{i}^{2}+4}\Bigr]+\beta_{i}\bigl(\eta^{2}\alpha_{i}^{2}+1\bigr),

(3.87)

\displaystyle U_{i}=

\displaystyle-\eta\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}\Bigl[\frac{\eta}{2}\alpha_{i}-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\eta^{2}\alpha_{i}^{2}+4}\Bigr]+\beta_{i}\bigl(\eta^{2}\alpha_{i}^{2}+1\bigr).

(3.88)

Herein, we set
\eta=6
that guarantees the joint Gaussian PDF, and hence integrand in the RHS of (
3.84
), is sufficiently small. Using an appropriate transformation of the form
y_{i}=2(t_{i}-L_{i})/(U_{i}-L_{i})-1
, for
i=1,2
, then the RHS of (
3.84
) becomes

\displaystyle E\bigl(T^{m}_{1}T^{n}_{2}\bigr)\approx

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{2}\frac{U_{i}-L_{i}}{4\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}}\times\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}\eta^{m}_{1}\eta^{n}_{2}\bm{\phi}_{2}\bigl(\bm{y}\big|\bm{0},\Sigma\bigr)\Big[\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{i}}{y_{i}}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{i}}{y_{i}}\Bigr)^{\frac{3}{2}}\Bigr]dy_{1}dy_{2},

(3.89)

where
\eta_{i}=(y_{i}+1)(U_{i}-L_{i})/2+L_{i}
for
i=1,2
. The RHS of (
3.89
) can be approximated through a
k
-point Gauss- Legendre rule. Therefore

\displaystyle E\bigl(T^{m}_{1}T^{n}_{2}\bigr)\approx

\displaystyle\prod_{r=1}^{2}\frac{U_{r}-L_{r}}{4\alpha_{r}\beta_{r}}\times\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\omega_{1i}\omega_{2j}\Bigl[\frac{1}{2}(y_{1i}+1)(U_{1}-L_{1})+L_{1}\Bigr]^{m}

\displaystyle\times\Bigl[\frac{1}{2}(y_{2j}+1)(U_{2}-L_{2})+L_{2}\Bigr]^{n}\bm{\phi}_{2}\Bigl((y_{1i},y_{2j})^{\top}\Big|\bm{0},\Sigma\Bigr)

\displaystyle\times\Big[\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{1}}{y_{1i}}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{1}}{y_{1i}}\Bigr)^{\frac{3}{2}}\Bigr]\Big[\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{2}}{y_{2j}}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{2}}{y_{2j}}\Bigr)^{\frac{3}{2}}\Bigr],

(3.90)

where
\omega_{rs}
and
y_{rs}
are, accordingly, the
s
th (for
s=1,\cdots,k
) weight and node for the
r
th coordinate (for
r=1,2
) computed based on a
k
-point Gauss-Legendre rule. A simulation study have been carried out to check the performance of approximation (
3.90
). The results are shown in Table
3.7
. We notice that when
\rho\neq 0
, then the Gauss-Legendre rule works well only when
k
is large.

3-24

Suppose we are interested in computing double integral

\displaystyle I\bigl(\bm{\theta}\bigr)=\int_{a}^{b}\int_{c(x_{1})}^{d(x_{1})}f\bigl(x_{1},x_{2}\big|\bm{\theta}\bigr)dx_{2}dx_{1}.

(3.91)

For approximating
I\bigl(\bm{\theta}\bigr)
, we may proceed based on a Gauss-Legendre rule as follows. First we apply a
k_{1}
-point Gauss-Legendre rule for inner integral as

\displaystyle\int_{c(x_{1})}^{d(x_{1})}f\bigl(x_{1},x_{2}\big|\bm{\theta}\bigr)dx_{2}=

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}f\Bigl[x_{1},\frac{\bigl(x_{2}+1\bigr)\bigl(d(x_{1})-c(x_{1})\bigr)}{2}+c(x_{1})\Big|\bm{\theta}\Bigr]dx_{2}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{d(x_{1})-c(x_{1})}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}}\omega_{2j}f\Bigl[x_{1},\frac{\bigl(x_{2j}+1\bigr)\bigl(d(x_{1})-c(x_{1})\bigr)}{2}+c(x_{1})\Big|\bm{\theta}\Bigr],

(3.92)

where
\omega_{2j}
and
x_{2j}
, for
j=1,\cdots,k_{2}
are, accordingly, the weights and nodes constructed based on a
k_{2}
-point Gauss-Legendre rule. Then, we complete computing
I\bigl(\bm{\theta}\bigr)
by approximating the outer integral as follows.

\displaystyle I\bigl(\bm{\theta}\bigr)\approx

\displaystyle\frac{b-a}{4}\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}}\omega_{1i}\omega_{2j}\bigl[d(x_{1i})-c(x_{1i})\bigr]

\displaystyle\times f\Bigl[\frac{\bigl(x_{1i}+1\bigr)\bigl(b-a\bigr)}{2}+a,\frac{\bigl(x_{2j}+1\bigr)\bigl(d(x_{1i})-c(x_{1i})\bigr)}{2}+c(x_{1i})\Big|\bm{\theta}\Bigr],

(3.93)

where
\omega_{1i}
and
x_{1i}
, for
i=1,\cdots,k_{1}
are, accordingly, the weights and nodes constructed based on a
k_{1}
-point Gauss-Legendre rule.

3-25

Indeed, computing the probability of a bivariate Gaussian distribution is simply expressed in terms of
I\bigl(\bm{\theta}\bigr)
given in (
3.91
). Let
\bm{X}=\bigl(X_{1},X_{2}\bigr)^{\top}
follow a Gaussian distribution with mean
\bm{\mu}=\bigl(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\bigr)^{\top}
and covariance matrix
\Sigma=\bigl[(\Sigma_{1,1},\Sigma_{1,2})^{\top},(\Sigma_{2,1},\Sigma_{2,2})^{\top}\bigr]
. Recall from Subsection
3.5
that discusses computing
{\cal{P}}=P(\bm{a}\leq\bm{X}\leq\bm{b})
using a transformation
\bm{y}=L(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})
in which
L
denotes the Cholesky decomposition of
\Sigma
. We have

\displaystyle{\cal{P}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\frac{a_{1}-\mu_{1}}{L_{1,1}}}^{\frac{b_{1}-\mu_{1}}{L_{1,1}}}\int_{c(y_{1})}^{d(y_{1})}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{y^{2}_{1}+y^{2}_{2}}{2}\Bigr\}dy_{2}dy_{1},

(3.94)

where
\bm{a}=\bigl(a_{1},a_{2}\bigr)^{\top}
,
\bm{b}=\bigl(b_{1},b_{2}\bigr)^{\top}
, and

\displaystyle c(y_{1})=\frac{a_{2}-\mu_{2}-L_{2,1}y_{1}}{L_{2,2}},

\displaystyle d(y_{1})=\frac{b_{2}-\mu_{2}-L_{2,1}y_{1}}{L_{2,2}}.

Following (
3-24
) for approximating the RHS of (
3.94
), it turns out that

\displaystyle{\cal{P}}\approx

\displaystyle\frac{(b_{1}-a_{1})(b_{2}-a_{2})}{4L_{1,1}L_{2,2}}\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}}\omega_{1i}\omega_{2j}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{z^{2}_{1i}+z^{2}_{2j}}{2}\Bigr\},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}}\omega_{1i}\omega_{2j}g\bigl(z_{1i},z_{2i}\bigr),

(3.95)

where

\displaystyle z_{1i}=

\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(x_{1i}+1\bigr)\bigl(b_{1}-a_{1}\bigr)}{2L_{1,1}}+\frac{a_{1}-\mu_{1}}{L_{1,1}},

\displaystyle z_{2j}=

\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(x_{2j}+1\bigr)\bigl(b_{2}-a_{2}\bigr)}{2L_{2,2}}+\frac{a_{2}-\mu_{2}-L_{2,1}z_{1i}}{L_{2,2}}.

The
R
code below approximates
{\cal{P}}
given in (
3.94
) using a
k
-point Gauss-Legendre rule using Cholesky decomposition of covariance matrix.

3.8
Computing the CDF of multivariate Gaussian distribution using Gaussian Quadrature

Recall form Subsection
3.5
that computes the CDF of Gaussian distribution using SOV technique proposed by
Genz (1992)
. Herein, we are willing to compute

{\cal{P}}
through the Gaussian quadrature based on spectral decomposition (
2.93
) of covariance matrix.
(
Forbes and Wraith, 2014
)
show that the PDF of each
p
-dimensional Gaussian distribution can be represented as the product of
p
univariate Gaussian PDFs as

\displaystyle\bm{\phi}_{p}\bigl(\bm{x}\big|\bm{\mu},\Sigma\bigr)=\bm{\phi}_{p}\bigl(\bm{x}\big|\bm{\mu},VDV^{\top}\bigr)=\prod_{i=1}^{p}{\phi}\Bigl(\bm{x}\Big|\bigl[V^{\top}\bm{z}\big]_{i},d_{i}\Bigr)=\prod_{i=1}^{p}{\phi}\Bigl(\bigl[V^{\top}\bm{x}\bigr]_{i}\Big|\bigl[V^{\top}\bm{\mu}\bigr]_{i},d_{i}\Bigr),

(3.96)

where
\bm{z}=\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}
,
\bigl[V^{\top}\bm{z}\bigr]_{i}
denotes the
i
th element of vector
V^{\top}\bm{z}
, and
d_{i}
is the
i
th diagonal entry of the diagonal matrix
D
whose main diagonal elements are eigen values of
\Sigma
. Let
p
-dimensional random vector
\bm{X}
follows
{\cal{N}}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma)
. For computing
{\cal{P}}=P(\bm{a}\leq\bm{X}\leq\bm{b})
, based on spectral decomposition, we have

\displaystyle{\cal{P}}=

\displaystyle\int_{a_{1}}^{b_{1}}\cdots\int_{a_{p}}^{b_{p}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}{\phi}\Bigl(\bigl[V^{\top}\bm{x}\bigr]_{i}\Big|\bigl[V^{\top}\bm{\mu}\bigr]_{i},d_{i}\Bigr)dx_{1}\cdots dx_{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{a_{1}-\mu_{1}}^{b_{1}-\mu_{1}}\cdots\int_{a_{p}-\mu_{p}}^{b_{p}-\mu_{p}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}{\phi}\Bigl(\bigl[V^{\top}\bm{x}\bigr]_{i}\Big|0,d_{i}\Bigr)dx_{1}\cdots dx_{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\cdots\int_{-1}^{1}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Bigl[\frac{b_{i}-a_{i}}{2}\Bigr]{\phi}\Bigl(\Bigl[V^{\top}\bm{y}\Bigr]_{i}\Big|0,d_{i}\Bigr)dx_{1}\cdots dx_{p},

(3.97)

where
\bm{y}=(\bm{x}+1)(\bm{b}-\bm{a})/2+\bm{a}-\bm{\mu}
. Using the Gauss-Laguerre rule the quantity
{\cal{P}}
can be approximated as

\displaystyle{\cal{P}}\approx

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Bigl[\frac{b_{i}-a_{i}}{2\sqrt{2\pi d_{i}}}\Bigr]\times\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{k_{1}}\cdots\sum_{i_{p}=1}^{k_{p}}\omega_{1i_{1}}\cdots\omega_{pi_{p}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{r=1}^{p}\frac{\bigl(\sum_{c=1}^{p}v_{r,c}\times z_{ci_{c}}\bigr)^{2}}{d_{r}}\Bigr\},

(3.98)

where
z_{ci_{c}}=\bigl(x_{ci_{c}}+1\bigr)\bigl(b_{c}-a_{c}\bigr)/2+a_{c}-\mu_{c}
in which
x_{ci_{c}}
is the
i_{c}
th node computed based on a
k_{c}
-point Gauss-Laguerre rule for the
c
th coordinate and furthermore
v_{r,c}
denotes the
(r,c)
th entry of matrix
V^{T}
.

3-26

Herein, we want to compute
{\cal{P}}
given in Example
3-25
based on spectral decomposition discussed earlier. For
p=2
and
k_{1}=k_{2}=k
, it follows from (
3.98
) that

\displaystyle{\cal{P}}\approx

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{2}\Bigl[\frac{b_{i}-a_{i}}{2\sqrt{2\pi d_{i}}}\Bigr]\times\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\omega_{1i}\omega_{2j}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\bigl(v_{1,1}z_{1i}+v_{1,2}z_{2j}\bigr)^{2}}{2d_{1}}-\frac{\bigl(v_{2,1}z_{1i}+v_{2,2}z_{2j}\bigr)^{2}}{2d_{2}}\Bigr\},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\omega_{1i}\omega_{2j}g\bigl(z_{1i},z_{2i}\bigr),

(3.99)

where
z_{ij}=\bigl(x_{ij}+1\bigr)\bigl(b_{i}-a_{i}\bigr)/2+a_{i}-\mu_{i}
in which
x_{ij}
(or
\omega_{ij}
) for
i=1,2
and
j=1,\cdots,k
is the
j
th node (or weight) computed based on a
k
-point Gauss-Laguerre rule for the
i
th coordinate. Table
3.8
shows the performance of the
k
-point Gauss-Legendre rule for approximating
{\cal{P}}=P(\bm{a}\leq\bm{X}\leq\bm{b})
when
\bm{\mu}=(0,0)^{\top}
and covariance matrix
\Sigma=\bigl[(1,\rho)^{\top},(1,\rho)^{\top}\bigr]
. It is worth to note that both of the Cholesky and spectral decompositions show the same results in cases discussed in Table
3.8
.

Figure
3.9
displays the address of nodes and product of weights using the Gauss-Legendre rule with
k_{1}=k_{2}=5
points for approximating
{\cal{P}}
when
\bm{\mu}=(0,0)^{\top}
and
\Sigma=\bigl[(1,\rho)^{\top},(1,\rho)^{\top}\bigr]
. More investigations reveal that a
k
-point Gauss-Legendre rule based on spectral decomposition shows superior performance than the Cholesky decomposition since in the latter case the integration region is rectangular. In general, for approximating a
p
-dimensional integral one needs to compute
k^{p}
nodes and weights that computationally is expensive when
p
becomes large. In such case, the Gaussian quadrature rules are not applicable. Alternatively, we suggest the use of
R
package
TruncatedNormal
that has been developed by
(
Botev, 2017
)
and is available at CRAN. Our proposed function
spec_quad
works well for small
p
(
p\leq 5
, say).

. Second row: contour plot of

). The filled black points show the address of nodes based on the spectral decomposition for (c):

,
\bm{b}=(2,2)^{\top}
, and
\rho=-0.50
and (d):
\bm{a}=(0,0)^{\top}
,
\bm{b}=(\infty,\infty)^{\top}
, and
\rho=0.50
.

3.9
Moments of truncated distributions

Truncated distribution is widely used in different fields of statistics such as, survival analysis, regression analysis, and model-based clustering. We write
{\cal{X}}
to denote the truncated version of random variable
X
on
(a,b)
. The random variable may take any value on real line, but
{\cal{X}}
just takes values on
(a,b)
. The latter is known as the doubly truncated of
X
. There are two other types of truncated random variable including the left truncated random variables. If
{\cal{X}}
is defined on
(b,\infty)
, then we may say that
{\cal{X}}
is the left truncated version of
X
at
b
. Likewise, if
{\cal{X}}
is defined on
(-\infty,a)
, then we may say that
{\cal{X}}
is the right truncated version of
X
at
a
. The PDF of random variable
{\cal{X}}
truncated on
(a,b)
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{f(x)}{\int_{a}^{b}f(u)du},

(3.100)

where
-\infty<a<x<b<\infty
. Each doubly random variable
{\cal{X}}
is in fact a conditional version of random variable
X
. In other words

\displaystyle{{\cal{X}}}_{a,b}=X\big|X\in(a,b).

(3.101)

Let
F(\cdot)
denotes the CDF of random variable
X
. Elementary calculations show that the CDF of
{{\cal{X}}}
can be represented in terms of
F(\cdot)
as follows.

\displaystyle\frac{F(x)-F(a)}{F(b)-F(a)},\penalty\ \penalty\ -\infty<a<x<b<\infty.

(3.102)

Hence, in univariate case, the
m
th moment of truncated random variable
X
on
(a,b)
, for
m=1,2,3,\cdots
is

\displaystyle\Omega^{m}=E\bigl(X^{m}\big|a<X<b\bigr)=\frac{\int_{a}^{b}u^{m}f(u)du}{F(b)-F(a)},\penalty\ -\infty<a<x<b<\infty.

(3.103)

In multivariate case, the PDF of truncated random vector
\bm{X}
on hyper-cube
(\bm{a},\bm{b})\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
with lower bound
\bm{a}=(a_{1},\cdots,a_{p})^{\top}
and upper bound
\bm{b}=(b_{1},\cdots,b_{p})^{\top}
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{f(\bm{x})}{\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}f(\bm{u})d\bm{u}}=\frac{f(\bm{x})}{F(\bm{b})-F(\bm{a})},

(3.104)

where
\bm{a}<\bm{x}<\bm{b}
and
F(\cdot)
is the CDF of
\bm{X}
. So, the first and second moments of truncated random vector
\bm{X}
, respectively, are

\displaystyle\bm{\Omega}=

\displaystyle E\bigl(\bm{X}\big|\bm{a}<\bm{X}<\bm{b}\bigr)=E\bigl(\bm{X}\big|a_{1}<X_{1}<b_{1},\cdots,a_{p}<X_{p}<b_{p}\bigr),

\displaystyle\bm{\Omega}^{2}=

\displaystyle E\bigl(\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\top}\big|\bm{a}<\bm{X}<\bm{b}\bigr)=E\bigl(\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\top}\big|a_{1}<X_{1}<b_{1},\cdots,a_{p}<X_{p}<b_{p}\bigr).

Herein, the concern of study mainly is devoted to computing the first and second moments given by since these quantities have received more attention than other moments in practice.
If
\bm{X}\sim f(\cdot)
, by definition, the first moment of
\bm{X}
is computed as

\displaystyle\bm{\Omega}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{F(\bm{b})-F(\bm{a})}\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}\bm{u}f(\bm{u})d\bm{u}.

(3.105)

The Monte Carlo approximation of
\bm{\Omega}
can be obtained by considering the instrumental distribution with PDFs
f(\cdot)/\bigl[F(a)-F(b)\bigr]
. Here, we can use the rejection approach for simulating from truncated random variable as follows. Suppose,
x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}
, for sufficiently large
N
, are realizations from instrumental with PDF
f_{1}
, the Monte Carlo approximation of
\bm{\Omega}
is obtained as

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\Omega}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}f_{1}\bigl(x_{i}\bigr).

(3.106)

The pseudo code for sampling from
f(\cdot)/\bigl[F(a)-F(b)\bigr]
is given as follows.

1.

Read
{a},{b}
, and
N
;

2.

Set
j=0
and
{y}=0
;

3.

Simulate
{x}\sim f(\cdot)

4.

If
a<x<b
, then accept
{x}
as a generation from
f(\cdot)/\bigl[F(a)-F(b)\bigr]
, set
{y}={y}+x
and
j=j+1
;

5.

If
j=N
, then go to the next step, otherwise return to step 2;

6.

Approximate
\Omega
as
\hat{\Omega}=y/N
;

7.

End.

The focus of this chapter is placed on compute the the first and second moments of truncated Gaussian distribution in univariate and multivariate cases.
Herein, we proceed to compute the exact values of first and second moments of the truncated univariate and multivariate Gaussian distributions.

3.10
Moments of truncated Gaussian distribution in univariate case

For simplicity, we use generic symbols
\phi\bigl({x}\big|{\mu},\sigma^{2}\bigr)
and
{\Phi}\bigl({x}\big|{\mu},\sigma^{2}\bigr)
to show, accordingly, the PDF and CDF of a Gaussian distribution at point
{x}
for which
{\mu}
and
\sigma
are the location and scale parameters, respectively. We further write
{X}\sim{\cal{N}}({\mu},\sigma^{2})
to show that random variable
{X}
follows a Gaussian distribution with location parameter
{\mu}
and variance
\sigma^{2}
. The corresponding truncated family on
(a,b)
is represented by
t{\cal{N}}\bigl({\mu},\sigma^{2},a,b\bigr)
.

3.10.1
First two moments of truncated Gaussian distribution

Let
X\sim{\cal{N}}\bigl(\mu,\sigma^{2}\bigr)
. By definition, from (
3.103
) for
m=1
, it follows that

\displaystyle\Omega=E\bigl(X\big|a<X<b\bigr)=\frac{1}{\Phi(b|\mu,\sigma^{2})-\Phi(a|\mu,\sigma^{2})}\int_{a}^{b}x\times\phi(x|\mu,\sigma^{2})dx.

(3.107)

It is easy to check that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\phi(x|\mu,\sigma^{2})=

\displaystyle\Bigl(\frac{x-\mu}{\sigma^{2}}\Bigr)\phi(x|\mu,\sigma^{2}).

(3.108)

Integrating both sides of (
3.108
) and dividing by
P_{G}=\Phi(b|\mu,\sigma^{2})-\Phi(a|\mu,\sigma^{2})
yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{P_{G}}\int_{a}^{b}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\phi(x|\mu,\sigma^{2})dx=\frac{\Omega}{\sigma^{2}}-\frac{\mu}{\sigma^{2}}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\Omega

\displaystyle=\mu+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{P_{G}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\int_{a-\mu}^{b-\mu}\phi(u|0,\sigma^{2})du

\displaystyle=\mu+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{P_{G}}\Bigl[\phi(a|\mu,\sigma^{2})-\phi(b|\mu,\sigma^{2})\Bigr].

Hence,

\displaystyle\Omega=E\bigl(X\big|a<X<b\bigr)=\mu+\sigma^{2}\frac{\phi(a|\mu,\sigma^{2})-\phi(b|\mu,\sigma^{2})}{\Phi(b|\mu,\sigma^{2})-\Phi(a|\mu,\sigma^{2})}.

(3.109)

By definition, from (
3.103
) for
m=2
, it follows that

\displaystyle\Omega^{2}=E\bigl(X^{2}\big|a<X<b\bigr)=\frac{1}{P_{G}}\int_{a}^{b}x^{2}\phi(x|\mu,\sigma^{2})dx,

Taking the second order derivative from (
3.108
) with respect to
\mu
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\mu^{2}}\phi(x|\mu,\sigma^{2})=

\displaystyle\Bigl(\frac{x-\mu}{\sigma^{2}}\Bigr)^{2}\phi(x|\mu,\sigma^{2})-\frac{\phi(x|\mu,\sigma^{2})}{\sigma^{2}}.

(3.110)

Integrating both sides of (
3.110
), dividing by
P_{G}
, and then rearranging yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{P_{G}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\mu^{2}}\int_{a-\mu}^{b-\mu}\phi(x|0,\sigma^{2})dx=\frac{1}{P_{G}}\int_{a}^{b}\Bigl(\frac{x-\mu}{\sigma^{2}}\Bigr)^{2}\phi(x|\mu,\sigma^{2})dx-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}.

(3.111)

It is easy to check that the LHS of (
3.111
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{1}{P_{G}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\mu^{2}}\int_{a-\mu}^{b-\mu}\phi(x|0,\sigma^{2})dx=\frac{1}{P_{G}}\Bigl[\Bigl(\frac{a-\mu}{\sigma^{2}}\Bigr)\phi(a|\mu,\sigma^{2})-\Bigl(\frac{b-\mu}{\sigma^{2}}\Bigr)\phi(b|\mu,\sigma^{2})\Bigr],

(3.112)

and further

\displaystyle\frac{1}{P_{G}}\int_{a}^{b}\Bigl(\frac{x-\mu}{\sigma^{2}}\Bigr)^{2}\phi(x|\mu,\sigma^{2})dx=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}E\Bigl[\Bigl(\frac{X-\mu}{\sigma}\Bigr)^{2}\Big|a<X<b\Bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sigma^{4}}E\bigl(X^{2}\big|a<X<b\bigr)-2\frac{\mu}{\sigma^{4}}E\bigl(X\big|a<X<b\bigr)\Bigr]+\frac{\mu^{2}}{\sigma^{4}}.

(3.113)

Hence, substituting the RHS of (
3.112
) and (
3.113
) into (
3.111
) and rearranging, we obtain

\displaystyle\Omega^{2}=\sigma^{2}\frac{(a-\mu)\phi(a|\mu,\sigma^{2})-(b-\mu)\phi(b|\mu,\sigma^{2})}{\Phi(b|\mu,\sigma^{2})-\Phi(a|\mu,\sigma^{2})}+\sigma^{2}-\mu^{2}+2\mu\Omega.

In what follows
R
function
Ex
is given for computing the first and second moments of truncated univariate Gaussian distribution.

3.11
First moment of truncated multivariate Gaussian distribution

For simplicity, we use generic symbols
\bm{\phi}_{p}\bigl(\bm{x}\big|\bm{\mu},\Sigma\bigr)
and
\bm{\Phi}_{p}\bigl(\bm{x}\big|\bm{\mu},\Sigma\bigr)
to denote, accordingly, the PDF and CDF of a
p
-dimensional Gaussian distribution at point
\bm{x}
for which
\bm{\mu}
and
\Sigma
are the location and scale parameters, respectively. Also, we write
\bm{X}\sim{\cal{N}}_{p}\bigl(\bm{\mu},\Sigma\bigr)
to indicate that random vector
\bm{X}
follows a Gaussian distribution with location vector
\bm{\mu}
and scale matrix
\Sigma
. The corresponding family of Gaussian distributions truncated on on hyper-cube
(\bm{a},\bm{b})\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
is shown by
{\cal{TN}}_{p}\bigl(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\bm{a},\bm{b}\bigr)
. Furthermore,
\bm{\Omega}
denotes the first moment of family
{\cal{TN}}_{p}\bigl(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\bm{a},\bm{b}\bigr)
. Suppose
p
-dimensional random vector
{\bm{X}}=(X_{1},\cdots,X_{p})^{\top}
follows
{\cal{N}}_{p}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma)
, we define the Mahalanobis distance as

\displaystyle\delta(\bm{x},\Sigma)=\bm{{{x}}}^{\top}{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{x},

(3.114)

and

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{i}[x]=\bigl({v}_{1},\cdots,{v}_{i-1},x,{v}_{i+1},\cdots,{v}_{p}\bigr),

(3.115)

\displaystyle\bm{v}[-i]=\bigl({v}_{1},\cdots,{v}_{i-1},{v}_{i+1},\cdots,{v}_{p}\bigr),

(3.116)

in which
\bm{v}=\bigl(v_{1},v_{2},\cdots,v_{p}\bigr)^{\top}
is a vector of real values. By definition, we can write

\displaystyle\bm{\Omega}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\cal{P}}_{G}}\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}\bm{x}\times\bm{\phi}_{p}\bigl(\bm{x}\big|\bm{\mu},\Sigma\bigr)d\bm{x}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}\bm{x}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)}{2}\Bigr\}d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)}{2}\Bigr\}d\bm{x}

\displaystyle+\frac{\bm{\mu}}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)}{2}\Bigr\}d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Sigma\bm{I}+\bm{\mu},

(3.117)

where
{\bf{C}}_{G}=(2\pi)^{p/2}|\Sigma|^{1/2}
,
{\cal{P}}_{G}=\bm{\Phi}_{p}\bigl(\bm{b}\big|\bm{\mu},{\Sigma}\bigr)-\bm{\Phi}_{p}\bigl(\bm{a}\big|\bm{\mu},{\Sigma}\bigr)
.
Since

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{\mu}}\frac{\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)}{2}=-{\Sigma}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}),

then

\displaystyle\bm{I}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{\mu}}\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)}{2}\Bigr\}d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{\mu}}\int_{\bm{a}-\bm{\mu}}^{\bm{b}-\bm{\mu}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta(\bm{u},\Sigma)}{2}\Bigr\}d\bm{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl({{I}}_{1},{{I}}_{2},\cdots,{{I}}_{p}\bigr)^{\top},

where

\displaystyle{{I}}_{i}=\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\int_{\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]}^{\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]}\biggl[\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}[a_{i}-\mu_{i}],\Sigma\bigr)}{2}\Bigr\}-\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}[b_{i}-\mu_{i}],\Sigma\bigr)}{2}\Bigr\}\biggr]d\bm{u}[-i],

(3.118)

for
i=1,\cdots,p
. We note that the quadratic form
\delta\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}[x],\Sigma\bigr)
can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\delta\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}[x],\Sigma\bigr)=x^{2}\times\bigl(\Sigma_{i,i}\bigr)^{-1}+\delta\Bigl(\bm{u}[-i]-\bm{\xi}(x),\Delta_{i}\Bigr),

(3.119)

where

\displaystyle\bm{\xi}(x)=

\displaystyle\Sigma_{i,-i}\times\bigl(\Sigma_{i,i}\bigr)^{-1}\times{x},

(3.120)

\displaystyle\Delta_{i}=

\displaystyle\Sigma_{-i,-i}-\bigl(\Sigma_{i,i}\bigr)^{-1}\times\bigl(\Sigma_{i,-i}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma_{i,-i},

(3.121)

in which
\Sigma_{i,j}
denotes the
(i,j)
th element of
\Sigma
,
\Sigma_{-i,-j}
denotes the matrix
\Sigma
when its
i
th row and
j
th column is eliminated, and
\Sigma_{i,-j}
is the
i
th row of matrix
\Sigma
when its
j
the column is eliminated, for
i,j=1,\cdots,p
.
Hence, using expression (
3.119
), the integrand in the RHS of (
3.118
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle I_{i}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{(a_{i}-\mu_{i})^{2}}{2\Sigma_{i,i}}\Bigr\}\int_{\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]}^{\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{2}\delta\Bigl(\bm{u}[-i]-\bm{\xi}(a_{i}-\mu_{i}),\Delta_{i}\Bigr)\Bigr\}d\bm{u}[-i]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{(b_{i}-\mu_{i})^{2}}{2\Sigma_{i,i}}\Bigr\}\int_{\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]}^{\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{2}\delta\Bigl(\bm{u}[-i]-\bm{\xi}(b_{i}-\mu_{i}),\Delta_{i}\Bigr)\Bigr\}d\bm{u}[-i].

The quantity
{\bf{C}}_{G}
can be represented as

\displaystyle{\bf{C}}_{G}=\sqrt{2\pi\Sigma_{i,i}}(2\pi)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\big|\Delta_{i}\big|^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(3.122)

It follows that

\displaystyle I_{i}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\cal{P}}_{G}}\phi\Bigl(a_{i}\Big|\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i,i}\Bigr)\biggl[\bm{\Phi}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]\Big|\bm{\xi}(a_{i}-\mu_{i}),\Delta_{i}\Bigr)

\displaystyle-\bm{\Phi}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]\Big|\bm{\xi}(a_{i}-\mu_{i}),\Delta_{i}\Bigr)\biggr]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{{\cal{P}}_{G}}\phi\Bigl(b_{i}\Big|\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i,i}\Bigr)\biggl[\bm{\Phi}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]\Big|\bm{\xi}(b_{i}-\mu_{i}),\Delta_{i}\Bigr)

\displaystyle-\bm{\Phi}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]\Big|\bm{\xi}(b_{i}-\mu_{i}),\Delta_{i}\Bigr)\biggr].

(3.123)

Computing
I_{i}
in (
3.123
), for
i=1,\cdots,p
, the first moment of truncated Gaussian distribution is then obtained by substituting the constructed vector
\bm{I}
into the RHS of (
3.117
).

Here, we carry out a small simulation study for computing the first moment of vector
\bm{X}\sim{{\cal{TN}}}_{2}(\bm{0},\Sigma,\bm{a},\bm{b})
under two scenarios. Under the first scenario, we assume that
\sigma_{1}^{2}=2,\sigma_{2}^{2}=1,\rho=0.3535
,
\bm{a}=(-1,-1)^{\top}
, and
\bm{b}=(2,2)^{\top}
. Under the second scenario it is assumed that
\sigma_{1}^{2}=2,\sigma_{2}^{2}=2,\rho=0.25
,
\bm{a}=(0,0)^{\top}
, and
\bm{b}=(+\infty,+\infty)^{\top}
. For both scenarios, we compute
\bm{\Omega}
by generating samples of sizes
N=2000,5000
, and 20000 based on 5000 runs. The instrumental distribution is set to be
{\cal{N}}_{2}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma)
. For computing
\bm{\Omega}
through the
{\cal{N}}_{2}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma)
, one can follow two ways. In the first way the quantity
\bm{\Omega}
is estimated as

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\Omega}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\bm{x}_{i}\big|

(3.124)

where
\big|\bm{x}_{i}\big|=\bigl(\big|x_{1i}\big|,\big|x_{2i}\big|\bigr)^{\top}
where
\bm{x}_{i}
s come independently from
{\cal{N}}_{2}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma)
, for sufficiently large
N
. The second method computes
\hat{\bm{\Omega}}
based on sample of size
N
when the lower and upper truncation bounds are
\bm{a}=\bm{0}
and
\bm{b}=+\bm{\infty}
, respectively. Details for implementing the second method are given as follows.

1.

Read
\bm{a}=\bm{0},\bm{b}=+\bm{\infty},\bm{\mu}
,
\Sigma
, and
N
;

2.

Set
j=0
and
\bm{y}=(0,0)^{\top}
;

3.

Simulate
\bm{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})^{\top}\sim{\cal{N}}_{2}(\bm{0},\Sigma)

4.

If
a_{1}<x_{1}<b_{1}
and
a_{2}<x_{2}<b_{2}
, then accept
\bm{x}
as a generation from
{{\cal{TN}}}_{2}(\bm{0},\Sigma,\bm{a},\bm{b})
, set
\bm{y}=\bm{y}+\bigl(x_{1},x_{2}\bigr)^{\top}
and
j=j+1
;

5.

If
j=N
, then go to the next step, otherwise return to step 2;

6.

Compute an approximation of
\bm{\Omega}=\bigl({{\Omega}}_{1},{{\Omega}}_{2}\bigr)^{\top}
as
\hat{\bm{\Omega}}=\bigl({y}_{1}/N,{y}_{2}/N\bigr)^{\top}
;

7.

End.

While the exact value of
\bm{\Omega}
under both scenarios is computed using (
3.117
), Table
3.9
shows the results of simulation study for approximating
\bm{\Omega}
under both scenarios through the instrumental
{\cal{N}}_{2}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma)
.

The PDF of bivariate Gaussian distribution and the corresponding truncated version under two scenarios mentioned above is depicted in Figure

.

Figure 3.10:
