3.12
Second moment of truncated Gaussian distribution

Let
\bm{v}=\bigl(v_{1}\cdots,v_{p}\bigr)^{\top}
represent a vector of
p
real-valued constants. Based on vector
\bm{v}
, for
i<j
, we define the following notations.

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{i,j}[x,y]=\bigl({v}_{1},\cdots,{v}_{i-1},x,{v}_{i+1},\cdots,{v}_{j-1},y,{v}_{j+1},\cdots,{v}_{p}\bigr),

(3.125)

\displaystyle\bm{v}[-i,-j]=\bigl({v}_{1},\cdots,{v}_{i-1},{v}_{i+1},\cdots,{v}_{j-1},{v}_{j+1},\cdots,{v}_{p}\bigr),

(3.126)

\displaystyle\bm{v}[i,j]=\bigl({v}_{i},{v}_{j}\bigr).

(3.127)

Let
\bm{X}\sim{\cal{TN}}_{p}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\bm{a},\bm{b})
. By definition, the second (raw) moment of truncated Gaussian distribution is given by

3.12

\displaystyle\bm{\Omega}^{2}=E\bigl(\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\top}\bigr)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}\bm{x}\bm{x}^{\top}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)}{2}\Bigr\}d\bm{x}.

We recall that
{\bf{C}}_{G}=(2\pi)^{p/2}|\Sigma|^{1/2}
,
{\cal{P}}_{G}=\bm{\Phi}_{p}\bigl(\bm{b}\big|\bm{\mu},{\Sigma}\bigr)-\bm{\Phi}_{p}\bigl(\bm{a}\big|\bm{\mu},{\Sigma}\bigr)
. Using matrix algebra, one can see that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\bm{\mu}\partial\bm{\mu}^{\top}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)}{2}\Bigr\}=

\displaystyle\Bigl[{\Sigma}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}-\Sigma^{-1}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\times\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)}{2}\Bigr\}.

(3.128)

Integrating both sides of (
3.128
), then multiplying from left and right by
\Sigma
, and finally rearranging, we have

\displaystyle\bm{\Omega}^{2}=

\displaystyle\bm{\Omega}\bm{\mu}^{\top}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\Omega}^{\top}-\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu}^{\top}+\Sigma+\frac{\Sigma}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\bm{\mu}\partial\bm{\mu}^{\top}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)}{2}\Bigr\}d\bm{x}\Sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\Omega}\bm{\mu}^{\top}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\Omega}^{\top}-\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu}^{\top}+\Sigma+\frac{\Sigma}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\bm{\mu}\partial\bm{\mu}^{\top}}\int_{\bm{a}-\bm{\mu}}^{\bm{b}-\bm{\mu}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta(\bm{u},\Sigma)}{2}\Bigr\}d\bm{u}\Sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\Omega}\bm{\mu}^{\top}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\Omega}^{\top}-\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu}^{\top}+\Sigma+\Sigma\bm{I}\Sigma,

(3.129)

where

\displaystyle\bm{I}=\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\bm{\mu}\partial\bm{\mu}^{\top}}\int_{\bm{a}-\bm{\mu}}^{\bm{b}-\bm{\mu}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta(\bm{u},\Sigma)}{2}\Bigr\}d\bm{u}.

(3.130)

As it may seen,
\bm{I}
is a
p\times p
square matrix whose
(i,j)
th element is shown by
I_{i,j}
. In order to compute
I_{i,j}
, we consider three scenarios given by the following.

•

Scenario 1
:
i\neq j=1,\cdots,p
and
p=2

•

Scenario 2
:
i\neq j=1,\cdots,p
and
p>2

•

Scenario 3
:
i=j=1,\cdots,p

In what follows, we proceed to compute
I_{i,j}
under three scenarios mentioned above.

•

Scenario 1
(
i\neq j=1,\cdots,p
, and
p=2
): It follows from (
3.130
) that

\displaystyle I_{1,2}=\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\mu_{1}\partial\mu_{2}}\int_{a_{1}-\mu_{1}}^{b_{1}-\mu_{1}}\int_{a_{2}-\mu_{2}}^{b_{2}-\mu_{2}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta(\bm{u},\Sigma)}{2}\Bigr\}du_{1}du_{2}.

(3.131)

Applying the Leibniz integral rule for the RHS of (
3.131
), we have

\displaystyle I_{1,2}=\frac{1}{{\cal{P}}_{G}}\Bigl[\phi\bigl(\bm{b}\big|\bm{\mu},\Sigma\bigr)-\phi\bigl((b_{1},a_{2})\big|\bm{\mu},\Sigma\bigr)+\phi\bigl(\bm{a}\big|\bm{\mu},\Sigma\bigr)-\phi\bigl((a_{1},b_{2})\big|\bm{\mu},\Sigma\bigr)\Bigr].

Obviously
I_{2,1}=I_{1,2}
.

•

Scenario 2
(
i\neq j=1,\cdots,p
, and
p>2
):
For a given vector
(x,y)^{\top}
, it can be seen that the quadratic form
\delta\bigl(\bm{u}_{i,j}[x,y],\Sigma\bigr)
, for which
\bm{u}_{i,j}[x,y]
is defined in (
3.125
), can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\delta\bigl(\bm{u}_{i,j}[x,y],\Sigma\bigr)=\delta_{ij}(x,y)+\delta\Bigl(\bm{u}[-i,-j]-\bm{\xi}(x,y),\Delta_{ij}\Bigr),

(3.132)

where
\delta_{ij}(x,y)=(x,y)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}_{{\cal{I}},{\cal{I}}}(x,y)
for
{\cal{I}}=\{i,j\}
and

\displaystyle\bm{\xi}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\Sigma_{-{\cal{I}},{\cal{I}}}\bigl(\Sigma_{{\cal{I}},{\cal{I}}}\bigr)^{-1}(x,y),

(3.133)

\displaystyle\Delta_{ij}=

\displaystyle\Sigma_{-{\cal{I}},-{\cal{I}}}-\Sigma_{-{\cal{I}},{\cal{I}}}\bigl(\Sigma_{{\cal{I}},{\cal{I}}}\bigr)^{-1}\Sigma_{{\cal{I}},-{\cal{I}}}.

(3.134)

Herein,
\bm{\xi}(x,y)
is a vector of length
p-2
,
\Delta_{ij}
is a
(p-2)\times(p-2)
positive definite scale matrix, and
2\times 2
positive definite scale matrix matrix
\Sigma_{{\cal{I}},{\cal{I}}}
is given by

\displaystyle\Sigma_{{\cal{I}},{\cal{I}}}=\biggl[\begin{matrix}\Sigma_{i,i}&\Sigma_{i,j}\\
\Sigma_{j,i}&\Sigma_{j,j}\end{matrix}\biggr].

For constructing
(p-2)\times 2
matrix
\Sigma_{-{\cal{I}},{\cal{I}}}
, first construct a two-column matrix based on the
i
th and
j
th columns of matrix
\Sigma
and then remove
i
th and
j
th rows of the constructed two-column matrix. We apply the Leibniz integral rule twice to the RHS of (
3.130
), and simultaneously utilize the property (
3.132
), to see that

\displaystyle I_{i,j}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta_{ij}(\bm{b}[i,j]-\bm{\mu}[i,j])}{2}\Bigr\}\times I_{1}\bigl(\bm{b}[i,j]\bigr)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta_{ij}\bigl((\bm{a}[i],\bm{b}[j])-\bm{\mu}[i,j]\bigr)}{2}\Bigr\}\times I_{1}\bigl(\bm{a}[i],\bm{b}[j]\bigr)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta_{ij}\bigl((\bm{b}[i],\bm{a}[j])-\bm{\mu}[i,j]\bigr)}{2}\Bigr\}\times I_{1}\bigl(\bm{b}[i],\bm{a}[j]\bigr)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta_{ij}(\bm{a}[i,j]-\bm{\mu}[i,j])}{2}\Bigr\}\times I_{1}\bigl(\bm{a}[i,j]\bigr),

(3.135)

where

\displaystyle I_{1}(\bm{z})=

\displaystyle\int_{\bm{a}[-i,-j]-\bm{\mu}[-i,-j]}^{\bm{b}[-i,-j]-\bm{\mu}[-i,-j]}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{2}\delta\Bigl(\bm{u}[-i,-j]-\bm{\xi}(\bm{z}),\Delta_{ij}\Bigr)\Bigr\}d\bm{u}[-i,-j].

(3.136)

We further notice that the quantity
{\bf{C}}_{G}
can be represented as

\displaystyle{\bf{C}}_{G}=(2\pi)^{\frac{p}{2}}|\Sigma|^{\frac{1}{2}}={2\pi\big|\Sigma_{{\cal{I}},{\cal{I}}}\big|^{\frac{1}{2}}}(2\pi)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\big|\Delta_{ij}\big|^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(3.137)

Substituting (
3.137
) into the RHS of (
3.135
), it follows that

\displaystyle I_{i,j}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\cal{P}}_{G}}\bm{\phi}_{2}\Bigl(\bm{b}[i,j]\Big|\bm{\mu}[i,j],\Sigma_{{\cal{I}},{\cal{I}}}\Bigr)\times I_{2}(\bm{b}[i,j])

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{{\cal{P}}_{G}}\bm{\phi}_{2}\Bigl((\bm{a}[i],\bm{b}[j])\Big|\bm{\mu}[i,j],\Sigma_{{\cal{I}},{\cal{I}}}\Bigr)\times I_{2}(\bm{a}[i],\bm{b}[j])

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{{\cal{P}}_{G}}\bm{\phi}_{2}\Bigl((\bm{b}[i],\bm{a}[j])\Big|\bm{\mu}[i,j],\Sigma_{{\cal{I}},{\cal{I}}}\Bigr)\times I_{2}(\bm{b}[i],\bm{a}[j])

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{{\cal{P}}_{G}}\bm{\phi}_{2}\Bigl(\bm{b}[i,j]\Big|\bm{\mu}[i,j],\Sigma_{{\cal{I}},{\cal{I}}}\Bigr)\times I_{2}(\bm{a}[i,j]),

(3.138)

where

\displaystyle I_{2}(\bm{z})=

\displaystyle\bm{\Phi}_{p-2}\Bigl(\bm{b}[-i,-j]-\bm{\mu}[-i,-j]\Big|\bm{\xi}(\bm{z}),\Delta_{ij}\Bigr)

\displaystyle-\bm{\Phi}_{p-2}\Bigl(\bm{b}[-i,-j]-\bm{\mu}[-i,-j]\Big|\bm{\xi}(\bm{z}),\Delta_{ij}\Bigr).

The matrix
\bm{I}
is constructed by computing
I_{i,j}
in (
3.138
) for all
i\neq j=1,\cdots,p
. The second moment of truncated Gaussian distribution is then obtained by substituting
\bm{I}
into the RHS of (
3.129
).

•

Scenario 3
(
i=j=1,\cdots,p
): Using the Leibniz integral rule, the first order derivative of the RHS of (
3.130
) is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial{\mu}_{i}}\bm{I}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\frac{\partial}{\partial{\mu}_{i}}\int_{\bm{a}-\bm{\mu}}^{\bm{b}-\bm{\mu}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta(\bm{u},\Sigma)}{2}\Bigr\}d\bm{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{G}}\int_{\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]}^{\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]}\biggl[\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}[a_{i}-\mu_{i}],\Sigma\bigr)}{2}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle-\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}[b_{i}-\mu_{i}],\Sigma\bigr)}{2}\Bigr\}\biggr]d\bm{u}[-i],

(3.139)

for
i=1,\cdots,p
. We note that the quadratic form
\delta\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}[x],\Sigma\bigr)
, for which
\bm{u}_{i}[x]
is defined in (
3.125
), can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\delta\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}[x],\Sigma\bigr)=\delta_{ii}(x,x)+\delta\Bigl(\bm{u}[-i]-\bm{\xi}(x),\Delta_{ii}\Bigr),

(3.140)

where
\delta_{ii}(x,x)=\bigl(x_{i}-\mu_{i}\bigr)^{2}\times\bigl(\Sigma_{i,i}\bigr)^{-1}
is a scalar and

\displaystyle\bm{\xi}(x)=

\displaystyle\Sigma_{i,-i}\times\bigl(\Sigma_{i,i}\bigr)^{-1}\times x,

(3.141)

\displaystyle\Delta_{ii}=

\displaystyle\Sigma_{-i,-i}-\Sigma_{i,-i}\bigl(\Sigma_{i,-i}\bigr)^{\top}\times\bigl(\Sigma_{i,i}\bigr)^{-1}.

(3.142)

We note that
\bm{\xi}(x)
is a vector of length
p-1
and
\Delta_{ii}
is a
(p-1)\times(p-1)
positive definite scale matrix. We further notice that the quantity
{\bf{C}}_{G}
can be represented as

\displaystyle{\bf{C}}_{G}={(2\pi)^{\frac{1}{2}}\big|\Sigma_{i,i}\big|^{\frac{1}{2}}}(2\pi)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\big|\Delta_{i}\big|^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(3.143)

Using information given in (
3.140
), (
3.141
), (
3.142
), and (
3.143
), the RHS of (
3.139
) can be represented as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial{\mu}_{i}}\bm{I}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\cal{P}}_{G}}{\phi}\Bigl(a_{i}\Big|{\mu}_{i},\Sigma_{i,i}\Bigr)\times\biggl[\bm{\Phi}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]\Big|\bm{\xi}({a}_{i}-{\mu}_{i}),\Delta_{ii}\Bigr)

\displaystyle-\bm{\Phi}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]\Big|\bm{\xi}({a}_{i}-{\mu}_{i}),\Delta_{ii}\Bigr)\biggr]-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\cal{P}}_{G}}{\phi}\Bigl({b}_{i}\Big|{\mu}_{i},\Sigma_{i,i}\Bigr)\times\biggl[\bm{\Phi}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}_{i}\Big|\bm{\xi}({b}_{i}-{\mu}_{i}),\Delta_{ii}\Bigr)

\displaystyle-\bm{\Phi}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]\Big|\bm{\xi}({b}_{i}-{\mu}_{i}),\Delta_{ii}\Bigr)\biggr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{I}_{ia}-{I}_{ib}.

(3.144)

Now, taking the second partial derivative with respect to
\mu_{i}
from RHS of (
3.144
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{I}_{ia}}{\partial{\mu}_{i}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(a_{i}-{\mu}_{i}\bigr)}{\Sigma_{i,i}\times{\cal{P}}_{G}}{\phi}\Bigl(a_{i}\Big|{\mu}_{i},\Sigma_{i,i}\Bigr)\biggl[\bm{\Phi}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]\Big|\bm{\xi}({a}_{i}-{\mu}_{i}),\Delta_{ii}\Bigr)-

\displaystyle\bm{\Phi}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]\Big|\bm{\xi}({a}_{i}-{\mu}_{i}),\Delta_{ii}\Bigr)\biggr]-\frac{{\phi}\bigl(a_{i}\big|{\mu}_{i},\Sigma_{i,i}\bigr)}{\Sigma_{i,i}\times{\cal{P}}_{G}}\times\Sigma_{-i,i}\bigl(\Delta_{ii}\bigr)^{-1}\times

\displaystyle\int_{\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]}^{\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]}\Bigl(\bm{u}[-i]-\bm{\xi}({a}_{i}-{\mu}_{i})\Bigr)\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\delta\bigl(\bm{u}[-i]-\bm{\xi}({a}_{i}-{\mu}_{i}),\Delta_{ii}\bigr)}{2}\Bigr\}d\bm{u}[-i].

(3.145)

Clearly, the RHS of (
3.145
) can be represented in terms of the first moment of a truncated Gaussian distribution. That means

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{I}_{ia}}{\partial{\mu}_{i}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Sigma_{i,i}\times{\cal{P}}_{G}}{\phi}\Bigl(a_{i}\Big|{\mu}_{i},\Sigma_{i,i}\Bigr)\biggl[\bm{\Phi}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]\Big|\bm{\xi}({a}_{i}-{\mu}_{i}),\Delta_{ii}\Bigr)-

\displaystyle\bm{\Phi}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]\Big|\bm{\xi}({a}_{i}-{\mu}_{i}),\Delta_{ii}\Bigr)\biggr]\times\Bigl[a_{i}-\mu_{i}-\Sigma_{-i,i}\bigl(\Delta_{ii}\bigr)^{-1}\bm{\Omega}_{a}\Bigr],

(3.146)

where

\displaystyle\bm{\Omega}_{a}=E\Bigl(\bm{Y}_{a}\Big|\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]-\bm{\xi}({a}_{i}-{\mu}_{i})<\bm{Y}_{a}<\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]-\bm{\xi}({a}_{i}-{\mu}_{i})\Bigr),

in which
\bm{Y}_{a}\sim{\cal{N}}_{p-1}\bigl(\bm{0},\Delta_{ii}\bigr)
. For computing
\partial{I}_{ib}/\partial{\mu}_{i}
, we likewise have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{I}_{ib}}{\partial{\mu}_{i}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Sigma_{i,i}\times{\cal{P}}_{G}}{\phi}\Bigl(b_{i}\Big|{\mu}_{i},\Sigma_{i,i}\Bigr)\biggl[\bm{\Phi}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]\Big|\bm{\xi}({b}_{i}-{\mu}_{i}),\Delta_{ii}\Bigr)-

\displaystyle\bm{\Phi}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]\Big|\bm{\xi}({b}_{i}-{\mu}_{i}),\Delta_{ii}\Bigr)\biggr]\times\Bigl[b_{i}-\mu_{i}-\Sigma_{-i,i}\bigl(\Delta_{ii}\bigr)^{-1}\bm{\Omega}_{b}\Bigr],

(3.147)

where

\displaystyle\bm{\Omega}_{b}=E\Bigl(\bm{Y}_{b}\Big|\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]-\bm{\xi}({b}_{i}-{\mu}_{i})<\bm{Y}_{b}<\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]-\bm{\xi}({b}_{i}-{\mu}_{i})\Bigr),

in which
\bm{Y}_{b}\sim{\cal{N}}_{p-1}\bigl(\bm{0},\Delta_{ii}\bigr)
. Once we have computed
\partial{I}_{ia}/\partial{\mu}_{i}
in (
3.146
) and
\partial{I}_{ib}/\partial{\mu}_{i}
in (
3.147
), for
i=1,\cdots,p
, then the diagonal elements of
\bm{I}
given by (
3.129
) are available.

Once we have construct matrix
\bm{I}
, we can compute
\bm{\Omega}^{2}
by substituting computed
\bm{I}
at the RHS of (
3.129
).

Herein, we carry out a small simulation study for computing the first moment of vector
(X,Y)^{\top}
under two scenarios. Within the first scenario, we assume that
(X,Y)^{\top}
follows truncated bivariate Gaussian distribution with parameters
\bm{\mu}=\bm{0}
,
\Sigma\penalty\ \bigl(\sigma_{1}^{2}=2,\sigma_{2}^{2}=1,\rho=0.3535\bigr)
truncated on area with lower limits
\bm{a}=(-1,-1)^{\top}
and upper limits
\bm{b}=(2,2)^{\top}
. Under the second scenario it is assumed that
(X,Y)^{\top}
follows truncated bivariate Gaussian distribution with parameters
\bm{\mu}=\bm{0}
,
\Sigma

\bigl(\sigma_{1}^{2}=2,\sigma_{2}^{2}=2,\rho=0.25\bigr)
truncated on area with lower limits
\bm{a}=(0,0)^{\top}
and upper limits
\bm{b}=(+\infty,+\infty)^{\top}
. We note that the concern of the second scenario is placed on computing the absolute moment, that is
\bm{\Omega}=E(|\bm{X}|)
. For both scenario, we compute
\bm{\Omega}
by generating samples of sizes
N=2000,5000
, and 20000 based on 5000 runs. The instrumental distribution is set to be
{\cal{N}}_{2}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma)
. For computing
\bm{\Omega}=E\bigl(\bm{{\cal{X}}}_{a,b}\bigr)
through the
{\cal{N}}_{2}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma)
, one can follow two ways. In the first way the quantity
\bm{\Omega}
is estimated as

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\Omega}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\bm{x}_{i}\big|

(3.148)

where
\big|\bm{x}_{i}\big|=\bigl(\big|x_{1i}\big|,\big|x_{2i}\big|\bigr)^{\top}
where
\bm{x}_{i}
s come independently from
{\cal{N}}_{2}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma)
, for sufficiently large
N
. The second method proceeds to compute
\hat{\bm{\Omega}}
based on sample of size
N
when the lower and upper truncation bounds are
\bm{a}=\bm{0}
and
\bm{b}=+\bm{\infty}
, respectively. Details for implementing the second method are given as follows. The corresponding
R
function
EXX
for computing
\bm{\Omega}
is given by the following.

⬇

3.13
Truncated skew Gaussian distribution

Here,
we introduce the class of skew Gaussian distributions that is known in the literature as the canonical fundamental unrestricted skew Gaussian distribution, see
Arellano-Valle and Azzalini (2006)
.
We write
\bm{X}\sim\text{SG}_{p,q}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,{\Lambda})
to denote that
p
-dimensional random vector
\bm{X}
follows a canonical fundamental unrestricted skew Gaussian distribution with PDF given by

\displaystyle f(\bm{x}\big|\bm{\mu},{\Sigma},{{\Lambda}})=2^{q}\bm{\phi}_{p}\bigl(\bm{x}|\bm{\mu},{\Omega}\bigr)\bm{\Phi}_{q}\bigl(\bm{m}|\bm{0}_{q},{\Delta}\bigr),

(3.149)

where
{\Omega}={\Sigma}+{{\Lambda}}{{\Lambda}}^{\top}
,
{\Delta}=\bm{I}_{q}-{{\Lambda}}^{\top}{\Omega}^{-1}{{\Lambda}}
,
\bm{m}={\Lambda}^{\top}{{\Omega}}^{-1}(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu})
, and
\bm{I}_{q}
denotes the

q\times q
identity matrix. Further,
\bm{\phi}_{p}\bigl(.\big|\bm{\mu},{\Omega}\bigr)
denotes the PDF of a
p
-dimensional Gaussian distribution with location vector
\bm{\mu}
and dispersion matrix
\Sigma
, and
\bm{\Phi}_{q}\bigl(.\big|\bm{0}_{q},{\Delta}\bigr)
is the CDF of a
q
-dimensional Gaussian distribution with location vector
\bm{0}_{q}
(a vector of zeros of length
q
) and dispersion matrix
{\Delta}
. The random vector
\bm{X}
admits stochastic representation as follows, see
(
Arellano-Valle and Genton, 2005
;
Arellano-Valle and Azzalini, 2006
;
Arellano-Valle et al., 2007
)
.

\displaystyle\bm{X}\mathop{=}\limits^{d}\bm{\mu}+{\Lambda}\big|\bm{Z}_{0}\big|+{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{Z}_{1},

(3.150)

where
\mathop{=}\limits^{d}
means “distributed as” and random vectors
\bm{Z}_{0}\sim{\cal{N}}_{q}\bigl(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{q}\bigr)
and
\bm{Z}_{1}\sim{\cal{N}}_{q}\bigl(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{p}\bigr)
are independent.
Furthermore, it can be seen that
(
Lee and McLachlan, 2016
;
Maleki et al., 2019
;
Morales et al., 2022
)

\displaystyle\bm{X}\mathop{=}\limits^{d}\bm{\mu}+\bigl(\bm{W}\big|\bm{W}_{0}>\bm{0}\bigr),

(3.151)

in which

\displaystyle\bm{Y}=\biggl[\begin{matrix}\bm{W}_{0}\\
\bm{W}_{1}\end{matrix}\biggr]\sim{\cal{N}}_{q+p}\biggl(\biggl[\begin{matrix}\bm{0}_{q}\\
\bm{0}_{p}\end{matrix}\biggr],\biggl[\begin{matrix}\bm{I}_{q}&\bm{\Lambda}^{\top}\\
\bm{\Lambda}&{\Sigma}+\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Lambda}^{\top}\\
\end{matrix}\biggr]\biggr).

(3.152)

By the property (
3.152
), we can construct a method for simulating from
\bm{X}\big|\bm{a}<\bm{X}<\bm{b}
when
\bm{X}
admits relation (
3.150
). To this end, we need to simulate from
(p+q)
-dimensional Gaussian random vector
\bm{Y}
given by (
3.152
). Some algebra show

\displaystyle\bm{X}\big|\bigl(\bm{a}<\bm{X}<\bm{b}\bigr)\mathop{=}\limits^{d}\bm{\mu}^{*}+\bm{Y}\big|\bigl(\bm{a}^{*}-\bm{\mu}^{*}<\bm{Y}<\bm{b}^{*}-\bm{\mu}^{*}\bigr),

where
a^{*}=\bigl(\bm{0}^{\top}_{q},\bm{a}^{\top}\bigr)^{\top}
,
b^{*}=\bigl(\bm{\infty}^{\top}_{q},\bm{b}^{\top}\bigr)^{\top}
, and
\bm{\mu}^{*}=\bigl(\bm{0}^{\top}_{q},\bm{\mu}^{\top}\bigr)^{\top}
in which
\bm{\infty}_{q}
is a vector of length
q
of infinities.
Hence, using the methodology given in Sections
3.11
and
3.12
for computing the first and second moments of multivariate Gaussian distribution, we can compute the first and second moments of truncated skew Gaussian distribution by computing
E\bigl(\bm{Y}\big|\bm{a}^{*}<\bm{Y}<\bm{b}^{*}\bigr)
and
E\bigl(\bm{Y}\bm{Y}^{\top}\big|\bm{a}^{*}<\bm{Y}<\bm{b}^{*}\bigr)
, respectively. We note that an
R
package called
MomTrunc
uploaded at
https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/MomTrunc/inde

x.html
developed for this aim
(
Morales et al., 2022
)
. The following
R
code can be used for computing first two moments of the truncated random vector
\bm{X}
where
\bm{X}\sim\text{SG}_{2,2}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,{\Lambda})
truncated on
(\bm{a},\bm{b})
in which
\bm{\mu}=(0,0)^{\top}
,
\bm{a}=(-3,-3)^{\top}
,
\bm{b}=(2,2)^{\top}
,

\displaystyle\Sigma=\left[\begin{matrix}2&0.5\\
0.5&2\\
\end{matrix}\right],\Lambda=\left[\begin{matrix}4&2\\
2&-5\\
\end{matrix}\right].

⬇

3.14
Gaussian scale mixture model

Here, we introduce the useful property known in the literature as
Gaussian scale mixture model
. Based on this property, one can represent the PDF and other characteristics of a wide range of distributions in terms of the Gaussian model.

Definition 3.14.1
.

Let random variable
Z
follows a standard Gaussian distribution. For given positive valued function
h(\cdot)
and positive random variable
G
, the random variable
X
is said to follow a Gaussian scale mixture model if we can write

\displaystyle X=\mu+\sigma\frac{Z}{h(G)},

(3.153)

where
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
and
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
are the location and scale parameters, respectively. Herein, and random variable
G
is the mixing variable.

Likewise, the multivariate version of the Gaussian scale mixture model (representation) is given as follows.

Definition 3.14.2
.

Let random variable
\bm{Z}
follows a
p
-dimensional standard Gaussian distribution. For given positive valued function
h(\cdot)
and positive random variable
G
. The
p
-dimensional random vector
\bm{X}
is said to follow a Gaussian scale mixture model if we have

\displaystyle\bm{X}=\bm{\mu}+A\frac{\bm{Z}}{h(G)}

(3.154)

where
\bm{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
p\times p
lower triangular matrix
A
is called the Cholesky decomposition of positive definite scale matrix
\Sigma
such that
\Sigma=AA^{\top}
. Herein, the univariate random variable
G
plays the role of the mixing variable.

The well-known example of Gaussian scale mixture model is the Student’s
t
distribution, with PDF given by (
3.155
), for which
h(G)=\sqrt{G}
where
G\sim{\cal{G}}(\nu/2,\nu/2)
.

3.15
Moment of truncated Student’s
t
distribution

Let random vector
\bm{X}=(X_{1},\cdots,X_{p})^{\top}
follows a
p
-dimensional Student’s
t
distribution with
\nu>0
degrees of freedom with PDF given by

\displaystyle f(\bm{x}\big|\bm{\Psi}\bigr)=

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu+p}{2}\bigr)}{(\nu\pi)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}\bigr)|\Sigma|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\biggl[1+\frac{\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)}{\nu}\biggr]^{-\frac{\nu+p}{2}},

(3.155)

where
\bm{\Psi}=(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\nu)
is the parameter vector and Mahalanobis distance
\delta(\cdot)
is defined in (
3.114
). By definition

\displaystyle E(\bm{X})=

\displaystyle\int_{-\bm{\infty}}^{\bm{\infty}}\frac{\bm{x}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu+p}{2}\bigr)}{(\nu\pi)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}\bigr)|\Sigma|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\biggl[1+\frac{\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)}{\nu}\biggr]^{-\frac{\nu+p}{2}}d\bm{x},

(3.156)

for
\nu>1
. The first moment of the truncated Student’s
t
distribution (shown by
{\cal{TT}}_{p}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\nu,\bm{a},\bm{b})
) can be evaluated through relation (
3.105
) in which
f(\bm{u})
is replaced with
f(\bm{x}\big|\bm{\Psi}\bigr)
given in (
3.155
) and
F(\bm{b})-F(\bm{a})=P(\bm{a}<\bm{X}<\bm{b})=\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}f(\bm{x}\big|\bm{\Psi}\bigr)d\bm{x}
. However, herein, we proceed to compute
\bm{\Omega}=E\bigl(\bm{{{X}}}\big|\bm{a}<\bm{X}<\bm{b}\bigr)
based on the property of
Gaussian scale mixture model
introduced in Section
3.14
.

3.15.1
First two moments of truncated Student’s
t
distribution distribution in univariate case

Herein, we proceed to compute the first and second moments of an univariate Student’s
t
distribution. For simplicity, we use generic symbols
{t}\bigl({x}\big|{\mu},\sigma^{2},\nu\bigr)
and
{T}\bigl({x}\big|{\mu},\sigma^{2},\nu\bigr)
to denote, accordingly, the PDF and CDF of a Student’s
t
distribution with
\nu
degrees of freedom at point
{x}
for which
{\mu}
,
\sigma
, and
\nu
play the role of location, scale, and degrees of freedom parameters, respectively. Furthermore, we write
{X}\sim{t}({\mu},\sigma^{2},\nu)
to show that random variable
{X}
follows a Student’s
t
distribution with location parameter
{\mu}
, scale parameter
\sigma
, and
\nu
degrees of freedom. The corresponding truncated family on
(a,b)
is represented by
{\cal{TT}}\bigl({\mu},\sigma^{2},\nu,a,b\bigr)
.

If
X\sim t\bigl(\mu,\sigma^{2},\nu\bigr)
, it can be seen from (
3.103
) for
m=1
that

\displaystyle\Omega=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{a}^{b}x\times{t}\bigl({x}\big|{\mu},\sigma^{2},\nu\bigr)dx,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{a}^{b}\frac{x\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu+1}{2}\bigr)}{\sqrt{\nu\pi}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}\bigr)\sigma}\biggl[1+\frac{({x}-{\mu})^{2}}{\nu\sigma^{2}}\biggr]^{-\frac{\nu+1}{2}}d{x},

(3.157)

where
{\cal{P}}_{t}=T\bigl({b}\big|{\mu},\sigma^{2},\nu\bigr)-{T}\bigl({a}\big|{\mu},\sigma^{2},\nu\bigr)
. Rather computing
\Omega
through (
3.157
), we prefer to use Definition
3.14.1
that states each Student’s
t
distribution is a Gaussian scale mixture model. In fact
{X}
admits the hierarchy given by

\displaystyle{X}|G

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{N}}\Bigl({\mu},\frac{\sigma^{2}}{{G}}\Bigr),

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{G}}\Bigl(\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr).

Using above hierarchy, the quantity
\Omega
can be computed as follows.

\displaystyle\Omega

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{a}^{b}{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\phi}\Bigl({x}\Big|\bm{\mu},\frac{\sigma^{2}}{g}\Bigr){\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dgd{x},

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{C_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{0}^{\infty}g^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{a}^{b}{x}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr\}dx{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dg,

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{C_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{0}^{\infty}g^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int_{a}^{b}\frac{g(x-\mu)}{\sigma^{2}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr\}dx{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dg+\mu,

where
C_{G}=\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma
. On the other hand, since

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr\}=

\displaystyle g\times\Bigl(\frac{x-\mu}{\sigma^{2}}\Bigr)\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr\},

(3.158)

it turns out that

\displaystyle\Omega=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma^{2}}{C_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{0}^{\infty}g^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\int_{a}^{b}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr\}dx{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dg+\mu,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma^{2}}{C_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{0}^{\infty}g^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\int_{a-\mu}^{b-\mu}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{gu^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr\}du{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dg+\mu,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma^{2}}{C_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{0}^{\infty}g^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Bigl[\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g(a-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr\}-\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g(b-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr\}\Bigr]{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dg+\mu,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma^{2}}{C_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}\bigr)}\Bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}g^{\frac{\nu-1}{2}-1}\exp\Bigl\{-g\Bigl[\frac{(a-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}+\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr]\Bigr\}dg

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{C_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}\bigr)}\Bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}g^{\frac{\nu-1}{2}-1}\exp\Bigl\{-g\Bigl[\frac{(b-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}+\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr]\Bigr\}dg+\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma^{2}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu-1}{2}\bigr)}{C_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}\bigr)}\Bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}\biggl[\Bigl[\frac{(a-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}+\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr]^{-\frac{\nu-1}{2}}+\Bigl[\frac{(b-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}+\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr]^{-\frac{\nu-1}{2}}\biggr]+\mu.

(3.159)

Rearranging the RHS of (
3.159
) in terms of Student’s
t
PDF, we obtain

\displaystyle\Omega=\frac{\sigma_{\nu}}{{\cal{P}}_{t}}\biggl[t\Bigl(\frac{a-\mu}{\sigma_{\nu}}\Big|0,1,\nu-2\Bigr)-t\Bigl(\frac{b-\mu}{\sigma_{\nu}}\Big|0,1,\nu-2\Bigr)\biggr]+\mu,

(3.160)

where
\sigma_{\nu}=\sigma\sqrt{\nu}/\sqrt{\nu-2}
for
\nu>2
. Applying the above argument to computing
\Omega
, we have

\displaystyle\Omega^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{a}^{b}{x^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\phi}\Bigl({x}\Big|\bm{\mu},\frac{\sigma^{2}}{g}\Bigr){\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dgd{x}.

For computing
\Omega^{2}
, we take into account the fact that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\mu^{2}}\exp\Bigl\{-g\frac{(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr\}=

\displaystyle\Bigl[-\frac{g}{\sigma^{2}}+g^{2}\times\Bigl(\frac{x-\mu}{\sigma^{2}}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigr]\exp\Bigl\{-g\frac{(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr\}.

(3.161)

Rearranging equation (
3.161
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{g^{2}x^{2}}{\sigma^{4}}\exp\Bigl\{-g\frac{(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr\}=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\mu^{2}}\exp\Bigl\{-g\frac{(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle+\biggl[\Bigl(\frac{g}{\sigma^{2}}-\frac{\mu^{2}}{\sigma^{4}}g^{2}+2\frac{\mu}{\sigma^{4}}g^{2}x\Bigr)\exp\Bigl\{-g\frac{(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr\}\biggr].

(3.162)

Multiplying both sides of (
3.162
) by
\sigma^{4}g^{-3/2}{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)
, then integrating over
x
and
g
, and finally dividing by
C_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}
, we have

\displaystyle\Omega^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma^{4}}{C_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{0}^{\infty}g^{-\frac{3}{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\mu^{2}}\int_{a}^{b}\exp\Bigl\{-g\frac{(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr\}{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dgd{x}

\displaystyle+\biggl[\frac{\sigma^{2}}{C_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}}{\cal{R}}_{-\frac{1}{2},0}-\frac{\mu^{2}}{C_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}}{\cal{R}}_{\frac{1}{2},0}+2\frac{\mu}{C_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}}{\cal{R}}_{\frac{1}{2},1}\biggr],

(3.163)

where

\displaystyle{\cal{R}}_{i,j}=

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\int_{0}^{\infty}g^{i}x^{j}\exp\Bigl\{-g\frac{(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr\}{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dgdx.

(3.164)

For integral in the RHS of (
3.163
), it is not hard to check that

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma^{4}}{C_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{0}^{\infty}g^{-\frac{3}{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\mu^{2}}\int_{a}^{b}\exp\Bigl\{-g\frac{(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr\}{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dgdx

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{\nu}}{{\cal{P}}_{t}}(a-\mu)t\Bigl(\frac{a-\mu}{\sigma_{\nu}}\Big|0,1,\nu-2\Bigr)-\frac{\sigma_{\nu}}{{\cal{P}}_{t}}(b-\mu)t\Bigl(\frac{b-\mu}{\sigma_{\nu}}\Big|0,1,\nu-2\Bigr),

(3.165)

where
\sigma_{\nu}=\sigma\sqrt{\nu}/\sqrt{\nu-2}
for
\nu>2
. Hence, replacing the RHS of (
3.165
) with integral in RHS of (
3.163
), we have

\displaystyle\Omega^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{\nu}}{{\cal{P}}_{t}}(a-\mu)t\Bigl(\frac{a-\mu}{\sigma_{\nu}}\Big|0,1,\nu-2\Bigr)-\frac{\sigma_{\nu}}{{\cal{P}}_{t}}(b-\mu)t\Bigl(\frac{b-\mu}{\sigma_{\nu}}\Big|0,1,\nu-2\Bigr)

\displaystyle+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{C_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}}{\cal{R}}_{-\frac{1}{2},0}-\frac{\mu^{2}}{C_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}}{\cal{R}}_{\frac{1}{2},0}+2\frac{\mu}{C_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}}{\cal{R}}_{\frac{1}{2},1}.

(3.166)

Moreover, it may seen, for
i>(1-\nu)/2
and
j=\{0,1\}
, that

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\cal{R}}_{i,j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle\frac{2^{i}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu(i)}{2}\bigr)\sigma_{i}\nu^{-i}}{\bigl[\pi\nu(i)\bigr]^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}\bigr)}\biggl[T\Bigl(\frac{b-\mu}{\sigma_{i}}\Big|0,1,\nu(i)\Bigr)-T\Bigl(\frac{a-\mu}{\sigma_{i}}\Big|0,1,\nu(i)\Bigr)\biggr],\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{{if}}\ j=0,\\
\displaystyle\frac{2^{i}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu(i)}{2}\bigr)\sigma_{i}\nu^{-i}}{\bigl[\pi\nu(i)\bigr]^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}\bigr)}\biggl[T\Bigl(\frac{b-\mu}{\sigma_{i}}\Big|0,1,\nu(i)\Bigr)-T\Bigl(\frac{a-\mu}{\sigma_{i}}\Big|0,1,\nu(i)\Bigr)\biggr]\Omega_{*}^{j},\mathrm{if}\ j>0,\end{array}\right.

where
\sigma_{i}=\sigma\sqrt{\nu}/\sqrt{\nu+2i-1}
(for
i>(1-\nu)/2
),
\nu(i)=\nu+2i-1
, and
\Omega_{*}^{j}=E\bigl(Y^{j}\big|a<Y<b\bigr)
is the
j
th moment of
Y\sim{\cal{TT}}\bigl(\mu,\sigma_{i}^{2},\nu(i),a,b\bigr)
. Computing the quantity
{\cal{R}}_{i,j}
represented in (
3.166
) using (
3.15.1
), for
i=\{-1/2,1/2\}
and
j=\{0,1\}
, and then replacing the results in the RHS of (
3.163
), one can compute the quantity
\Omega^{2}
. The
R

3.16
First moment of truncated Student’s
t
distribution in multivariate case

For simplicity, we use generic symbols
\bm{t}_{p}\bigl(\bm{x}\big|\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\nu\bigr)
and
\bm{T}_{p}\bigl(\bm{x}\big|\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\nu\bigr)
to denote, accordingly, the PDF and CDF of a
p
-dimensional Student’s
t
distribution with
\nu
degrees of freedom at point
\bm{x}
for which
\bm{\mu}
,
\Sigma
, and
\nu
play the role of location, scale, and degrees of freedom parameters, respectively. Furthermore, we write
\bm{X}\sim\bm{t}_{p}\bigl(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\nu\bigr)
to indicate that random vector
\bm{X}
follows a Student’s
t
distribution with location vector
\bm{\mu}
, scale matrix
\Sigma
, and
\nu
degrees of freedom. The corresponding family of Student’s
t
distributions truncated on on hyper-cube
(\bm{a},\bm{b})\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
is shown by
{\cal{TT}}_{p}\bigl(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\nu,\bm{a},\bm{b}\bigr)
.

Recalling from Definition
3.14.2
that each Student’s
t
distribution is a Gaussian scale mixture model. In fact if
\bm{X}\sim\bm{t}_{p}\bigl(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\nu\bigr)
, then it admits the hierarchy given by

\displaystyle\bm{X}|G

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{N}}_{p}\Bigl(\bm{\mu},\frac{\Sigma}{{G}}\Bigr),

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{G}}\Bigl(\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr).

(3.170)

Using above hierarchy, the quantity
E(\bm{X})
in (
3.156
) can be represented as

\displaystyle E(\bm{X})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\bm{\infty}}^{\bm{\infty}}\bm{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bm{\phi}_{p}\Bigl(\bm{x}\Big|\bm{\mu},\frac{\Sigma}{g}\Bigr){\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dgd\bm{x}.

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}}\int_{0}^{\infty}g^{\frac{p}{2}}\int_{-\bm{\infty}}^{\bm{\infty}}\bm{x}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g}{2}\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)\Bigr\}d\bm{x}{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dg,

where
{\bf{C}}_{G}=(2\pi)^{p/2}\big|\Sigma\big|^{1/2}
. The first moment of truncated Student’s
t
distribution is

\displaystyle\bm{\Omega}=E(\bm{X}|\bm{a}<\bm{X}<\bm{b})=\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}g^{\frac{p}{2}}\bm{x}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g}{2}\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)\Bigr\}d\bm{x}{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dg,

where
{\cal{P}}_{t}=P(\bm{a}<\bm{X}<\bm{b})
.
We can write

\displaystyle\bm{\Omega}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}g^{\frac{p}{2}}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g}{2}\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)\Bigr\}d\bm{x}{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dg

\displaystyle+\frac{\bm{\mu}}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}g^{\frac{p}{2}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g}{2}\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)\Bigr\}d\bm{x}{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dg

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\bf{C}}_{G}{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}g^{\frac{p}{2}}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g}{2}\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)\Bigr\}d\bm{x}{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dg+\bm{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{I}+\bm{\mu}.

(3.171)

Since

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{\mu}}\frac{g}{2}\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)=-g{\Sigma}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}),

we have

\displaystyle\bm{I}=

\displaystyle\frac{\Sigma}{{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{g^{\frac{p}{2}-1}}{{\bf{C}}_{G}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{\mu}}\int_{\bm{a}}^{\bm{b}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g}{2}\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu},\Sigma)\Bigr\}d\bm{x}{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dg.

Applying the change of variable
\bm{u}=\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\bm{I}=

\displaystyle\frac{\Sigma}{{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{g^{\frac{p}{2}-1}}{{\bf{C}}_{G}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{\mu}}\int_{\bm{a}-\bm{\mu}}^{\bm{b}-\bm{\mu}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g}{2}\delta(\bm{u},\Sigma)\Bigr\}d\bm{u}{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dg

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl({{I}}_{1},{{I}}_{2},\cdots,{{I}}_{p}\bigr)^{\top},

where

\displaystyle{{I}}_{i}=

\displaystyle\frac{\Sigma}{{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{g^{\frac{p}{2}-1}}{{\bf{C}}_{G}}\int_{\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]}^{\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]}\Biggl[\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g\delta\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}[a_{i}-\mu_{i}],\Sigma\bigr)}{2}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle-\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g\delta\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}[b_{i}-\mu_{i}],\Sigma\bigr)}{2}\Bigr\}\Biggr]d\bm{u}[-i]{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)dg,

(3.172)

for
i=1,\cdots,p
. Using identity (
3.119
) and the fact that

\displaystyle\frac{g^{\frac{p}{2}-1}}{{\bf{C}}_{G}}=\Bigl[\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\Sigma_{i,i}}}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\big|\Delta_{ii}\big|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Bigr]\times g^{\frac{p}{2}-1}=\frac{g^{-1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\Bigl[\frac{\Sigma_{i,i}}{g}\Bigr]^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}}\Big|\frac{\Delta_{ii}}{g}\Big|^{-\frac{1}{2}},

the integrand in the RHS of (
3.172
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle I_{i}=

\displaystyle\frac{\Sigma}{{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{g^{-1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\Bigl[\frac{\Sigma_{i,i}}{g}\Bigr]^{-\frac{1}{2}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g(a_{i}-\mu_{i})^{2}}{2\Sigma_{i,i}}\Bigr\}{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\times\int_{\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]}^{\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}}\Big|\frac{\Delta_{ii}}{g}\Big|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g}{2}\delta\Bigl(\bm{u}[-i]-\bm{\xi}(a_{i}-\mu_{i}),\Delta_{ii}\Bigr)\Bigr\}d\bm{u}[-i]dg

\displaystyle-\frac{\Sigma}{{\cal{P}}_{t}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{g^{-1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\Bigl[\frac{\Sigma_{i,i}}{g}\Bigr]^{-\frac{1}{2}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g(b_{i}-\mu_{i})^{2}}{2\Sigma_{i,i}}\Bigr\}{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\times\int_{\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]}^{\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}}\Big|\frac{\Delta_{ii}}{g}\Big|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g}{2}\delta\Bigl(\bm{u}[-i]-\bm{\xi}(b_{i}-\mu_{i}),\Delta_{ii}\Bigr)\Bigr\}d\bm{u}[-i]dg,

(3.173)

where
\bm{\xi}(x)
and
\Delta_{ii}
are defined in (
3.141
) and (
3.142
), respectively. Moreover, it is not hard to check that

\displaystyle\frac{g^{-1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\Bigl[\frac{\Sigma_{i,i}}{g}\Bigr]^{-\frac{1}{2}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g(a_{i}-\mu_{i})^{2}}{2\Sigma_{i,i}}\Bigr\}{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\nu_{2}}{\Sigma_{i,i}}}t\Bigl(a_{i}\Big|\mu_{i},\nu_{2}\Sigma_{i,i},\nu-2\Bigr){\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu-1}{2},\lambda_{a}\Bigr),

(3.174)

where
\nu_{2}=\nu/(\nu-2)
,
\lambda_{a}=(a_{i}-\mu_{i})^{2}/(2\Sigma_{i,i})+\nu/2
. Further, for given vectors
\bm{c}_{1}
and
\bm{c}_{2}
of length
p-1
such that
\bm{c}_{1}<\bm{c}_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\bm{c}_{1}}^{\bm{c}_{2}}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}}\Big|\frac{\Delta_{ii}}{g}\Big|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g}{2}\delta\bigl(\bm{y}-\bm{m},\Delta_{ii}\bigr)\Bigr\}d\bm{y}{\cal{G}}\Bigl(g\Big|\frac{\nu-1}{2},\lambda_{a}\Bigr)dg

\displaystyle=\int_{\bm{c}_{1}}^{\bm{c}_{2}}\frac{\lambda^{\frac{\nu-1}{2}}_{a}}{(2\pi)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\big|\Delta_{ii}\big|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu+p-2}{2}\bigr)}{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu-1}{2}\bigr)}\Bigl[\frac{\delta\bigl(\bm{y}-\bm{m},\Delta_{ii}\bigr)}{2}+\lambda_{a}\Bigr]^{-\frac{\nu+p-2}{2}}d\bm{y}

\displaystyle=\bm{T}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{c}_{2}\Big|\bm{m},\frac{2\lambda_{a}}{\nu-1}\Delta_{ii},\nu-1\Bigr)-\bm{T}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{c}_{1}\Big|\bm{m},\frac{2\lambda_{a}}{\nu-1}\Delta_{ii},\nu-1\Bigr),

(3.175)

where
\nu>1
.
Using the facts given in (
3.174
) and (
3.175
), the RHS of (
3.173
) becomes

\displaystyle I_{i}=\sqrt{\frac{\nu_{2}}{\Sigma_{i,i}}}\frac{\Sigma}{{\cal{P}}_{t}}

\displaystyle t\Bigl(a_{i}\Big|\mu_{i},\nu_{2}\Sigma_{i,i},\nu-2\Bigr)\biggl[\bm{T}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]\Big|\bm{\xi}(a_{i}-\mu_{i}),\frac{2\lambda_{a}}{\nu-1}\Delta_{ii},\nu-1\Bigr)

\displaystyle-\bm{T}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]\Big|\bm{\xi}(a_{i}-\mu_{i}),\frac{2\lambda_{a}}{\nu-1}\Delta_{ii},\nu-1\Bigr)\biggr]-

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\nu_{2}}{\Sigma_{i,i}}}\frac{\Sigma}{{\cal{P}}_{t}}

\displaystyle t\Bigl(b_{i}\Big|\mu_{i},\nu_{2}\Sigma_{i,i},\nu-2\Bigr)\biggl[\bm{T}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{b}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]\Big|\bm{\xi}(b_{i}-\mu_{i}),\frac{2\lambda_{b}}{\nu-1}\Delta_{ii},\nu-1\Bigr)

\displaystyle-\bm{T}_{p-1}\Bigl(\bm{a}[-i]-\bm{\mu}[-i]\Big|\bm{\xi}(b_{i}-\mu_{i}),\frac{2\lambda_{b}}{\nu-1}\Delta_{ii},\nu-1\Bigr)\biggr],

(3.176)

where
\nu>2
and
\lambda_{b}=(b_{i}-\mu_{i})^{2}/(2\Sigma_{i,i})+\nu/2
. Computing
I_{i}
in (
3.176
), for
i=1,\cdots,p
, the first moment of truncated Student’s
t
distribution is then obtained by substituting the constructed vector
\bm{I}
into the RHS of (
3.171
). In what follows, we perform a small simulation study for computing
\bm{\tau}
when
\bm{X}=(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})^{\top}
follows three-dimensional Student’s
t
distribution with parameters
\nu=4
,
\bm{\mu}=(1,1,1)^{\top}
and

\displaystyle\Sigma=\left[\begin{matrix}2&0.5&0.4\\
0.5&1.5&0.3\\
0.4&0.3&1\end{matrix}\right].

(3.177)

The truncation bounds are
\bm{a}=(0,0,0)^{\top}
and
\bm{b}=(10,10,10)^{\top}
. To this end, we compute
\bm{\Omega}
using the exact method described above and the importance sampling in which the instrumental distribution is
\bm{t}_{3}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\nu)
. For computing
\bm{\Omega}
through the importance sampling, we generate a sample of size
N
through the rejection method. Table
3.10
shows the results of simulation study for approximating
\bm{\Omega}
under both scenarios through the instrumental
t_{2}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\nu)
.

Chapter 4
Bayesian inference

A Bayesian prior is an
assumption of an infinite
amount of past relevant
experience. But you cannot
forget that you have just made
up a whole bunch of data
1
1

1

https://rss.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/pdf/10.1111/j.1740-9713.2010.00460.x

– Bradley Efron, Stanford University

In the framework of classical (or frequentist) statistics, the unknown parameter is assumed to be fixed and there is not any guess or belief about its true value. The only source to estimate the unknown parameter is the sample evidence (information). Contrary to this school of thinking, the Bayesian statistics permits to involve our
prior
knowledge or belief in process of estimating of unknown parameter. For this reason, the classical and Bayesian statistics are sometimes called
objective
and
subjective
2
2

2

The belief about unknown parameter differs from individual to another one, so it is
subjective
.
statistics, respectively. In other words, the subjectivity of belief about unknown parameter leads us to assume that unknown parameter is a random variable with a known distribution, called in the literature
prior
distribution. The Bayesian statistics would make a revision on the experimenter belief about the unknown parameter using the sample evidence. Let the PDF corresponds to the prior distribution of unknown parameter
\theta
is shown by
\pi(\theta)
. Furthermore suppose the revised version of
\pi(\theta)
given sample evidence
\bm{x}=\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\}
, is represented as
\pi(\theta|\bm{x})
that is known in the literature as the
posterior
PDF. The elementary Bayesian formula links the
prior
with
posterior
as

\displaystyle\pi(\theta|\bm{x})=\frac{f(\bm{x}|\theta)}{\int_{\theta}f(\bm{x}|\theta)\pi(\theta)d\theta}\pi(\theta)=\frac{f(\bm{x}|\theta)}{m(\bm{x})}\pi(\theta)\propto f(\bm{x}|\theta)\pi(\theta)=L(\theta|\bm{x})\pi(\theta),

(4.1)

where
L(\theta|\bm{x})=f(\bm{x}|\theta)=f(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i}|\theta)
is the likelihood function. Hence,

\displaystyle\pi(\theta|\bm{x})\propto\Pi_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i}|\theta)\pi(\theta),

(4.2)

Notice that joint PDF of sample evidence in (
4.2
), is represented as the product of marginal PDFs since the sample evidence
x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}
are assumed to be independent. Notice that in (
5.26
) the
prior
may be either
proper
or
improper
3
3

3

The
prior

\pi(\theta)
is called improper if
\int_{\theta}\pi(\theta)d\theta
diverges; otherwise
\pi(\theta)
is called
proper
.
. If the choosen
prior

\pi(\theta)
is
improper
, then the formal Bayes formula (
5.26
) is valid if we have
\pi(\theta|\bm{x})
is
proper
or equivalently
m(\bm{x})=\int_{\theta}f(\bm{x}|\theta)\pi(\theta)d\theta<\infty
. Furthermore, we note that the parameter space may be
d
-dimensional (multi-parameter case), represented as
\bm{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{d})^{\top}
.

4.1
Prior selection

Although the prior reflects experimenter’s belief or
prior
knowledge on
\theta
, but the term
subjectivity
does not mean that experimenter is free to choose any candidate for distribution of
prior
. In practice, there are a veriety of rules for this mean that makes the process of
prior
selection a quite
objective
4
4

4

The term
objective
means that experimenter has some plausible guideline for selecting the distribution of
prior
.
process that may need comprehensive study or knowledge about the unknown parameter. The structure of
prior
may also depend on some extra parameter(s) that is known in the literature as
hyperparameter
. In what follows, we cite some known classes of
objective prior
.

4.1.1
Uniform prior

The first class is the uniform prior
\pi_{U}(\theta)
, that is useful when the parameter space is compact interval and experimenter believes that all points in parameter space are equally likely outcomes. The famous example of this type may happen when one is interested in Bayesian inference for the success probability in a Bernoulli process for which we may have
\pi_{U}(\theta)\propto 1
. Considering a beta distribution for the law of
prior
,
then
\pi_{U}(\theta|a,b)
, for which
a
and
b
play the role of
hyperparameters
, is no longer uniform.

Doob (1949)

4.1.2
Reference prior

The first class of
priors
is known as the reference
prior

\pi_{R}(\theta)
,
proposed by
Bernardo (1979)
. For convenience let us to give some useful definitions as follows.

Definition 4.1.1
.

Let
F(x|\bm{\theta})
and
f(x|\bm{\theta})
denote, respectively, the CDF and PDF of the random variable
X
with unknown parameter
\theta\in\bm{\theta}
.

(i)

location family
: The family of random variable
X
is a location family if
{{F}}(x-\theta|\bm{\theta})
or
f(x-\theta|\bm{\theta})
for
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}
no longer depends on
\theta
. Statistically speaking, distribution of transformation
X-\theta
does not depend on
\theta
. Herein,
\theta
is known in the literature as the location parameter.

(ii)

scale family
: The family of random variable
X
is a scale family if
{{F}}(x/\theta|\bm{\theta})
or
1/\theta\times f(x/\theta|\bm{\theta})
for
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}^{+}}
no longer depends on
\theta
. Statistically speaking, distribution of transformation
X/\theta
does not depend on
\theta
. Herein,
\theta
is known in the literature as the scale parameter.

(iii)

sufficient statistic
Let
\bm{x}=\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\}
denote a sequence of observations of random variables
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{n}
that follow independently a family of distributions with PDF
f(x|\bm{\theta})
. A function of random variables
\bm{X}=\{X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{n}\}
such as
S(\bm{X})
is called sufficient statistic if
S(\bm{X})
provides information enough about unknown parameter
\theta\in\bm{\theta}
. Theoretically speaking, the conditional distribution of
\bm{X}
given
S(\bm{X})
does not depend on
\theta
.

The reference
prior
is obtained by maximizing the expected value of the Kullback-Leibler (or logarithmic) divergence between the
prior
and the posterior. The logarithmic divergence of PDF
\pi(\theta)
from PDF
\tilde{\pi}(\theta)
is represented as

\displaystyle{\cal{K}}(\pi||\tilde{\pi})=\int_{\theta}{\pi}(\theta)\log\frac{{\pi}(\theta)}{\tilde{\pi}(\theta)}d\theta,

(4.3)

provided that integral (
4.3
) is finite. The reference
prior
must justify two convincing rationales including
permissibility
and
expected logarithmically convergence

(
Berger et al., 2009
, Definition 5)
. There follows a listing of nice properties of the reference prior including:

(i)

Consistency under reparameterization of the unknown parameter
\theta

(ii)

Independence of sample size when observations are independent

(iii)

Compatibility with sufficient statistic

(iiii)

For a distribution for which nothing is known about unknown parameter
\theta
, the reference prior follows the uniform distribution

For a proof of properties noted above, we refer the reader to
Berger et al. (2009)
. The property (i) states, for a location family, that the reference
prior
for location parameter is flat (equally likely over
\mathbb{R}
) and within a scale family, the reference
prior
of the scale parameter
\theta
is proportional to
1/\theta
that is the same of Jeffreys
prior
, that is
\pi_{R}({\theta})=\pi_{J}({\theta})\propto 1/\theta

(
Bernardo, 2005
)
. Although the reference prior is an objective prior and inherits nice properties among them some noted above, but it suffers from some drawbacks as follows.

(i)

The reference prior must be computed point-by-point and then approximated through interpolation techniques.

(ii)

The burden of computations grows when sample size increases and it may be critical if
f(x|\theta)
lacks closed form such as
\alpha
-stable distribution.

(iii)

Although the conditions for existence of reference prior for multi-parameter case is guaranteed, but obviously demands for more computational complexity.

In general, a reference prior is obtained by following the steps of the algorithm given below
(
Berger et al., 2009
)
.

Algorithm 11

Computing the reference prior

1:

Choose a moderate integer value for
k
;

2:

Choose an arbitrary positive function
\pi^{*}(\theta)
such as
\pi^{*}(\theta)=1
;

3:

Consider the sequence
\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{m}
to be
m
arbitrary distinct points that
\theta
can take on;

4:

Set
j=1
;

5:

while

j\leq m

do

6:

Generate realizations
x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{k}
from PDF
f\bigl(\cdot\big|\theta_{j}\bigr)
;

7:

Compute the integral
c_{j}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}f\bigl(x_{i}\big|u\bigr)\pi^{*}\bigl(u\bigr)du
;

8:

Compute
r_{j}=\log\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{k}f\bigl(x_{j}\big|\theta_{j}\bigr)\pi^{*}\bigl(\theta_{j}\bigr)/c_{j}\Bigr)
;

9:

Compute
\pi\bigl(\theta_{j}\bigr)=\exp\bigl\{m^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{m}r_{j}\bigr\}
and store the pair
\bigl(\theta_{j},\pi(\theta_{j})\bigr)

10:

Set
j\leftarrow j+1
;

11:

end

12:

The set
\bigl\{\bigl(\theta_{j},\pi(\theta_{j})\bigr)\bigr\}_{j=1}^{m}
is the sequence of
m
paired from which
\pi(\theta)
can be obtained through interpolation.

4.1.3
Jeffreys prior

The second class of objective
prior
is known as the Jeffreys
prior

\pi_{J}(\bm{\theta})
that was proposed by
Jeffreys (1946)
. We have

\displaystyle\pi_{J}(\bm{\theta})\propto\sqrt{{\text{det}}I(\bm{\theta})},

(4.4)

where
{I}(\bm{\theta})=-E\bigl[\partial^{2}/\partial\theta_{i}\partial\theta_{j}\log L(\bm{\theta}|\bm{x})\bigr]
, for
i,j=1,\cdots,d
is the Fisher information matrix.

4.1.4
Conjugate prior

The last class of priors is the conjugate prior
\pi_{C}(\theta)
. If prior and posterior follow the same family of distributions, then the corresponding
prior
is called a conjugate
prior
. For example, let
x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}
independently follow
{\cal{N}}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
. Considering a
prior
with PDF
{\cal{N}}(\cdot|\mu_{0},\sigma_{0}^{2})
, it is easy to see that
posterior
of
\mu
is

\displaystyle\pi(\mu|\bm{x})\sim{\cal{N}}\biggl(\frac{\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\mu_{0}+\sigma_{0}^{2}\bar{x}}{\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}+\sigma_{0}^{2}},\Bigl(\frac{n}{\sigma^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{0}^{2}}\Bigr)^{-1}\biggr),

(4.5)

where
\bar{x}
is the sample mean and
\bm{\theta}_{0}=(\mu_{0},\sigma_{0})^{\top}
is vector of
hyperparameters
. As it is seen from (
4.5
), the
posterior
follows a Gaussian distribution too.
We note that the conjugate prior is an
subjective
prior since it is chosen by experimenter without rationale and just for the sake of simplicity in form and specially sampling from. The main advantage of the conjugate
prior
is that sampling from the corresponding posterior is an easy task.

If the PDF of family under study has not closed form, finding
\pi_{J}(\theta)
,
\pi_{R}(\theta)
, or even
\pi_{C}(\theta)
may be difficult. As we will see in the next section, uniform and conjugate priors will be proposed for
\alpha
and
\delta=\sigma^{2}
, respectively. These types were chosen by
(
Buckle, 1995
;
Lombardi, 2007
;
Godsill and Kuruoglu, 1999
)
for Bayesian estimation of tail thickness and scale parameters of
\alpha
-
stable
distribution.
However, some drawbacks of IG conjugate
prior
were addressed by
(
Gelman, 2006
;
Teimouri et al., 2024
)
.
Indeed, investigating advantages and disadvantages of above-noted
priors
in more details needs more space that is out of scope of this work. We refer the reader to
(
Jeffreys, 1946
;
Jaynes, 1968
;
Gelman et al., 1995
;
Berger et al., 2009
;
Box and Tiao, 2011
)
for further specific information.

4.2
Empirical Bayes

As noted earlier, the prior itself may depend on hyperparameter(s). Hence, sometimes we use the notation
\pi(\theta|\bm{\theta}_{0})
rather than
\pi(\theta)
to emphasize the fact that prior depends on hyperparameter
\bm{\theta}_{0}
. Evidently, for characterizing distribution of the unknown parameter
\theta
through the posterior in (
5.26
), it may be seen

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\theta\big|\bm{x},\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)=\frac{f(\bm{x}|\theta)}{m\bigl(\bm{x}|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)}\pi\bigl(\theta|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr),

(4.6)

where the unknown
hyperparameter

\bm{\theta}_{0}
plays the role of a
nuisance
parameter. The empirical Bayes is essentially an inferential approach, for estimating
\bm{\theta}_{0}
, that was introduced by
Robbins (1956)
. It takes two
non-parametric
and
parametric
types
(
Casella, 1985
)
. The empirical Bayes proposed by
Robbins (1956)
is a
non-parametric
approach under which experimenter has no any assumption about specification(s) of the prior’s distribution while within a
parametric
framework the family of prior is fully specified by experimenter
(
Morris, 1983
;
Casella, 1985
)
. More precisely, the
parametric
empirical Bayes takes into account information provided by sample evidence
\bm{x}
for estimating
hyperparameter

\bm{\theta}_{0}
. Herein, we assume that
\bm{\theta}_{0}
is fixed, but unknown. If
\bm{\theta}_{0}
itself follows some distribution, then the procedure of estimating
\theta
within Bayesian framework is called
hierarchical Bayesian inference
. For a single observation
x_{i}
, we recall from (
4.6
) that

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\theta\big|{x}_{i},\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)=\frac{f({x}_{i}|\theta)}{m\bigl({x}_{i}|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)}\pi\bigl(\theta|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr).

(4.7)

If
\pi\bigl(\theta\big|{x}_{i},\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)
is proper, then
m\bigl({x}_{i}|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)
in denominator of (
4.7
) itself is a PDF since
0<m\bigl({x}_{i}|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)<\infty
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}m\bigl({x}_{i}|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)d\bm{x}=1
. In fact,
m\bigl({x}_{i}|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)
plays the role of
marginal
PDF (likelihood function) when
\bm{\theta}
is integrated out. The unknown hyperparameter
\bm{\theta}_{0}
is then estimated through the maximum likelihood (ML) by maximizing
\prod_{i=1}^{n}m\bigl({x}_{i}|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)
with respect to
\bm{\theta}_{0}
or using the method of moments
(
Teimouri et al., 2024
)
. In what follows, we proceed to compute the
parametric
empirical Bayes through an example.

4-27

Let
x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}
independently follow
{\cal{N}}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
in which scale parameter
\sigma>0
is assumed to be known. Furthermore, a Gaussian prior
\pi(\mu)={\cal{N}}\bigl(\mu\big|\mu_{0},\sigma_{0}^{2}\bigr)
, is assumed for
\mu
. For estimating
\bm{\theta}_{0}=(\mu_{0},\sigma_{0})^{\top}
, we can write

\displaystyle m\bigl({x}_{i}|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f\bigl({x}_{i}\big|\mu\bigr)\pi\bigl(\mu\big|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)d\mu=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f\bigl(x_{i}\big|\mu\bigr)\pi\bigl(\mu\big|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)d\mu,

where
f\bigl(x_{i}\big|\mu\bigr)={\cal{N}}\big(x_{i}\big|\mu,\sigma^{2}\big)
. We have

\displaystyle m\bigl({x}_{i}|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\bigl(x_{i}-\mu\bigr)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr\}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma_{0}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\bigl(\mu-\mu_{0}\bigr)^{2}}{2\sigma_{0}^{2}}\Bigr\}d\mu.

Simplifying and some algebra leads to

\displaystyle m\bigl({x}_{i}|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)={\cal{N}}\big(x_{i}\big|\mu_{0},\sigma^{2}+\sigma_{0}^{2}\big).

It is well known that the ML estimator of mean and variance of each Gaussian distribution are the sample counterparts. So, the empirical Bayesian estimator of
\mu_{0}
and
\sigma_{0}
are
\widehat{\mu_{0}}_{EB}=\bar{x}
and
\widehat{\sigma_{0}}_{EB}=\sqrt{|S^{2}-\sigma^{2}|}
where
\bar{x}
and
S^{2}
are the sample mean and variance, respectively. It is noteworthy that the quantity
S^{2}-\sigma^{2}
may be negative especially when sample size is small. Hence, the absolute sign is used to avoid negative value for
\widehat{\sigma_{0}}_{EB}
.

It is immediate from Example
4-27
above that the Bayesian estimator of
\mu
, that is
\hat{\mu}_{B}
, becomes the average of the posterior (
4.5
). Hence, substituting
\widehat{\mu_{0}}_{EB}
and
\widehat{\sigma_{0}}_{EB}
obtained above in RHS of (
4.5
) yields
\hat{\mu}_{B}=\bar{x}
with standard error

\displaystyle SE\bigl(\hat{\mu}_{B}\bigr)=\Bigl(\frac{n}{\sigma^{2}}+\frac{1}{|S^{2}-\sigma^{2}|}\Bigr)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Evidently,
\hat{\mu}_{B}
is more efficient than the ML counterpart
\hat{\mu}_{ML}=\bar{x}
since

\displaystyle SE\bigl(\hat{\mu}_{B}\bigr)<SE\bigl(\hat{\mu}_{ML}\bigr)=\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}.

A different version of Example
4-27
in which
x_{i}
s independently, for
i=1,\cdots,n
, follow
{\cal{N}}(\mu_{i},\sigma^{2})
is discussed by
Casella (1985)
.

4-28

Suppose
x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}
follow independently a Bernoulli distribution with success probability
y
, that is
X_{i}\sim{\cal{BER}}(y)
for
i=1,\cdots,n
and
0<y<1
. For computing the Bayes estimation of
y
, that is
\hat{y}_{B}
, we may consider a Beta distribution with PDF
\pi\bigl(y\big|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)=1/{\cal{B}}(a,b)y^{a-1}(1-y)^{b-1}
for the prior of
y
where
\bm{\theta}_{0}=(a,b)^{\top}
is the vector of hyperparameters. We can write

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(y\big|\bm{x},\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle=y^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}}(1-y)^{n-\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}}\pi(y|a,b)

\displaystyle\propto y^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}+a-1}(1-y)^{n-\sum_{i=1}x_{i}+b-1},

(4.8)

where
\bm{x}=\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\}
. Obviously,
y\big|\bm{x}
follows a beta distribution. So, we can consider the mean of posterior, that is
(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}+a-1)/(n+a+b-2)
, as the Bayes estimator for the success probability
y
. Recalling from Subsection
4.2
, for computing the vector of hyperparameters
\bm{\theta}_{0}=(a,b)^{\top}
, we can write

\displaystyle m\bigl({x}_{i}|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)=\int_{0}^{1}f\bigl({x}_{i}\big|y\bigr)\pi\bigl(y\big|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{y^{x_{i}+a-1}(1-y)^{b-x_{i}}}{{\cal{B}}(a,b)}dy

\displaystyle=\frac{{\cal{B}}(x_{i}+a,b-x_{i}+1)}{{\cal{B}}(a,b)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(x_{i}+a)\Gamma(b-x_{i}+1)}{(a+b)\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)}.

Hence the likelihood function becomes

\displaystyle m\bigl(\bm{x}|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)=\frac{\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\Gamma(x_{i}+a)\Gamma(b-x_{i}+1)}{(a+b)^{n}[\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)]^{n}}.

(4.9)

Differentiating the RHS of (
4.9
) with respect to
\bm{\theta}_{0}
and some algebra leads to the empirical Bayes estimator of
\bm{\theta}_{0}
as
\widehat{\bm{\theta}_{0}}_{EB}=\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}/n,(n-\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i})/n\bigr)^{\top}
.

If in Example
4-28
, the Bayesian estimator
\hat{y}_{B}
, of success probability
y
is desired, one could assume
\hat{y}_{B}
is equal to the expected value of posterior as usual. To obtain
\hat{y}_{B}
, we recall from (
4.8
) that

\displaystyle\pi(y\big|\bm{x})=\frac{y^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}+a-1}(1-y)^{n-\sum_{i=1}x_{i}+b-1}}{{\cal{B}}\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}+a-1,n-\sum_{i=1}x_{i}+b-1\bigr)}.

(4.10)

It turns out that
y\big|\bm{x}
follows a Beta distribution with parameters
\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}+a
and
n-\sum_{i=1}x_{i}+b
. Hence,

\displaystyle\hat{y}_{B}=E\bigl(y\big|\bm{x}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}y\pi(y\big|\bm{x})dy=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}+a}{n+a+b}.

(4.11)

Substituting
\bm{\theta}_{0}
computed above into the RHS of (
4.11
) yields

\displaystyle\hat{y}_{B}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}+\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}}{n}}{n+1}.

As it is see, the Bayesian estimator
\hat{y}_{B}
is asymptotically (that is,
n\rightarrow\infty
) equal to the maximum likelihood (ML) estimator
\hat{y}_{ML}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}/n
of success probability
y
.

4-29

Let
x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}
come independently from
{\cal{G}}(\alpha,\beta)
with unknown
\alpha
and known
\beta
. For computing the Bayesian estimator of
\alpha
, we suppose that
\pi\bigl(\alpha\big|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)={\cal{G}}\bigl(\alpha\big|\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}\bigr)
.
But, first we need to determine hyperparameter
\bm{\theta}_{0}=(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})^{\top}
. To this end, we proceed through the empirical Bayes as follows.

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\alpha\big|\bm{x},\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)=\frac{f(\bm{x}|\alpha)\pi\bigl(\alpha\big|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)}{m\bigl(\bm{x}|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)},

where

\displaystyle f(\bm{x}|\alpha)\pi\bigl(\alpha\big|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)=

\displaystyle\Bigl[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\beta^{\alpha}x_{i}^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}\exp\bigl\{-\beta x_{i}\bigr\}\Bigr]\frac{\beta_{0}^{\alpha_{0}}\alpha^{\alpha_{0}-1}}{\Gamma\bigl(\alpha_{0}\bigr)}\exp\bigl\{-\beta_{0}\alpha\bigr\},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta_{0}^{\alpha_{0}}\beta^{n\alpha}\alpha^{\alpha_{0}-1}}{\Gamma\bigl(\alpha_{0}\bigr)\bigl[\Gamma(\alpha)\bigr]^{n}}\Bigl[\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\Bigr]^{\alpha-1}\exp\bigl\{-\beta\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}-\beta_{0}\alpha\bigr\},

and

\displaystyle m\bigl({x}_{i}|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}f\bigl({x}_{i}\big|\alpha\bigr)\pi\bigl(\alpha\big|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)d\alpha

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\beta^{\alpha}\frac{x_{i}^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}\exp\bigl\{-\beta x_{i}\bigr\}\beta_{0}^{\alpha_{0}}\frac{\alpha^{\alpha_{0}-1}}{\Gamma\bigl(\alpha_{0}\bigr)}\exp\bigl\{-\beta_{0}\alpha\bigr\}d\alpha.

(4.12)

Obviously, the integral in the RHS of (
4.1
) has no closed form and so computing
\widehat{\bm{\theta}_{0}}_{EB}
using the ML approach is computationally cumbersome. Herein, we proceed to find the moment-based estimator of
{\bm{\theta}_{0}}
. First note that

\displaystyle E\bigl(X^{s}\big|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{s}\int_{0}^{\infty}f\bigl(x\big|\alpha\bigr)\pi\bigl(\alpha\big|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)d\alpha dx

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{s}\int_{0}^{\infty}\beta^{\alpha}\frac{x^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}\exp\bigl\{-\beta x\bigr\}\beta_{0}^{\alpha_{0}}\frac{\alpha^{\alpha_{0}-1}}{\Gamma\bigl(\alpha_{0}\bigr)}\exp\bigl\{-\beta_{0}\alpha\bigr\}d\alpha dx

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\beta^{\alpha}\beta_{0}^{\alpha_{0}}\alpha^{\alpha_{0}-1}\exp\bigl\{-\beta_{0}\alpha\bigr\}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma\bigl(\alpha_{0}\bigr)}\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{s+\alpha-1}\exp\bigl\{-\beta x\bigr\}dxd\alpha

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\beta^{\alpha}\beta_{0}^{\alpha_{0}}\alpha^{\alpha_{0}-1}\Gamma(s+\alpha)\exp\bigl\{-\beta_{0}\alpha\bigr\}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma\bigl(\alpha_{0}\bigr)\beta^{s+\alpha}}d\alpha,

(4.13)

where
s\in\mathbb{N}
. It is easy to check that

\displaystyle E\bigl(X\big|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\beta_{0}\beta},

(4.14)

and

\displaystyle E\bigl(X^{2}\big|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\beta_{0}\beta^{2}}+\frac{\alpha_{0}\bigl(\alpha_{0}+1\bigr)}{\beta^{2}_{0}\beta^{2}}.

(4.15)

Using (
4.14
) and (
4.15
), and some simplifications yields

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}=\frac{\beta\bigl[E\bigl(X\big|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)\bigr]^{2}}{\beta\text{var}\bigl(X\big|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)-E\bigl(X\big|\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)}.

(4.16)

So, based on sample evidence
\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\}
, the moment-based estimator
\widehat{\alpha_{0}}_{EB}
is given by

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha_{0}}_{EB}=\frac{\beta\bar{x}^{2}}{\big|\beta S^{2}-\bar{x}\big|}.

(4.17)

Consequently, the moment-based estimator
\widehat{\beta_{0}}_{EB}
is then given by

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta_{0}}_{EB}=\frac{\widehat{\alpha_{0}}_{EB}}{\beta\bar{x}}.

(4.18)

As it is seen, theoretically, denominator in the RHS of (
4.16
) is zero. This means that
\widehat{\alpha_{0}}_{EB}
must be large. Moreover, the absolute sign is used in denominator of (
4.17
) to avoid negative value for
\widehat{\alpha_{0}}_{EB}
.

Figure 4.1:
Schematic diagram for PDF of underlying, prior, and posterior when (a):
n=50
and (b):
n=100
.

Figure
4.1
shows the visual comparison between prior and posterior distributions when sample evidence are generated from
{\cal{G}}(\alpha=2,\beta=2)
as underlying distribution for two data sets of sizes
n=50
and
n=100
.

4.3
Credible interval

One common type of inference in frequentist statistics is
confidence interval
. The Bayesian analogue of confidence interval is known as
credible interval
and defined as follows.

Definition 4.3.1
.

Given the observed sample evidence
\bm{x}=\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\}
, the interval
(L,U)
is a
100(1-\gamma)\%
credible interval for unknown parameter
\theta
if

\displaystyle P(L<\theta<U|\bm{x})=\int_{L}^{U}\pi(\theta|\bm{x})d\theta\geq 1-\gamma,

(4.19)

where
0<\gamma<1
.

Indeed, Definition
4.2
states that there is at least
100(1-\gamma)\%
confidence that interval
(L,U)
includes unknown parameter
\theta
. Figure
4.2
provides a schematic diagram for credible interval.

Figure 4.2:
Schematic diagram for credible interval.

4-30

Let
x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}
come independently from
{\cal{N}}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
with known variance. By considering a conjugate prior
{\cal{N}}(\cdot|\mu_{0},\sigma_{0}^{2})
for
\mu
, we recall from (
4.5
) that
\mu|\bm{x}
follows a Gaussian distribution. Hence

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{n}{\sigma^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{0}^{2}}}\biggl(\mu-\frac{n^{-1}\sigma^{2}\mu_{0}+\sigma_{0}^{2}\bar{x}}{n^{-1}\sigma^{2}+\sigma_{0}^{2}}\biggr)\sim{\cal{N}}(0,1).

(4.20)

Therefore,
100(1-\gamma)\%
credible interval for
\mu
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{n^{-1}\sigma^{2}\mu_{0}+\sigma_{0}^{2}\bar{x}}{n^{-1}\sigma^{2}+\sigma_{0}^{2}}\pm z_{\gamma/2}\sqrt{\frac{n}{\sigma^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{0}^{2}}},

(4.21)

where
z_{\gamma/2}
is the upper
\gamma/2
-quantile of the standard Gaussian distribution, that is,
P\bigl(Z>z_{\gamma/2}\bigr)=\gamma/2
in which
Z\sim{\cal{N}}(0,1)
.

As it is seen from Example
4-30
, for constructing credible interval, the upper and lower
\gamma/2
of the posterior must be known. If the posterior gets complicated from the latter quantities cannot be found easily. In such cases, credible interval is constructed based on upper
\gamma/2
-quantile of the samples generated from the posterior. Let
\pi_{\gamma/2}
denote the posterior’s upper
\gamma/2
-quantile, that is

\displaystyle\int_{\pi_{\gamma/2}}^{\infty}\pi(\theta|\bm{x})d\theta=\frac{\gamma}{2}.

(4.22)

Furthermore, suppose
\lceil{u}\rceil
denotes the smallest integer value equal or greater than real value
u
and
\theta_{(r)}
is
r
th order statistic of sample
\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\cdots,\theta_{N}\}
generated from posterior with PDF
\pi(\theta|\bm{x})
. When
N\rightarrow\infty
, then strong law of large numbers states that
\pi_{\gamma/2}\approx\theta_{(v)}
where
v=\lceil{(1-\gamma/2)N}\rceil
. The definition below gives the empirical credible interval.

Definition 4.3.2
.

Let
\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\cdots,\theta_{N}\}
, for large
N
, denote generated samples form posterior with PDF
\pi(\theta|\bm{x})
. A
100(1-\gamma)\%
empirical credible interval for unknown parameter
\theta
is then give by

\displaystyle\bigl(L_{e},U_{e}\bigr)=\bigl(\theta_{(v)},\theta_{(N-v)}\bigr),

(4.23)

where
v=\lceil{(1-\gamma/2)N}\rceil
.

4-31
continued

Recalling from Example
4-29
, suppose we observed
n=200
observations, represented as
\bm{x}=\bigl\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{200}\bigr\}
from
{\cal{G}}(\alpha=2,\beta=2)
. For known
\beta
, using the moment-based method described earlier in Example
4-29
, we obtain
\bm{\theta}_{0}=\bigl(\widehat{\alpha_{0}}_{EB},\widehat{\beta_{0}}_{EB}\bigr)^{\top}=(62.179,30.083)^{\top}
. The corresponding posterior is given by

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\alpha\big|\bm{x},\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)\propto

\displaystyle\Bigl[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\beta^{\alpha}x_{i}^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}\exp\bigl\{-\beta x_{i}\bigr\}\Bigr]\frac{\beta_{0}^{\alpha_{0}}\alpha^{\alpha_{0}-1}}{\Gamma\bigl(\alpha_{0}\bigr)}\exp\bigl\{-\beta_{0}\alpha\bigr\},

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle\frac{\beta^{n\alpha}\alpha^{\alpha_{0}-1}}{\bigl[\Gamma(\alpha)\bigr]^{n}}\Bigl[\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\Bigr]^{\alpha-1}\exp\bigl\{-\beta_{0}\alpha\bigr\}.

(4.24)

In order to construct a 95% empirical credible interval for
\alpha
, we need to sample from posterior (
4.24
) using methods such as MH or ARS. Taking into account into the fact that
\widehat{\alpha_{0}}_{EB}=62.179
and it is simple to see that the posterior (
4.24
) is log-concave for
\alpha_{0}\geq 1
. So, we proceed for simulating from this posterior using the ARS approach. Based on a sample of size 5000 generated from posterior (
4.24
), we obtain quantities
L_{e}=1.778
,
\hat{\alpha}_{B}=1.945
, and
U_{e}=2.121
. The scatterplot of generated samples as well as the latter quantities are shown in Figure
4.3
.

Figure 4.3:
(a): the 95% empirical credible interval for parameter
\alpha
based on a sample of
n=200
realizations generated from
{\cal{G}}(\alpha=2,\beta=2)
distribution and (b): histogram of the generated samples with fitted posterior
\pi\bigl(\alpha\big|\bm{x},\bm{\theta}_{0}\bigr)
.

\alpha
is given below.

Chapter 5
Gibbs sampling

“Statistics is the science of information gathering, especially when the information arrives in little pieces instead of big ones.”

– Bradley Efron, Stanford University

This section has three parts. First, we describe the concept of Gibbs sampling as a general framework for generating independent samples from the given multivariate target distribution. Second, we discuss the situation that the Gibbs sampling is utilized to estimate the model’s unknown parameter(s) through the Bayesian paradigm.

5.1
Gibbs sampling in broad sense

The Gibbs sampling (or sampler) is an iterative Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) sampling approach
was developed by
Geman and Geman (1984)
for image reconstruction, but its application in statistical analyses were shown by
Gelfand and Smith (1990)
. Assuming that we are at
t
th iteration of the Gibbs sampling and
\pi(\bm{x})
denote the PDF of target distribution from which generating sample
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{p})^{p}
is of interest. For convenience, there follows a set of generic symbols as

\displaystyle\bm{x}^{(t)}=\bigl({x}_{1}^{(t)},{x}_{2}^{(t)},\cdots,{x}_{p}^{(t)}\bigr),

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{i}^{(t)}[y]=\bigl({x}_{1}^{(t)},\cdots,{x}_{i-1}^{(t)},y,{x}_{i+1}^{(t)},\cdots,{x}_{p}^{(t)}\bigr),

\displaystyle\bm{x}^{(t)}[-i]=\bigl({x}_{1}^{(t)},\cdots,{x}_{i-1}^{(t)},{x}_{i+1}^{(t)},\cdots,{x}_{p}^{(t)}\bigr).

(5.1)

It is noteworthy that sampling from a
p
-dimensional distribution using the methods such as MH or AMH discussed earlier may involve undesired computational burden. In such cases, the Gibbs sampling technique that works efficiently is becoming increasingly popular in a wide range of applications. Indeed, each Gibbs sampling scheme works by generating sample from conditional distribution of vector
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{p})^{p}
that is known in the literature as the
full conditional
. For example, for generating
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{p})^{p}
form target PDF
\pi(\bm{x})
, the conditional distribution (or PDF)
\pi\bigl(x_{i}\big|\bm{x}[-i]\bigr)
is called
full conditional
of
x_{i}
(for
i=1,\cdots,p
). It is proved
(
Geman and Geman, 1984
)
that, after
burn-in
period
(
Roberts and Smith, 1994
)
, the generated sample comes from the target distribution whose PDF is
\pi(\bm{x})
. Each Gibbs sampling technique is summarized by the following algorithm.

Algorithm 12

Gibbs sampling Technique

1:

Set
t=1
, read
M
(
burn-in
period),
N
(total number of iterations), and

2:

initiate algorithm with randomly selected sample
\bm{x}^{(0)}
;

3:

while

t\leq N

do

4:

while

i\leq p

do

5:

Generate sample
y
from full conditional with PDF
\pi\bigl(x^{(t)}_{i}\big|\bm{x}^{(t-1)}[-i]\bigr)
;

6:

Set
\bm{x}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\bm{x}_{i}^{(t)}[y]
(that is

7:

{x}_{i}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow y
and
\bm{x}^{(t+1)}[-i]\leftarrow\bm{x}^{(t)}[-i]
);

8:

Set
i\leftarrow i+1
;

9:

end

10:

Set
t\leftarrow t+1
;

11:

end

12:

Sequence
\bigl\{\bm{x}_{M},\bm{x}_{M+1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{N}\bigr\}
is a sample of size
N-M+1
from

13:

target distribution with PDF
\pi\bigl(\bm{x})
.

5-32

Let
\bm{X}=\bigl(X_{1},X_{2}\bigr)^{\top}
follows a bivariate Gaussian distribution with PDF

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\bm{x}\big|\rho\bigr)=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}-2\rho x_{1}x_{2}}{2(1-\rho^{2})}\Bigr\}.

(5.2)

where
\bm{x}=\bigl(x_{1},x_{2}\bigr)^{\top}
and
-1<\rho<1
. Since we have just two variables
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, so for simulating from
\bm{X}
using the Gibbs sampling technique, we need to simulate from full conditionals
X_{1}\big|\bigl(X_{2},\rho\bigr)
and
X_{2}\big|\bigl(X_{1},\rho\bigr)
. It can be easily seen that

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(x_{1}\big|x_{2},\rho\bigr)\propto\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{x_{1}^{2}-2\rho x_{1}x_{2}}{2(1-\rho^{2})}\Bigr\}.

(5.3)

Since
x_{1}^{2}-2\rho x_{1}x_{2}=(x_{1}-\rho x_{2})^{2}-(\rho x_{2})^{2}
, then it follows that

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(x_{1}\big|x_{2},\rho\bigr)\propto\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{(x_{1}-\rho x_{2})^{2}}{2(1-\rho^{2})}\Bigr\}.

(5.4)

This means that

X_{1}\big|\bigl(X_{2},\rho\bigr)\sim{\cal{N}}(\rho x_{2},1-\rho^{2})
. Likewise, we can write
X_{2}\big|\bigl(X_{1},\rho\bigr)\sim{\cal{N}}(\rho x_{1},1-\rho^{2})
. Hence, given
\bm{x}^{(0)}=\bigl(x^{(0)}_{1},x^{(0)}_{2}\bigr)^{\top}
, for simulating a sample of
n=3000
observations when
M=2000
, the steps of the Gibbs sampling given in Algorithm (
12
) are as follows.

1.

Set
t=1
, and generate
\bm{x}^{(0)}=\bigl(x^{(0)}_{1},x^{(0)}_{2}\bigr)^{\top}
in which
x^{(0)}_{1}\sim{\cal{N}}\bigl(0,1-\rho^{2}\bigr)
and
x^{(0)}_{2}\sim{\cal{N}}\bigl(0,1-\rho^{2}\bigr)
;

2.

Set
t=0
;

3.

Simulate
x^{(t+1)}_{1}
from the full conditional
x^{(t+1)}_{1}\big|\bigl(x^{(t)}_{2},\rho\bigr)\sim{\cal{N}}\bigl(\rho x^{(t)}_{2},1-\rho^{2}\bigr)
;

4.

Having
x^{(t+1)}_{1}
, simulate
x^{(t+1)}_{2}
from the full conditional
x^{(t+1)}_{2}\big|\bigl(x^{(t)}_{1},\rho\bigr)\sim{\cal{N}}\bigl(\rho x^{(t+1)}_{1},1-\rho^{2}\bigr)
;

5.

If
t=5000
, then go to the next step, otherwise
t\leftarrow t+1
and return step 3;

6.

Sequence
\bigl\{\bm{x}_{2001},\bm{x}_{2002},\cdots,\bm{x}_{5000}\bigr\}
constitutes a sample of size 3000 from target distribution with PDF
\pi\bigl(\bm{x}\big|\rho\bigr)
.

The associated
R
code for generating 3000 realizations from
{\cal{N}}_{2}(\mu_{1}=0,\mu_{2}=0,\sigma_{1}^{2}=1,\sigma_{2}^{2}=1,\rho=0.50)
is given as follows.
