.

5.2
Gibbs sampling in Bayesian paradigm

Herein, we show how a Gibbs sampling technique described in the previous section can be used for computing the Bayesian estimator of the unknown parameter
\bm{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{p})^{\top}
. Notice that, based on random sample
\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\}
following a distribution with PDF
f(\cdot|\bm{\theta})
, the Bayes estimator of
\bm{\theta}
usually becomes the mean
1
1

1

Statistically speaking, the form of Bayesian estimator depends on the type of the loss function. For example, if the loss function takes either
square
or
absolute
form, then the Bayesian estimator is
mean
or
median
of posterior, respectively. But, the most commonly used summary representing the central tendency such as mean, median, or mode of the posterior would be considered as the Bayesian estimator.
of the posterior defined as

\displaystyle E\bigl[\bm{\theta}\big|\bm{x}\bigr]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}\bm{\theta}\pi(\bm{\theta}|\bm{x})d\bm{\theta}.

(5.5)

Computing integration (
5.5
) may involve huge computational burden especially when number of parameters
p
, is large. The law of large numbers guarantees that the sample mean converges with probability one to the population mean when sample size is sufficiently large, that is

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\theta}}=\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\operatorname{limit}}\penalty\ \frac{\bm{\theta}^{(1)}+\cdots+\bm{\theta}^{(N)}}{N}=E\bigl[\bm{\theta}\big|\bm{x}\bigr].

Hence, if we could generate sample enough from the posterior
\pi(\bm{\theta}|\bm{x})
, then
\bm{\theta}
can be estimated via
\hat{\bm{\theta}}
in which
\{\bm{\theta}^{(1)},\cdots,\bm{\theta}^{(N)}\}
is generated from posterior
\pi(\bm{\theta}|\bm{x})
when
N
is sufficiently large (here
\bm{\theta}^{(i)}
denotes the
i
th sample generated from posterior
\pi(\bm{\theta}|\bm{x})
). Generating sample from the target distribution (that is here
\pi(\bm{\theta}|\bm{x})
) using the MCMC techniques described in the previous section appears to be challenging when
p
is large. In such cases, the Gibbs sampling is guaranteed to work well.

It should be noted that since in each Bayesian framework the unknown parameter
\bm{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{p})^{\top}
itself is a random variable, in addition of the model’s variables, herein we must consider an extra number of
p
additional full conditionals within a Gibbs sampler schemes. For instance, recalling from the Example (3), we must consider three full conditionals given by variables
X_{1}\big|X_{2},\rho
,
X_{2}\big|X_{1},\rho
, and
\rho\big|X_{1},X_{2}
. In what follows, we consider the foregoing example when Bayesian inference on
\rho
is desired.

5-33

Let
{\underline{\bm{x}}}=\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\cdots,\bm{x}_{n}
} account for a sequence of
n
independent observations in which
\bm{x}_{i}=\bigl(x_{i1},x_{i2}\bigr)^{\top}
. Furthermore, assume
\bm{x}_{i}
(for
i=1,2,\cdots,n
) follows a bivariate Gaussian distribution with PDF given by

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\bm{x}_{i}\big|\bm{\theta}\bigr)=\frac{1}{2\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{2(1-\rho^{2})}\Bigl[\Bigl(\frac{x_{i1}}{\sigma_{1}}\Bigr)^{2}+\Bigl(\frac{x_{i2}}{\sigma_{2}}\Bigr)^{2}-2\rho\frac{x_{i1}x_{i2}}{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}\Bigr]\Bigr\},

(5.6)

where
\bm{\theta}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\rho)^{\top}
is the unknown parameter vector. Using a reparameterization
\bm{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3})^{\top}
in which
\theta_{1}=\sigma_{1}/\sigma_{2}
,
\theta_{2}=\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}
, and
\theta_{3}=\rho
, the bivariate Gaussian PDF is rewritten as
by

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\bm{x}_{i}\big|\bm{\theta}\bigr)=\frac{1}{2\pi\theta_{2}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{2(1-\theta_{3}^{2})^{1/2}\theta_{2}}\Bigl[\frac{x^{2}_{i1}}{\theta_{1}}+\theta_{1}x^{2}_{i2}-2\theta_{3}x_{i1}x_{i2}\Bigr]\Bigr\}.

(5.7)

For computing the Bayesian estimator of
\bm{\theta}
, we first note that

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\rho\big|{\underline{\bm{x}}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\propto\prod_{i=1}^{n}\pi\bigl(\bm{x}_{i}\big|\bm{\theta})\pi(\bm{\theta}\bigr).

(5.8)

The marginals of joint prior
\pi(\bm{\theta})
are assumed to be independent, that is,
\pi(\bm{\theta})=\pi(\theta_{1})\pi(\theta_{2})\pi(\theta_{3})
. Also, we consider gamma prior for both of
\theta_{1}
, inverse gamma prior for
\theta_{2}
, and an uniform on (-1,1) prior for
\theta
represented, respectively, as follows.

\displaystyle\pi(\theta_{1}\big|a_{1},b_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{b_{1}^{a_{1}}\theta_{1}^{a_{1}-1}}{\Gamma(a_{1})}\exp\bigl\{-b_{1}\theta_{1}\bigr\},\penalty\ \penalty\ \theta_{1}>0,

\displaystyle\pi(\theta_{2}\big|a_{2},b_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{b_{2}^{a_{2}}\theta_{2}^{-a_{2}-1}}{\Gamma(a_{2})}\exp\bigl\{-\frac{b_{2}}{\theta_{2}}\bigr\},\penalty\ \penalty\ \theta_{2}>0,

\displaystyle\pi(\theta_{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2},\penalty\ \penalty\ -1<\theta_{3}<1.

(5.9)

We note that, here we consider above priors just for the simplicity of computations. It follows that

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\bm{\theta}\big|{\underline{\bm{x}}}\bigr)\propto

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\theta_{2}^{n}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{2\theta_{2}\sqrt{1-\theta_{3}^{2}}}\Bigl[\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}^{2}}{\theta_{1}}+\theta_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i2}^{2}-2\theta_{3}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}x_{i2}{}\Bigr]\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\times\frac{b_{1}^{a_{1}}\theta_{1}^{a_{1}-1}}{\Gamma(a_{1})}\exp\bigl\{-b_{1}\theta_{1}\bigr\}\times\frac{b_{2}^{a_{2}}\theta_{2}^{-a_{2}-1}}{\Gamma(a_{2})}\exp\bigl\{-\frac{b_{2}}{\theta_{2}}\bigr\}\times\frac{1}{2}

(5.10)

We note that, herein, the model’s variables are
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}
, and

\theta_{3}
. So, by dropping out all terms that do not depend on
\theta_{1}
from the posterior (
5.10
), then the full conditional
{\theta}_{1}\big|(\theta_{2},\theta_{3},{\underline{\bm{x}}})
, after some algebra, is shown to be
2
2

2

It is noteworthy that
{\underline{\bm{x}}}
has been observed and we no longer look at it as variable. So, the model’s variables are
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}
, and

\theta_{3}
.

\displaystyle\pi\bigl({\theta}_{1}\big|\theta_{2},\theta_{3},{\underline{\bm{x}}}\bigr)\propto

\displaystyle\theta_{1}^{-a_{1}-1}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\theta_{1}}{2}\Bigl[2b_{1}+\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i2}^{2}}{\theta_{2}(1-\theta_{3})^{1/2}}\Bigr]-\frac{1}{2\theta_{1}}\Bigl[\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}^{2}}{\theta_{2}(1-\theta_{3})^{1/2}}\Bigr]\Bigr\}.

(5.11)

Comparing the RHSs of (
5.11
) and (
2.54
), it turns out that (
5.11
) is the pseudo PDF corresponds to a
{\cal{GIG}}(a,b,c)
distribution where
a=a_{1}
,
b=2b_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i2}^{2}/\bigl[\theta_{2}(1-\theta_{3})^{1/2}\bigr]
,
c=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}^{2}/\bigl[\theta_{2}(1-\theta_{3})^{1/2}\bigr]
. So,

\displaystyle{\theta}_{1}\big|(\theta_{2},\theta_{3},{\underline{\bm{x}}})\sim{\cal{GIG}}\Bigl(a_{1},2b_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i2}^{2}/\bigl[\theta_{2}(1-\theta_{3})^{1/2}\bigr],\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}^{2}/\bigl[\theta_{2}(1-\theta_{3})^{1/2}\bigr]\Bigr).

(5.12)

Likewise, we can see that the full conditional
{\theta}_{2}\big|(\theta_{1},\theta_{3},{\underline{\bm{x}}})
follows an inverse gamma distribution. We have

\displaystyle{\theta}_{2}\big|(\theta_{1},\theta_{3},{\underline{\bm{x}}})\sim

\displaystyle{\cal{IG}}\biggl(n+a_{2},b_{2}+\frac{1}{2\theta_{2}(1-\theta_{3})^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\times\Bigl[\theta^{-1}_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}^{2}+\theta_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i2}^{2}-2\theta_{3}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}x_{i2}\Bigr]\biggr),

(5.13)

and finally

\displaystyle{\theta}_{3}\big|(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},{\underline{\bm{x}}})\propto\exp\biggl\{-\frac{1}{2\theta_{2}\sqrt{1-\theta_{3}^{2}}}\Bigl[\theta_{1}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}^{2}+\theta_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i2}^{2}-2\theta_{3}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}x_{i2}\Bigr]\biggr\}.

(5.14)

As it is seen, the full conditional
{\theta}_{3}\big|(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},{\underline{\bm{x}}})
has no closed form. Hence, we can use the MH within Gibbs sampling technique. Recall from Subsection
, considering a symmetric uniform proposal on (-1,1), the transition probability
p\bigl(\theta_{3(n)}\rightarrow\theta_{3(n+1)}\bigr)=\min\bigl\{1,\exp\{R_{1}-R_{2}\}\bigr\}
, is

\displaystyle R_{1}=-\frac{1}{2\theta_{2}\sqrt{1-\theta_{3(n+1)}^{2}}}\Bigl[\theta_{1}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}^{2}+\theta_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i2}^{2}-2\theta_{3(n+1)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}x_{i2}{}\Bigr]

\displaystyle R_{2}=-\frac{1}{2\theta_{2}\sqrt{1-\theta_{3(n)}^{2}}}\Bigl[\theta_{1}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}^{2}+\theta_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i2}^{2}-2\theta_{3(n)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}x_{i2}{}\Bigr]

(5.15)

In what follows, we give the steps for computing Bayesian estimation of
{\bm{\theta}}
.

1.

Read
N,M,a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2}
, and suggest
\bm{\theta}^{(0)}=\bigl(\theta^{(0)}_{1},\theta^{(0)}_{2},\theta^{(0)}_{3}\bigr)^{\top}
arbitrarily;

2.

Set
t=0
;

3.

Simulate
\theta^{(t+1)}_{1}
from the full conditional
\theta^{(t+1)}_{1}\big|\bigl(\theta^{(t)}_{2},\theta^{(t)}_{3},{\underline{\bm{x}}}\bigr)
that follows

\displaystyle{\cal{GIG}}\Bigl(a_{1},2b_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{2i}^{2}/\bigl[\theta^{(t)}_{2}(1-\theta^{(t)}_{3})^{1/2}\bigr],\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{1i}^{2}/\bigl[\theta^{(t)}_{2}(1-\theta^{(t)}_{3})^{1/2}\bigr]\Bigr);

4.

Having
\theta^{(t+1)}_{1}
, simulate
\theta^{(t+1)}_{2}
from the full conditional
\theta^{(t)}_{2}\big|\bigl(\theta^{(t+1)}_{1},\theta^{(t)}_{3},{\underline{\bm{x}}}\bigr)
that follows

\displaystyle{\cal{GIG}}\biggl(n+a_{2},2b_{2},\Bigl[\frac{1}{\theta^{(t+1)}_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}^{2}+\theta^{(t+1)}_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i2}^{2}-2\theta^{(t)}_{3}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}x_{i2}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\Bigl[\theta^{(t)}_{2}(1-\theta^{(t)}_{3})^{1/2}\Bigr]^{-1}\biggr);

5.

Having
\theta^{(t+1)}_{1}
and
\theta^{(t+1)}_{2}
, simulate
\theta^{(t+1)}_{3}
from the full conditional
\theta^{(t)}_{3}\big|\bigl(\theta^{(t+1)}_{2},\theta^{(t)}_{3},{\underline{\bm{x}}}\bigr)
using MH-within-Gibbs sampling technique as follows.

(a)

Set
k=0
,
K=100
, and choose the initial state as

y_{0}\sim{\cal{U}}(-1,1)
;

(b)

Generate
y^{(k+1)}\sim{\cal{U}}(-1,1)

(c)

Compute
p\bigl(y^{(k)}\rightarrow y^{(k+1)}\bigr)=\min\bigl\{1,\exp\{R_{1}-R_{2}\}\bigr\}

where

R_{1}=-\frac{1}{2\theta^{(t+1)}_{2}\sqrt{1-\bigl(y^{(k+1)}\bigr)^{2}}}\Bigl[\frac{1}{\theta^{(t+1)}_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}^{2}+\theta_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}^{2}-2y^{(k+1)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}x_{i2}\Bigr]

and

R_{2}=-\frac{1}{2\theta^{(t+1)}_{2}\sqrt{1-\bigl(y^{(k)}\bigr)^{2}}}\Bigl[\frac{1}{\theta^{(t+1)}_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}^{2}+\theta^{(t+1)}_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i2}^{2}-2y^{(k)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i1}x_{i2}\Bigr]

(d)

Generate
u\sim{\text{U}}(0,1)
;

(e)

If
u<p\bigl(y^{(k)},y^{(k+1)}\bigr)
then
y^{(k)}\rightarrow y^{(k+1)}
and
k\rightarrow k+1
;

(f)

Repeat algorithm from step (b);

(g)

If
k=K
then
y^{(k+1)}\rightarrow\theta^{(t+1)}_{3}
and stop the algorithm.

6.

If
t=N
, then go to the next step, otherwise
t+1\rightarrow t
and return step 3;

7.

The Bayesian estimator
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{B}
, is the average of
N-M+1
samples, that is,
\bm{\theta}^{(M+1)},\bm{\theta}^{(M+2)},\cdots,\bm{\theta}^{(M+N)}
generated from target PDF
\pi\bigl(\bm{\theta}|{\underline{\bm{x}}}\bigr)
.

We note that, in practice, hyperparameters
a_{1},b_{1},a_{2}
and
b_{2}
are determined based on experimenter’s belief or the empirical Bayes. If sample size is small, then the improper Jeffreys
prior
is suggested. It is noteworthy that for simulating from GIG distribution, one can use the method proposed by
Hörmann and Leydold (2014)
.

The pertaining
R
code for implementing example above is given as follows. To this end, a sample of
N=5000
realizations have been generated from a zero-mean bivariate Gaussian when
\sigma_{1}=0.5
,
\sigma_{2}=1
, and
\rho=-0.75
. The output of the Gibbs sampler is displayed in Figure
5.2
.

Figure 5.2:
Scatterplot of 5000 samples generated from full conditionals: (a)

{\theta}_{1}\big|(\theta_{2},\theta_{3},{{\bm{x}}})
, (b):

{\theta}_{2}\big|(\theta_{1},\theta_{3},{{\bm{x}}})
, and (c):

{\theta}_{3}\big|(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},{{\bm{x}}})
.

5-34

Suppose response variable
Y
depends linearly on independent variable (covariate)
X
through a simple linear model as

\displaystyle y_{i}=

\displaystyle\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}x_{i}+\epsilon_{i};\penalty\ \penalty\ i=1,2,3,\cdots,n.

(5.16)

Recording
n
measurements on
Y
represented as
y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\cdots,y_{n}
at the corresponding levels of covariate
X
shown by
x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}
, the focus is placed on estimating the regression coefficients
\beta_{0}
and
\beta_{1}
through the Bayesian approach. Herein,
\epsilon_{i}
s are the simple linear model’s theoretical errors, independently (that is,
{\text{cov}}\bigl(\epsilon_{i},\epsilon_{j}\bigr)=0
for
i\neq j=1,2,3,\cdots,n
) assumed to follow
{\cal{N}}\bigl(0,\sigma^{2}\bigr)
. Of course the skewed Student’s
t

(
Teimouri et al., 2020
)
and
\alpha
-stable
(
Nolan and Ojeda-Revah, 2013
)
may be reasonable candidates for distribution of theoretical error in presence of outliers that yield more robust estimation of
{\bm{\beta}}=(\beta_{0},\beta_{1})^{\top}
than the Gaussian one. It is known that the least-square (LS) estimator
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{ls}
and ML estimator
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{ml}
, of the regression coefficients
\bm{\beta}
are the same and given by

\displaystyle\hat{{\beta}_{1}}_{ML}=\hat{{\beta}_{1}}_{LS}

\displaystyle={\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-{\bar{x}})(y_{i}-{\bar{y}})}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-{\bar{x}})^{2}}},

\displaystyle\hat{{\beta}_{0}}_{ML}=\hat{{\beta}_{0}}_{LS}

\displaystyle={\bar{y}}-\hat{{\beta}_{0}}_{ML}\times\bar{x},

(5.17)

where
\bar{x}=\bigl(x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{n}\bigr)/n
. Assuming independent Gaussian priors for elements of
\bm{\beta}
and a mutually independent conjugate inverse gamma prior for
\delta=\sigma^{2}
as

\displaystyle\pi(\beta_{0})=

\displaystyle{\cal{N}}\bigl(\beta_{0}\big|\mu_{0},\sigma^{2}_{0}\bigr),

\displaystyle\pi(\beta_{1})=

\displaystyle{\cal{N}}\bigl(\beta_{1}\big|\mu_{1},\sigma^{2}_{1}\bigr),

\displaystyle\pi(\delta)=

\displaystyle{\cal{IG}}\Bigl(\delta\Big|\frac{a_{0}}{2},\frac{b_{0}}{2}\Bigr).

It follows that

\displaystyle\pi(\bm{\theta}|\bm{y},\bm{x})=

\displaystyle L\bigl(\bm{\theta}\big|\bm{y},\bm{x}\bigr)\pi(\beta_{0})\pi(\beta_{1})\pi(\delta)

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta^{\frac{n}{2}}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{2\delta}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(y_{i}-\beta_{0}-\beta_{1}x_{i}\bigr)^{2}\Bigr\}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}_{0}}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\bigl(\beta_{0}-\mu_{0}\bigr)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}_{0}}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}_{1}}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\bigl(\beta_{1}-\mu_{1}\bigr)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}_{1}}\Bigr\}\Bigl(\frac{b_{0}}{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{a_{0}}{2}}\frac{\delta^{-\frac{a_{0}}{2}-1}}{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{a_{0}}{2}\bigr)}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{b_{0}}{2\delta}\Bigr\},

(5.18)

where
\delta=\sigma^{2}
and
\bm{\theta}=({\beta}_{0},{\beta}_{1},\delta)^{\top}
. Then more algebra shows

\displaystyle\pi\bigl({\beta}_{0}\big|\bm{y},\bm{x},\bm{\theta}_{(-\beta_{0})}\bigr)={\cal{N}}\biggl({\beta}_{0}\bigg|D_{0}\biggl[\frac{\mu_{0}}{\sigma^{2}_{0}}+\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(y_{i}-\beta_{1}x_{i}\bigr)}{\sigma^{2}}\biggr],D_{0}\biggr),

(5.19)

and

\displaystyle\pi\bigl({\beta}_{1}\big|\bm{y},\bm{x},\bm{\theta}_{(-\beta_{1})}\bigr)={\cal{N}}\biggl({\beta}_{1}\bigg|D_{1}\biggl[\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sigma^{2}_{1}}+\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\bigl(y_{i}-\beta_{0}\bigr)}{\sigma^{2}}\biggr],D_{1}\biggr),

(5.20)

where
D_{0}=\sigma_{0}^{2}\delta/\bigl(n\sigma_{0}^{2}+\delta\bigr)
and
D_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{2}\delta/\bigl(\sigma_{1}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x^{2}_{i}+\delta\bigr)
. Furthermore, by considering transformation
\delta=\sigma^{2}
, it can be seen easily that

\displaystyle\pi\bigl({\delta}\big|\bm{y},\bm{x},\bm{\theta}_{(-\bm{\beta})}\bigr)={\cal{IG}}\biggl(\delta\bigg|\frac{a_{0}+n}{2},\frac{a_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(y_{i}-{x}_{i}{\beta}\bigr)^{2}}{2}\biggr]\biggr).

(5.21)

Applying a monotone transformation such used as above is a common trick in each Bayesian paradigm to yield a conjugate prior. Finally, the simple reverse transformation gives back the Bayesian estimator of
\sigma
.
There follows the steps for computing Bayesian estimation of
{\bm{\theta}}
.

1.

Read
N,M
, determine hyperparameters
\mu_{0},\mu_{1},\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},a_{0},b_{0}
, and propose
\bm{\theta}^{(0)}=\bigl(\beta^{(0)}_{0},\beta^{(0)}_{1},\delta^{(0)}\bigr)^{\top}
;

2.

Set
t=0
;

3.

Simulate
\beta^{(t+1)}_{0}
from the full conditional
\beta_{0}\big|\bigl({\bm{y}},{\bm{x}},\bm{\theta}_{(-{\beta}_{0})}\bigr)
that follows

\displaystyle{\cal{N}}\biggl(D^{(t)}_{0}\biggl[\frac{\mu_{0}}{\sigma^{2}_{0}}+\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(y_{i}-\beta^{(t)}_{1}x_{i}\bigr)}{\delta^{(t)}}\biggr],D^{(t)}_{0}\biggr),

where
D^{(t)}_{0}=\sigma_{0}^{2}\delta^{(t)}/\bigl(n\sigma_{0}^{2}+\delta^{(t)}\bigr)
.

4.

Having
{\beta}^{(t+1)}_{0}
, simulate
\beta^{(t+1)}_{1}
from the full conditional
\beta_{1}\big|\bigl({\bm{y}},{\bm{x}},\bm{\theta}_{(-{\beta}_{1})}\bigr)
that follows

\displaystyle{\cal{N}}\biggl(D^{(t)}_{1}\biggl[\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sigma^{2}_{1}}+\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\bigl(y_{i}-\beta^{(t+1)}_{0}\bigr)}{{\delta^{(t)}}}\biggr],D^{(t)}_{1}\biggr),

where
D^{(t)}_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{2}{\delta^{(t)}}/\bigl(\sigma_{1}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x^{2}_{i}+{\delta^{(t)}}\bigr)
.

5.

Having
\bm{\beta}^{(t+1)}
, simulate
{\delta}^{(t+1)}
from the full conditional
\delta\big|\bigl({\bm{y}},{\bm{x}},\bm{\theta}_{(-\delta)}\bigr)
that follows

\displaystyle{\cal{IG}}\biggl(\delta\bigg|\frac{a_{0}+n}{2},\frac{b_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(y_{i}-{\beta}^{(t+1)}_{0}-{x}_{i}{\beta}^{(t+1)}_{1}\bigr)^{2}}{2}\biggr]\biggr).

6.

If
t=N
, then go to the next step. Otherwise set
t+1\rightarrow t
and return to step 3;

7.

The average of sequence
\bigl\{\bm{\theta}^{(M+1)},\bm{\theta}^{(M+2)},\cdots,\bm{\theta}^{(M+N)}\bigr\}
sampled from target distribution with PDF
\pi\bigl(\bm{\theta}|\bm{y},\bm{x}\bigr)
is the Bayesian estimator
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{B}=\bigl(\hat{\beta_{0}}_{B},\hat{\beta_{1}}_{B},\hat{\sigma}_{B}\bigr)^{\top}
.

Herein, we set the hyperparameters
\{\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\}
are supposed to be
\widehat{\bm{\beta}_{ml}}
and also, we set
\sigma_{0}=1
,
\sigma_{1}=1
, and
\sigma_{2}=1
. Furthermore,
a_{0}
and
b_{0}
are determined through the empirical Bayes as follows.

\displaystyle m\bigl(\bm{y}|{a}_{0},{b}_{0}\bigr)=

\displaystyle\Pi_{i=1}^{n}m\bigl({y}_{i}|{a}_{0},{b}_{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}f({y}_{i}|\delta)\pi\bigl(\delta\big|{a}_{0},{b}_{0}\bigr)d\delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{2\delta}\bigl(y_{i}-\beta_{0}-\beta_{1}x_{i}\bigr)^{2}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\times\bigl(\frac{b_{0}}{2}\bigr)^{\frac{a_{0}}{2}}\delta^{-\frac{a_{0}}{2}-1}{\Gamma^{-1}\bigl(\frac{a_{0}}{2}\bigr)}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{b_{0}}{2\delta}\Bigr\}d\delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(\frac{b_{0}}{2}\bigr)^{\frac{a_{0}}{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\delta^{-\frac{a_{0}+1}{2}-1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{a_{0}}{2}\bigr)}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{2\delta}\bigl[\bigl(y_{i}-\beta_{0}-\beta_{1}x_{i}\bigr)^{2}+b_{0}\bigr]\Bigr\}d\delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{a_{0}+1}{2}\bigr)}{\sqrt{b_{0}\pi}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{a_{0}}{2}\bigr)}\Bigl[1+\frac{\bigl(y_{i}-\beta_{0}-\beta_{1}x_{i}\bigr)^{2}}{b_{0}}\Bigr]^{-\frac{a_{0}+1}{2}},

(5.22)

where
f({y}_{i}|\delta)={\cal{N}}\bigl({y}_{i}\big|\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}x_{i},\delta=\sigma^{2}\bigr)
. Further, taking into account the fact that if
a_{0}=b_{0}
, the RHS of (
5.22
) becomes
\Pi_{i=1}^{n}{{t}}(y_{i}|\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}x_{i},b_{0},a_{0})
. Assuming
\bm{\beta}
is known, the ML estimator of
a_{0}
and
b_{0}
is obtained by maximizing the RHS of (
5.22
)
based on the sequence of
n
observed data
\bigl\{y_{i}-\beta_{0}-\beta_{1}x_{i}\bigr\}_{i=1}^{n}
. Moreover, the hyperparameters can be estimated through the the moment-based (MO) approach. While the likelihood function in the RHS of (
5.22
) is complicated in terms of
a_{0}
, a simple argument shows that this function gets its maximum when
b_{0}
is large. We consider this fact when finding the ML estimators of
a_{0}
and
b_{0}
.
To illustrate how one can estimate the simple regression coefficients within a Bayesian framework, we generate
n=200
realizations
x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}
form uniform distribution on (-1,1) and then responses
y_{1},y_{2},\cdots,y_{n}
are obtained using the linear relationship
y_{i}=\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}x_{i}+\epsilon_{i}=5+2x_{i}+\epsilon_{i}
where
\epsilon_{i}\sim{\cal{N}}(0,1)
for
i=1,2,\cdots,n
.

5-35

Suppose response variable
Y
depends linearly on independent variable (covariate)
X
through a simple linear model as

\displaystyle y_{i}=

\displaystyle\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}x_{i1}+\beta_{2}x_{i2}+\cdots+\beta_{K}x_{iK}+\epsilon_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}+\epsilon_{i};\penalty\ \penalty\ i=1,2,3,\cdots,n,

where
\bm{x}_{i}=\bigl(1,x_{i1},\cdots,x_{iK}\bigr)^{\top}
,
\bm{\beta}=\bigl(\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{K}\bigr)^{\top}
is the vector of unknown regression coefficients, and
\epsilon_{i}
s are the model’s theoretical error assumed to follow
{\cal{N}}\bigl(0,\sigma^{2}\bigr)
independently (that is,
{\text{cov}}\bigl(\epsilon_{i},\epsilon_{j}\bigr)=0
for
i\neq j=1,2,3,\cdots,n
). Of course the skewed Student’s
t

(
Teimouri et al., 2020
)
and
\alpha
-stable
(
Nolan and Ojeda-Revah, 2013
)
may be reasonable candidates for distribution of theoretical error in presence of outliers that yield more robust estimation of
{\bm{\beta}}
than the Gaussian one. It is known that the least-square (LS) estimator
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{ls}
and ML estimator
\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{ml}
, of the regression coefficients
\bm{\beta}
are the same and given by

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{ML}=\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{LS}=\bigl(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}\bigr)^{-1}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y},

(5.23)

where
\bm{y}
(vector of responses) and
\bm{X}
is
n\times(K+1)
design matrix are

\displaystyle\bm{y}=\left[\begin{matrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\\
\vdots\\
y_{n}\end{matrix}\right],\penalty\ \bm{X}=\left[\begin{matrix}\bm{x}_{1}\\
\bm{x}_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{x}_{n}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}1&x_{11}&x_{12}&\cdots&x_{1K}\\
1&x_{21}&x_{22}&\cdots&x_{2K}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
1&x_{n1}&x_{n2}&\cdots&x_{nK}\\
\end{matrix}\right].

(5.24)

Although it is possible to consider a Gaussian conjugate prior for each regression coefficient separately, but we prefer to consider a
(K+1)
-dimensional Gaussian prior for vector of regression coefficients
\bm{\beta}
and a mutually independent inverse gamma conjugate prior for
\delta=\sigma^{2}
as

\displaystyle\pi(\bm{\beta})=

\displaystyle{\cal{N}}_{K+1}\bigl(\bm{\beta}\big|\bm{\mu}_{0},\Sigma_{0}\bigr),

\displaystyle\pi(\delta)=

\displaystyle{\cal{IG}}\Bigl(\delta\Big|\frac{a_{0}}{2},\frac{b_{0}}{2}\Bigr).

(5.25)

It follows that

\displaystyle\pi(\bm{\Psi}|\bm{y},\bm{x})=

\displaystyle L\bigl(\bm{\Psi}\big|\bm{y},\bm{x}\bigr)\pi(\bm{\beta})

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle\delta^{-\frac{n}{2}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{2\delta}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(y_{i}-\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}\bigr)^{2}\Bigr\}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\bm{\beta}-\bm{\mu}_{0}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}\bigl(\bm{\beta}-\bm{\mu}_{0}\bigr)\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\times\Bigl(\frac{a_{0}}{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{a_{0}}{2}}\frac{\delta^{-a_{0}/2-1}}{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{a_{0}}{2}\bigr)}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{a_{0}}{2\delta}\Bigr\},

(5.26)

where
\bm{\Psi}=(\bm{\beta}^{\top},\delta)^{\top}
. For computing the full conditional
\bm{\beta}\big|\bigl(\bm{y},\bm{x},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\bm{\beta})}\bigr)
, it is worth taking into account to the fact that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(y_{i}-\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}\bigr)^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}y^{2}_{i}-2\bm{\beta}^{\top}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y}+\bm{\beta}^{\top}\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}\bm{\beta}.

Then more algebra shows

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\bm{\beta}\big|\bm{y},\bm{x},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\bm{\beta})}\bigr)\propto

\displaystyle\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\bm{\beta}-\bm{\mu}_{\bullet}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}_{\bullet}\bigl(\bm{\beta}-\bm{\mu}_{\bullet}\bigr)\Bigr\},

where
\bm{\mu}_{\bullet}=\Sigma_{\bullet}\bigl[\Sigma_{0}^{-1}\bm{\mu}_{0}+\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y}\bigr]
and
\Sigma_{\bullet}=\bigl[\Sigma_{0}^{-1}+\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}\bigr]^{-1}
.
So,

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\bm{\beta}\big|\bm{y},\bm{x},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\bm{\beta})}\bigr)={\cal{N}}_{K+1}\biggl(\bm{\beta}\Big|\Sigma_{\bullet}\Bigl[\Sigma_{0}^{-1}\bm{\mu}_{0}+\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y}\Bigr],\Sigma_{\bullet}\biggr).

Furthermore, it can be seen easily that

\displaystyle\pi\bigl({\delta}\big|\bm{y},\bm{x},\bm{\Psi}_{(-{\delta})}\bigr)={\cal{IG}}\biggl(\delta\bigg|\frac{a_{0}+n}{2},\frac{b_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(y_{i}-\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}\bigr)^{2}}{2}\biggr]\biggr).

The hyperparameters
a_{0}
and
b_{0}
can be estimated in the same way as in Example
5-34
,
\bm{\mu}_{0}
is assumed to be (
5.23
), and
\Sigma_{0}=\text{cov}\bigl(\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{ML}\bigr)=\sigma^{2}\bigl(\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}\bigr)^{-1}
.
In what follows, we give the steps for computing Bayesian estimation of
{\bm{\Psi}}
in Example
5-35
.

1.

Read
N,M
, and determine hyperparameters
a_{0}
,
b_{0}
,
\bm{\mu}_{0}
, and
\Sigma_{0}
;

2.

Set
t=0
;

3.

Simulate
\bm{\beta}^{(t+1)}
from the full conditional
\bm{\beta}\big|\bigl({\bm{y}},{\bm{x}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\bm{\beta})}\bigr)
with PDF

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\bm{\beta}\big|\bm{y},\bm{x},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\bm{\beta})}\bigr)={\cal{N}}_{K+1}\Bigl(\bm{\beta}\Big|\bm{\mu}_{\bullet},\Sigma_{\bullet}\Bigl),

where
\bm{\mu}_{\bullet}=\Sigma_{\bullet}\bigl[\Sigma_{0}^{-1}\bm{\mu}_{0}+\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{y}\bigr]
and
\Sigma_{\bullet}=\bigl[\Sigma_{0}^{-1}+\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}\bigr]^{-1}
.
So,

4.

Having
\bm{\beta}^{(t+1)}
, set
{\delta}^{(t+1)}=\sqrt{z}
where
z
follows
{\cal{IG}}
distribution with PDF

\displaystyle{\cal{IG}}\biggl(z\bigg|\frac{a_{0}+n}{2},\frac{a_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(y_{i}-\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\beta}^{(t+1)}\bigr)^{2}}{2}\biggr]\biggr).

5.

If
t=N
, then go to the next step. Otherwise set
t+1\rightarrow t
and return to step 3;

6.

The average of sequence
\bigl\{\bm{\Psi}^{(M+1)},\bm{\Psi}^{(M+2)},\cdots,\bm{\Psi}^{(M+N)}\bigr\}
sampled from target distribution with PDF
\pi\bigl(\bm{\Psi}|\bm{y},\bm{x}\bigr)
is the Bayesian estimator
\hat{\bm{\Psi}}_{B}=\bigl(\hat{\bm{\beta}}_{B},\hat{\sigma}_{B}\bigr)^{\top}
.

Herein, Example
5-35
will be illustrated with computing the Bayesian estimator of regression plane coefficients fitted to
trees
data. This data set is available in
R
environment. The corresponding
R
code for implementing this example is given as follows. The output of the Gibbs sampler is displayed in Figure
5.4
.

(20240601)

(

<-

$

(

5

<-

(

$

,

$

6

R
>

K

<-

3

#

number

of

regression

coefficients

7

R
>

X

<-

as
.
matrix
(

cbind
(1,

x
),

nrow

=

n
,

5.3
Gibbs sampling in Bayesian paradigm with one latent variable

Sometimes implementing the MCMC techniques such as MH or Gibbs sampling for drawing the Bayesian inference is computationally inefficient due to the complex form of the posterior. In such cases, inference about the unknown parameter(s) may be drawn by involving some latent (
unobservable
or
augmented
) variable(s). The rationale behind using the term “latent” traced back to this fact that the experimenter cannot observe some extra variable(s) existing in the model at quick glance, but there exists. For example, let random vector
\bm{X}=(X_{1},\cdots,X_{p})^{\top}
follows a
p
-dimensional Student’s
t
distribution with
\nu>0
degrees of freedom with PDF given by (
3.155
). Evidently implementing the MH or Gibbs sampling in which the full conditionals are constructed based on PDF (
3.155
) is quite difficult. On the other hand, it is easy to see that
\bm{X}
admits the stochastic representation

\bm{X}\mathop{=}\limits^{d}\bm{\mu}+\Sigma^{1/2}\bm{Z}/\sqrt{G}
where
\bm{Z}\sim{\cal{N}}_{p}\bigl(\bm{0}_{p},\bm{I}_{p}\bigr)
and
G\sim{\cal{G}}(\nu/2,\nu/2)
(
{\cal{G}}(a,b)
accounts for a gamma distribution with shape and rate parameters,
a
and
b
, respectively.). Herein, random variable
G
plays the role of a latent variable. We also can consider more than one latent variable in each statistical model. Nevertheless, the Gibbs sampling is a very useful approach in presence of latent variable in the case of either sampling or Bayesian inference. In what follows, we give a definition that plays a main role in understanding Bayesian inference for the parameters of a statistical model that includes latent variable(s).

Definition 5.3.1
.

Let
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
are a sample of
n
independent observed values from a model with PDF
f(x|\bm{\theta})
where
\bm{\theta}
is the unknown parameter vector. If model includes latent variable
g
whose PDF is
f(g|\bm{\theta}_{g})
in which
\bm{\theta}_{g}
is the unknown parameter vector of distribution of
g
. Furthermore, let
f(x,g|\bm{\Psi})
is the joint PDF of vector
(x,g)^{\top}
where
\bm{\Psi}=\bm{\theta}\cup\bm{\theta}_{g}
. Then we have:

•

The sequence
\bigl\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\bigr\}
and
\bigl\{g_{1},\cdots,g_{n}\bigr\}
, are called the observed and latent data, respectively.

•

The sequence of paired-data
\{\bigl(x_{1},g_{1}\bigr),\cdots,\bigl(x_{n},g_{n}\bigr)\}
is called complete data that evidently is the union of observed and latent data.

•

The complete data likelihood function becomes

L_{c}(\bm{\Psi}|\bm{x},\bm{g})=f(\bm{x},\bm{g}|\bm{\Psi})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}f\bigl(x_{i}\big|g_{i},\bm{\Psi}\bigr)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}f\bigl(x_{i}\big|g_{i},\bm{\theta}\bigr)f\bigl(g_{i}\big|\bm{\theta}_{g}\bigr).

(5.27)

Definition
5.3.1
is based on just one latent variable, but can be easily extended and is valid for two or more latent variables. It is noteworthy that for a Bayesian paradigm in which we have latent variable(s), we rewrite the posterior (
5.26
) in terms of the
complete data
likelihood function as follows.

\displaystyle\pi(\bm{\Psi}|\bm{x},\bm{g})\propto L_{c}(\bm{\Psi}|\bm{x},\bm{g})\pi(\bm{\Psi}),

(5.28)

where
L_{c}(\bm{\Psi}|\bm{x},\bm{g})
is given by Definition
5.3.1
. To implement the Bayesian paradigm, notice that the conditional PDF
f\bigl(x_{i}\big|g_{i},\bm{\theta}\bigr)
and marginal PDF
f\bigl(g_{i}\big|\bm{\theta}_{g}\bigr)
given in RHS of (
5.27
) must be known and possibly in closed form. In what follows, we give an example in which Bayesian inference on the parameters of a Student’s
t
distribution is desired.

5-36

He et al. (2021)

Suppose
{\underline{\bm{x}}}=\bigl(\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{n}\bigr)
is vector of
n
independent observations from a
p
-dimensional Student’s
t
distribution. Recall that for each Student’s
t
random vector
\bm{X}
we can write
\bm{X}\mathop{=}\limits^{d}\bm{\mu}+\Sigma^{1/2}\bm{Z}/\sqrt{G}
. Evidently
\bm{X}
admits the hierarchy given by

\displaystyle\bm{X}|G

\displaystyle\sim f\bigl(\bm{x}_{i}|g_{i},\bm{\theta}=(\bm{\mu},\Sigma)\bigr)={\cal{N}}_{p}\Bigl(\bm{x}_{i}\Big|\bm{\mu},\frac{\Sigma}{{G}}\Bigr),

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\sim f\bigl(g_{i}\big|\bm{\theta}_{g}=\nu\bigr)={\cal{G}}\bigl(g_{i}\big|\nu/2,\nu/2\bigr),

(5.29)

where
{\cal{N}}_{p}\bigl(.\big|\bm{\mu},{\Sigma}\bigr)
denotes the PDF of a
p
-dimensional Gaussian distribution with location vector
\bm{\mu}
and scale matrix
{\Sigma}
and
{\cal{G}}(\cdot\big|a,b)
accounts for the PDF of a gamma distribution with shape parameter
a
and rate parameter
b
. Hence, the complete data likelihood is

\displaystyle L_{c}(\bm{\Psi}|{\underline{\bm{x}}},\bm{g})=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Bigl[{\cal{N}}_{p}\bigl(\bm{x}_{i}\big|\bm{\mu},[g_{i}]^{-1}\Sigma\bigr)f\bigl(g_{i}\big|\bm{\theta}_{g}\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigl[\Gamma\Bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)\Bigr]^{-n}\Bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{n\nu}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\biggl\{\bigl[g_{i}\bigr]^{\frac{\nu+p}{2}-1}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g_{i}}{2}\Bigl[\nu+\bigl(\bm{x}_{i}-\bm{\mu}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bigl(\bm{x}_{i}-\bm{\mu}\bigr)\Bigr]\Bigr\}\biggr\},

(5.30)

where
\bm{\Psi}=\bm{\theta}\cup\bm{\theta}_{g}=(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\nu)
is the model’s parameter vector.
For the prior, we proceed to assume independence, that is
\pi(\bm{\Psi})=\pi(\bm{\mu})\pi(\Sigma)\pi(\nu)
in which
\pi(\bm{\mu})
and
\pi(\Sigma)
are conjugate Gaussian and inverse Wishart priors. Furthermore, we let
\pi(\nu)
to be the PDF of a gamma distribution. We have

\displaystyle\pi(\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{N}}_{p}({\cal{M}}_{0},{\cal{S}}_{0}),

\displaystyle\pi(\Sigma)

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{IW}}({\cal{D}}_{0},\nu_{0}),

\displaystyle\pi(\nu)

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{G}}\bigl(a_{0},b_{0}\bigr).

We note that in above an objective prior such as Jeffreys’ prior can be chosen, however, the most concern is placed on
\pi(\nu)
that was proposed by
Fernández and Steel (1999)
to be
\pi(\nu)=10\exp\{-10\nu\}
.
It can be seen that the jeffreys’ prior becomes
\pi(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\nu)=|\Sigma|^{-(p+1)/2}

(
Fernández and Steel, 1999
)
, and so considering a flat prior for both of
\bm{\mu}
and
\nu
is acceptable. We note that for drawing inference about
\bm{\Psi}
, one needs to generate from full conditional of the latent variable too. So, by considering
\bm{\mu}
,
\Sigma
,
\nu
, and
g
(latent variable) as the model’s variables, within a Gibbs sampling framework, we proceed to sample from full conditionals
\bm{\mu}|({\Sigma},\nu)
,
\Sigma|(\bm{\mu},\nu)
,
\nu|(\bm{\mu},{\Sigma})
. But, keep in mind that for each observed data
\bm{y}_{i}
,
g_{i}
(for
i=1,\cdots,n
) is also latent
unobservable). In order to
g_{i}
being known, we proceed to simulate
g_{i}
(for
i=1\cdots,n
) for each
\bm{y}_{i}
. To do this, in each iteration of the Gibbs sampling, we simulate (for each
\bm{y}_{i}
) from full conditional
g_{i}\big|\bigl(\bm{\mu},{\Sigma},\nu,{\bm{y}}_{i}\bigr)
as follows. From RHS of (
5.30
), for a single observed value
\bm{y}_{i}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(g_{i}\big|\bm{\Psi},\bm{y}_{i}\bigr)

\displaystyle\propto\bigl[g_{i}\bigr]^{\frac{\nu+p}{2}-1}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g_{i}}{2}\Bigl[\nu+\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}\bigr)\Bigr]\Bigr\}.

This means that

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(g_{i}\big|\bm{\Psi},\bm{y}_{i}\bigr)\sim{\cal{G}}\Bigl((\nu+p)/2,\nu/2+\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}\bigr)/2\Bigr).

Let
\bm{\Psi}_{(-\theta)}
denote the whole parameter vector excluding its element
\theta
, once we have generated a whole vector of latent variable as
\bm{g}=(g_{1},\cdots,g_{n})^{\top}
, the complete data is known and we proceed to simulate from other full conditionals as follows.

•

full conditional of
\bm{\mu}\big|\bigl({{\underline{\bm{y}}}},\bm{g},\Psi_{(-\bm{\mu})}\bigr)
:
By considering a conjugate prior, shown earlier by
\pi(\bm{\mu})\sim{\cal{N}}_{p}({\cal{M}}_{0},{\cal{S}}_{0})
, we have

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\bm{\mu}\big|{{\underline{\bm{y}}}},\bm{g},\Psi_{(-\bm{\mu})}\bigr)\propto L_{c}(\bm{\Psi}|{\underline{\bm{x}}},\bm{g}){\cal{N}}_{p}\bigl(\bm{\mu}\big|{\cal{M}}_{0},{\cal{S}}_{0}\bigr).

More algebra shows that

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}\big|\bigl({{\underline{\bm{y}}}},\bm{g},\Psi_{(-\bm{\mu})}\bigr)\sim{\cal{N}}_{p}\Bigl({\cal{Q}}_{0}\Bigl[{\cal{S}}^{-1}_{0}{\cal{M}}_{0}+\Sigma^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\bm{y}_{i}\Bigr],{\cal{Q}}_{0}\Bigr),

where

\displaystyle{\cal{Q}}_{0}=\Bigl[{\cal{S}}^{-1}_{0}+\Sigma^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\Bigr]^{-1}.

•

full conditional of

{\Sigma}\big|\bigl({{\underline{\bm{y}}}},\bm{g},\Psi_{(-{\Sigma})}\bigr)
:
By considering a conjugate prior, that is
{\Sigma}\sim{\cal{IW}}\bigl({\cal{D}}_{0},\nu_{0}\bigr)
, we can write

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\Sigma\big|{{\underline{\bm{y}}}},\bm{g},\Psi_{(-\Sigma)}\bigr)\propto L_{c}(\bm{\Psi}|{\underline{\bm{x}}},\bm{g}){\cal{IW}}(\Sigma\big|{\cal{D}}_{0},\nu_{0}\bigr),

where
{\cal{IW}}(\Sigma\big|{\cal{D}}_{0},\nu_{0}\bigr)
denotes the PDF of an inverse Wishart distribution.
More algebra shows that

\displaystyle{\Sigma}\big|\bigl({{\underline{\bm{y}}}},\bm{g},\Psi_{(-{\Sigma})}\bigr)\sim{\cal{IW}}\bigl({\cal{R}}_{0},\nu_{0}+n\bigr),

where
{\cal{R}}_{0}={\cal{D}}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}\bigr)\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}\bigr)^{\top}
.

•

full conditional of

{\nu}\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\bm{g},\Psi_{(-{\nu})}\bigr)
:

By considering a truncated exponential distribution with PDF

\pi(\nu)={\cal{G}}\bigl(\nu-2\big|1,b_{0}\bigr)
for
\nu\geq 2
, we can write

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\nu\big|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\bm{g},\Psi_{(-{\nu})}\bigr)

\displaystyle\propto L_{c}(\bm{\Psi}|\underline{\bm{x}},\bm{g})\pi(\nu)

\displaystyle\propto\Bigl[\Gamma\Bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)\Bigr]^{-n}\Bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{n\nu}{2}}b_{0}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\nu}{2}\bigl[2b_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\bigr]\Bigr\}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bigl[g_{i}\bigr]^{\frac{\nu}{2}}.

(5.31)

As it is seen, the full conditional (
5.31
) has not closed form. The MH algorithm can be suggested to draw sample from this full conditional. It is easy to check that
\pi\bigl(\nu\big|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\bm{g},\Psi_{(-{\nu})}\bigr)
is log-concave and hence the ARS-within-Gibbs sampling algorithm can be suggested for simulating from this full conditional. For computing the vector of hyperparameter
\bm{\theta}_{0}=b_{0}
, it follows from hierarchy (
5.29
) that

\displaystyle m\bigl(\underline{\bm{x}}|{b}_{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\int_{2}^{\infty}f\bigl(\bm{x}_{i}\big|\nu\bigr)\pi\bigl(\nu\big|{b}_{0}\bigr)d\nu

\displaystyle=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\int_{2}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu+p}{2}\bigr)\pi\bigl(\nu\big|{b}_{0}\bigr)d\nu}{(\nu\pi)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}\bigr)|\Sigma|^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigl[1+\frac{(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})}{\nu}\bigr]^{\frac{\nu+p}{2}}},

(5.32)

where
\underline{\bm{x}}=(\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{n})
. Maximizing the RHS of (
5.32
) with respect to
b_{0}
is not a simple task, an so we proceed to compute the moment-type estimator of
b_{0}
as follows. First notice that

\displaystyle E\bigl[(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})(\bm{X}-\bm{\mu})^{\top}\big|b_{0}\bigr)=

\displaystyle\int_{2}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})^{\top}f\bigl(\bm{x}\big|\nu\bigr)\pi\bigl(\nu\big|{b}_{0}\bigr)d\bm{x}d\nu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{2}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}\frac{(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})^{\top}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu+p}{2}\bigr)}{(\nu\pi)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}\bigr)|\Sigma|^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigl[1+\frac{(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu})}{\nu}\bigr]^{\frac{\nu+p}{2}}}

\displaystyle\times\frac{b^{b_{0}-1}_{0}}{\Gamma(b_{0})}(\nu-2)^{b_{0}-1}\exp\{-b_{0}(\nu-2)\}d\bm{x}d\nu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{2}^{\infty}\Bigl(\frac{\nu}{\nu-2}{\Sigma}\Bigr)\frac{b^{b_{0}-1}_{0}}{\Gamma(b_{0})}(\nu-2)^{b_{0}-1}\exp\{-b_{0}(\nu-2)\}d\nu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3-\frac{1}{b_{0}}.

Let AD denote the average of diagonal elements of matrix
\big[\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bm{x}^{2}_{i}-\bm{\mu})(\bm{x}^{2}_{i}-\bm{\mu})^{\top}/n\bigr]\Sigma^{-1}
, then
b_{0}
can be exploited as
b_{0}=1/(3-AD)
.

Furthermore, one can employ the MH-within-Gibbs sampling technique for drawing sample from full conditional (
5.31
). To this end, we assume gamma distribution for the prior with PDF
\pi(\nu)={\cal{G}}\bigl(.\big|b_{0},b_{0}\bigr)
. Herein there is just one hyperparameter that we prefer to compute it as
b_{0}=1/(3-AD)
. We further choose
gamma\bigl(b_{0},b_{0}\bigr)
as the candidate. For other hyperparameters including
{\cal{M}}_{0},{\cal{S}}_{0},{\cal{D}}_{0}
, and
\nu_{0}
, in example above, we set
{\cal{M}}_{0}
to be the mean of
{\underline{\bm{y}}}
and
\nu_{0}=1
. For
{\cal{S}}_{0}
and
{\cal{D}}_{0}
we suggest to consider
\bm{I}_{p}
. The steps for computing the Bayesian estimator of
{\bm{\Psi}}
are given by Algorithm
13
.

Algorithm 13

Bayesian inference for Student’s
t
distribution

1:

Read
N
,
M
,
{{K}}
,
{\cal{M}}_{0}
,
{\cal{S}}_{0}
,
{\cal{D}}_{0}
, and
\nu_{0}
;

2:

Set
t\leftarrow 0
;

3:

Set
\bm{\mu}^{(0)}\leftarrow{\cal{M}}_{0}
,
\Sigma^{(0)}\leftarrow 1/n\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}^{(0)}\bigr)\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}^{(0)}\bigr)^{\top}
,
{\nu}^{(0)}=2
,

4:

and set
\bm{\Psi}^{(0)}=\bigl(\bm{\mu}^{(0)},\Sigma^{(0)},\nu^{(0)}\bigr)
;

5:

while

t\leq N

do

6:

Set
i=1
;

7:

while

i\leq n

do

8:

Simulate
g_{i}
from
{\cal{G}}(a,b)
where
a=(\nu^{(t)}+p+1)/2

and

b=

9:

\Bigl[\nu^{(t)}/2+\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}^{(t)}\bigr)^{\top}\bigl[\Sigma^{(t)}\bigr]^{-1}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}^{(t)}\bigr)\Bigr]/2
;

10:

Set
i\leftarrow i+1
;

11:

end

12:

Generate
\bm{\mu}^{(t+1)}
from

{\cal{N}}_{p}\Bigl({\cal{Q}}_{0}\Bigl[{\cal{S}}^{-1}_{0}{\cal{M}}_{0}+\bigl[\Sigma^{(t)}\bigr]^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\bm{y}_{i}\Bigr],{\cal{Q}}_{0}\Bigr)

where

13:

{\cal{Q}}_{0}=\Bigl[{\cal{S}}^{-1}_{0}+\bigl[\Sigma^{(t)}\bigr]^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\Bigr]^{-1}
;

14:

Generate
\Sigma^{(t+1)}
from

{\cal{IW}}\bigl({\cal{R}}_{0},\nu_{0}+n\bigr)
where
{\cal{R}}_{0}={\cal{D}}_{0}
+

15:

\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}^{(t+1)}\bigr)\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}^{(t+1)}\bigr)^{\top}
;

16:

Use the MH-within-Gibbs sampling technique for simulating
\nu^{(t+1)}

17:

as follows;

18:

Set
k=0
and
x_{0}\leftarrow\nu_{0}

19:

while

k\leq{{K}}

do

20:

x_{0}\sim{\cal{G}}\bigl(a_{0},b_{0}\bigr)
;

21:

Generate
x^{(k+1)}\sim{\cal{G}}\bigl(a_{0},b_{0}\bigr)
;

22:

Compute
p\bigl(x^{(k)}\rightarrow x^{(k+1)}\bigr)=\min\bigl\{1,\exp\{R_{1}-R_{2}\}\bigr\}

where

23:

R_{1}=-n\log\Gamma\bigl(\frac{x^{(k+1)}}{2}\bigr)+\frac{nx^{(k+1)}}{2}\log\bigl(\frac{x^{(k+1)}}{2}\bigr)+(b_{0}-1)\log x^{(k+1)}

24:

-\frac{x^{(k+1)}}{2}\bigl[2b_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\bigr]+\frac{x^{(k+1)}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log g_{i}

25:

and

26:

R_{2}=-n\log\Gamma\bigl(\frac{x^{(k)}}{2}\bigr)+\frac{nx^{(k)}}{2}\log\bigl(\frac{x^{(k)}}{2}\bigr)+(b_{0}-1)\log x^{(k)}

27:

-\frac{x^{(k)}}{2}\bigl[2b_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\bigr]+\frac{x^{(k)}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log g_{i}
;

28:

Generate
u\sim{\cal{U}}(0,1)
;

29:

If
u<p\bigl(x^{(k)},x^{(k+1)}\bigr)
then
x^{(k)}\rightarrow x^{(k+1)}
and
k\rightarrow k+1
;

30:

Repeat algorithm from line (21);

31:

If
k={{K}}
then
\nu^{(t+1)}\leftarrow x^{(k+1)}
and stop the algorithm;

32:

end

33:

Set
\bm{\Psi}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\bigl(\bm{\mu}^{(t+1)},\Sigma^{(t+1)},\nu^{(t+1)}\bigr)
and
t\leftarrow t+1
;

34:

end

35:

Sequence
\bigl\{\bm{\Psi}^{(M)},\bm{\Psi}^{(M+1)},\cdots,\bm{\Psi}^{(M)}\bigr\}
is a sample of size
N-M+1
from

36:

posterior with PDF
\pi\bigl(\bm{\Psi}|\underline{\bm{x}})
;

37:

The Bayesian estimator of
\hat{\bm{\Psi}}_{B}
is given by
\frac{1}{N-M+1}\sum_{t=1}^{N-M+1}\bm{\Psi}^{(t)}
.

The pertaining
R
code for implementing example above is given as follows. The
R
code is applied to a sample of

\sigma_{22}=0.75
, and

Figure 5.6:
Scatterplot of 5000 samples generated from full conditionals: (a)
\mu_{1}\big|\bigl({{\bm{y}}},{\bm{g}},\Psi_{(-{\mu}_{1})}\bigr)
, (b):
{\mu}_{2}\big|\bigl({{\bm{y}}},{\bm{g}},\Psi_{(-{\mu}_{2})}\bigr)
, (c):
{\Sigma}_{11}\big|\bigl({{\bm{y}}},{\bm{g}},\Psi_{(-\Sigma_{11})}\bigr)
, (d)
{\Sigma}_{12}\big|\bigl({{\bm{y}}},{\bm{g}},\Psi_{(-\Sigma_{12})}\bigr)
, (e)
{\Sigma}_{22}\big|\bigl({{\bm{y}}},{\bm{g}},\Psi_{(-\Sigma_{22})}\bigr)
, and (f)
{\nu}\big|\bigl({{\bm{y}}},{\bm{g}},\Psi_{(-\nu)}\bigr)
. The blue line, in each subfigure, shows the Bayesian estimator.

5.4
Gibbs sampling in Bayesian paradigm with two latent variables

5.4.1
Heavy-tailed skew models

The Bayesian paradigm for
\text{SG}_{p,q}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,{\Lambda})
distribution with representation (
3.150
) has been developed by
Liseo and Parisi (2013)
. The skew Gaussian model introduced in Section
2.8.4
can be developed for modelling the heavy tailed skewed data. This can be accomplished by involving an extra latent variable
G
. Let

\displaystyle\bm{Y}\mathop{=}\limits^{d}\bm{\mu}+\frac{\bm{X}}{\sqrt{h(G)}},

(5.33)

in which
\bm{X}\sim\text{SG}_{p,q}(\bm{0}_{p},\Sigma,{\Lambda})
and
h(\cdot)
is a continuous monotone real function. Equivalently,

\displaystyle\bm{Y}\mathop{=}\limits^{d}\bm{\mu}+\frac{{\Lambda}\big|\bm{Z}_{0}\big|}{\sqrt{h(G)}}+{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\bm{Z}_{1}}{\sqrt{h(G)}}.

(5.34)

It is easy to see that random vector
\bm{Y}
in (
5.34
) admits the hierarchy given by

\displaystyle\bm{Y}|{\bm{U}},G

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{N}}_{p}\Bigl(\bm{\mu}+{\Lambda}{\bm{U}},\frac{\Sigma}{h(G)}\Bigr),

\displaystyle{\bm{U}}|G

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{HN}}_{q}\Bigl(\bm{0}_{q},\frac{\bm{I}_{q}}{h(G)}\Bigr),

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\sim f(g|\bm{\theta}),

(5.35)

where the short form
{\cal{HN}}_{q}(\bm{0}_{q},\Sigma)
accounts for the half-normal distribution truncated on
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{q+}
with a zero-location vector of length
q
and scale matrix
\Sigma
.
Regarding
\bigl\{\bm{y}_{1},\cdots,\bm{y}_{n}\bigr\}
as the sequence of observed data and two sequences of latent variables are represented as
\bigl\{\bm{u}_{1},\cdots,\bm{u}_{n}\bigr\}
and
\bigl\{g_{1},\cdots,g_{n}\bigr\}
. Based on hierarchy (
5.35
)
the complete data log-likelihood is

\displaystyle L_{c}(\bm{\Psi}|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},\bm{g})=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Bigl[\bm{\phi}_{p}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}\big|\bm{\mu}+\bm{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i},[h(g_{i})]^{-1}\Sigma\bigr)\bm{\phi}_{q}\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}\big|\bm{0}_{q},[h(g_{i})]^{-1}\bm{I}_{q}\bigr)f\bigl(g_{i}\big|\bm{\theta}\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bigl[h(g_{i})\bigr]^{\frac{p+q}{2}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{h(g_{i})}{2}\Bigl[\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-\bm{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-\bm{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)

\displaystyle+\bm{u}^{\top}_{i}\bm{I}_{q}\bm{u}_{i}\Bigr]\Bigr\}f\bigl(g_{i}\big|\bm{\theta}\bigr),

(5.36)

where
\bm{\Psi}=(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\bm{\Lambda},\bm{\theta})
is the model’s parameter vector. We write
\bm{Y}\sim\text{SGX}_{p,q}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\bm{\Lambda},\bm{\theta})
(the term SG in SGX is a short form for skewed Gaussian and X refers to distribution of latent variable
G
), to denote the family of distributions that follow representation (
5.34
). For example, if in (
5.34
)
G
follows a generalized Lindley distribution, then
\text{SGGL}_{p,q}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\bm{\Lambda},\bm{\theta})
refers to the class of skew Gaussian generalized Lindely distribution in which
\bm{\theta}=(\omega,\beta,\gamma)^{\top}
.

Theorem 5.4.1
.

Let
\bm{Y}\sim\text{SGGL}_{p,q}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,{\Lambda},\bm{\theta})
. The PDF of
\bm{Y}
is given by

\displaystyle f(\bm{y}|\bm{\Psi})=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{q}\beta^{1-\frac{p}{2}}\omega^{\frac{p}{2}}}{(\beta+\gamma)}\bm{t}_{p}\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{\omega}{\beta}}(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu})\bigg|\bm{0},{\Omega},2\omega\Bigr)\bm{T}_{q}\Bigl(\bm{m}\sqrt{\frac{p+2\omega}{2\beta+d(\bm{y})}}\bigg|\bm{0},{\Delta},p+2\omega\Bigr)

\displaystyle+\frac{2^{q}\gamma\beta^{-\frac{p}{2}}(\omega+1)^{\frac{p}{2}}}{(\beta+\gamma)}\bm{t}_{p}\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{\omega+1}{\beta}}(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu})\bigg|\bm{0},{\Omega},2\omega+2\Bigr)

\displaystyle\times\bm{T}_{q}\Bigl(\bm{m}\sqrt{\frac{p+2\omega+2}{2\beta+d(\bm{y})}}\bigg|\bm{0},{\Delta},p+2\omega+2\Bigr),

where
\bm{\Psi}=(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,{\Lambda},\bm{\theta})

,
\bm{\theta}=(\omega,\beta,\gamma)^{\top}
,
d(\bm{y})=(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu})^{\top}{{\Omega}^{-1}}(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu})
,

\bm{m}={\Lambda}^{\top}{{\Omega}}^{-1}(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu})
,

{\Omega}={\Sigma}+{{\Lambda}}{{\Lambda}}^{\top}
,
{\Delta}=\bm{I}_{q}-{{\Lambda}}^{\top}{\Omega}^{-1}{{\Lambda}}
,
\bm{t}_{p}(.|\bm{0},\Omega,2\omega)
is the PDF of a
p
-dimensional Student’s
t
with location parameter
\bm{0}
, dispersion matrix
\Omega
and
2\omega
degrees of freedom, and
\bm{T}_{q}(.|\bm{0},{\Delta},p+2\omega+2)
is the CDF of
q
-dimensional Student’s
t
distribution with location parameter
\bm{0}
, dispersion matrix
{\Delta}
, and
p+2\omega+2
degrees of freedom.

Proof:

By definition, based on (
5.35
), we have

\displaystyle f_{\bm{Y}}(\bm{y}\big|{\Psi})=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{q+}}{\int_{0}^{\infty}}f(\bm{y}|\bm{t},g,{\Psi})f(\bm{t}|g)f(g|\bm{\theta})dgd\bm{t},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{q}|{\Sigma}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{(2\pi)^{\frac{p+q}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{q+}}{\int_{0}^{\infty}}g^{\frac{p+q}{2}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g}{2}(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu}-{\Lambda}\bm{t})^{\top}{{\Sigma}}^{-1}(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu}-{\Lambda}\bm{t})\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\times\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g}{2}\bm{t}^{\top}\bm{I}_{q}\bm{t}\Bigr\}f(g|\bm{\theta})dgd\bm{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{q}}{(2\pi)^{\frac{p+q}{2}}|{\Sigma}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{q+}}{\int_{0}^{\infty}}g^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g}{2}\Bigl[d(\bm{y})+(\bm{t}-\bm{m})^{\top}\Delta^{-1}(\bm{t}-\bm{m})\Bigr]\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\times f(g|\bm{\theta})dgd\bm{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{q}\beta^{\alpha+1}}{(\beta+\gamma)\Gamma(\alpha)(2\pi)^{\frac{p+q}{2}}|{\Sigma}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{q+}}{\int_{0}^{\infty}}g^{\frac{p+q+2\alpha}{2}-1}

\displaystyle\times\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g}{2}\Bigl[d(\bm{y})+(\bm{t}-\bm{m})^{\top}\Delta^{-1}(\bm{t}-\bm{m})+2\beta\Bigr]\Bigr\}dgd\bm{t}

\displaystyle+\frac{2^{q}\gamma\beta^{\alpha+1}}{\alpha(\beta+\gamma)\Gamma(\alpha)(2\pi)^{\frac{p+q}{2}}|{\Sigma}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{q+}}{\int_{0}^{\infty}}g^{\frac{p+q+2\alpha+2}{2}-1}

\displaystyle\times\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{g}{2}\Bigl[d(\bm{y})+(\bm{t}-\bm{m})^{\top}\Delta^{-1}(\bm{t}-\bm{m})+2\beta\Bigr]\Bigr\}dgd\bm{t}.

It follows that

\displaystyle f_{\bm{Y}}(\bm{y}\big|{\Psi})=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{q}\beta^{\alpha+1}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{p+q+2\alpha}{2}\bigr)|{\Sigma}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{(\beta+\gamma)\Gamma(\alpha)(2\pi)^{\frac{p+q}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{q+}}\frac{d\bm{t}}{\Bigl[\frac{2\beta+d(\bm{y})+(\bm{t}-\bm{m})^{\top}\Delta^{-1}(\bm{t}-\bm{m})}{2}\Bigr]^{\frac{p+q+2\alpha}{2}}}

\displaystyle+\frac{2^{q}\gamma\beta^{\alpha+1}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{p+q+2\alpha+2}{2}\bigr)|{\Sigma}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{\alpha(\beta+\gamma)\Gamma(\alpha)(2\pi)^{\frac{p+q}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{q+}}\frac{d\bm{t}}{\Bigl[\frac{2\beta+d(\bm{y})+(\bm{t}-\bm{m})^{\top}\Delta^{-1}(\bm{t}-\bm{m})}{2}\Bigr]^{\frac{p+q+2\alpha+2}{2}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I_{1}+I_{2}.

(5.37)

Using a change of variable
\bm{v}=(\bm{t}-\bm{m})\sqrt{p+2\alpha}/\sqrt{2\beta+d(\bm{y})}
, we can write

\displaystyle I_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{q}\beta^{\alpha+1}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{p+q+2\alpha}{2}\bigr)|{\Sigma}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{(\beta+\gamma)\Gamma(\alpha)(2\pi)^{\frac{p+q}{2}}}\frac{2^{\frac{p+q+2\alpha}{2}}(p+2\alpha)^{-\frac{q}{2}}}{\Bigl[2\beta+d(\bm{y})\Bigr]^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2}}{}}{\int_{-\bm{\infty}}^{\bm{m}_{0}}}\frac{d\bm{v}}{\Bigl[1+\frac{\bm{v}^{\top}\Delta^{-1}\bm{v}}{p+2\alpha}\Bigr]^{\frac{p+q+2\alpha}{2}}},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{q}\beta^{\alpha+1}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{p+2\alpha}{2}\bigr)|{\Sigma}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{(\beta+\gamma)\Gamma(\alpha)(2\pi)^{\frac{p}{2}}}\frac{2^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2}}|{\Delta}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\Bigl[2\beta+d(\bm{y})\Bigr]^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2}}{}}\bm{T}_{q}\Bigl(\bm{m}\sqrt{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2\beta+d(\bm{y})}}\Big|\bm{0},\Delta,p+2\alpha\Bigr),

where
\bm{m}_{0}=\bm{m}\sqrt{p+2\alpha}/\sqrt{2\beta+d(\bm{y})}
. It follows that

\displaystyle I_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{q}\beta^{\alpha+1}2^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2}}(2\alpha)^{\frac{p}{2}}{|\Omega}|^{\frac{1}{2}}|{\Delta}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{(\beta+\gamma)2^{\frac{p}{2}}|{\Sigma}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{p+2\alpha}{2}\bigr)}{\pi^{\frac{p}{2}}(2\alpha)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Gamma(\alpha){|\Omega}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigl[2\beta+d(\bm{y})\Bigr]^{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2}}}

\displaystyle\times\bm{T}_{q}\Bigl(\bm{m}\sqrt{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2\beta+d(\bm{y})}}\Big|\bm{0},\Delta,p+2\alpha\Bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{q}\beta^{1-\frac{p}{2}}\alpha^{\frac{p}{2}}}{(\beta+\gamma)}\bm{t}_{p}\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu})\Big|\bm{0},{\Omega},2\alpha\Bigr)\bm{T}_{q}\Bigl(\bm{m}\sqrt{\frac{p+2\alpha}{2\beta+d(\bm{y})}}\Big|\bm{0},\Delta,p+2\alpha\Bigr).

(5.38)

It should be noted that for obtaining the term in the RHS of (
5.38
), we use this fact that
|{\Delta}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Omega|^{\frac{1}{2}}/|{\Sigma}|^{\frac{1}{2}}=1
. Likewise, we can obtain
I_{2}
as

\displaystyle I_{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{q}\gamma\beta^{\alpha+1}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{p+2\alpha+2}{2}\bigr)}{\alpha(\beta+\gamma)\Gamma(\alpha)(2\pi)^{\frac{p}{2}}|{\Sigma}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{2^{\frac{p+2\alpha+2}{2}}|{\Delta}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\Bigl[2\beta+d(\bm{y})\Bigr]^{\frac{p+2\alpha+2}{2}}{}}

\displaystyle\times\bm{T}_{q}\Bigl(\bm{m}\sqrt{\frac{p+2\alpha+2}{2\beta+d(\bm{y})}}\Big|\bm{0},\Delta,p+2\alpha\Bigr),

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{q}\gamma\beta^{\alpha+1}2^{\frac{p+2\alpha+2}{2}}(2\alpha+2)^{\frac{p}{2}}{|\Omega}|^{\frac{1}{2}}|{\Delta}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{(\beta+\gamma)2^{\frac{p}{2}}|{\Sigma}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{p+2\alpha+2}{2}\bigr)\Bigl[2\beta+d(\bm{y})\Bigr]^{-\frac{p+2\alpha+2}{2}}}{\pi^{\frac{p}{2}}(2\alpha+2)^{\frac{p}{2}}\alpha\Gamma(\alpha)|\Omega|^{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\times\bm{T}_{q}\Bigl(\bm{m}\sqrt{\frac{p+2\alpha+2}{2\beta+d(\bm{y})}}\Big|\bm{0},\Delta,p+2\alpha+2\Bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{q}\gamma\beta^{-\frac{p}{2}}(\alpha+1)^{\frac{p}{2}}}{(\beta+\gamma)}\bm{t}_{p}\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{\alpha+1}{\beta}}(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu})\Big|\bm{0},{\Omega},2\alpha+2\Bigr)

\displaystyle\times\bm{T}_{q}\Bigl(\bm{m}\sqrt{\frac{p+2\alpha+2}{2\beta+d(\bm{y})}}\Big|\bm{0},\Delta,p+2\alpha+2\Bigr).

The proof is complete.

Corollary 5.4.1
.

If in (
5.35
)
G\sim{\cal{G}}(\nu/2,\nu/2)
, then
\bm{Y}\sim\text{SNG}_{p,q}(\nu,\bm{\mu},\Sigma,{\Lambda})
follows skew Student’s
t
distribution with PDF given by

\displaystyle f(\bm{y}|\bm{\Psi})=

\displaystyle{2^{q}}\bm{t}_{p}\bigl(\bm{y}\big|\bm{\mu},{\Omega},\nu\bigr)\bm{T}_{q}\Bigl(\bm{m}\sqrt{\frac{\nu+p}{\nu+d(\bm{y})}}\bigg|\bm{0},{\Delta},p+\nu\Bigr),

where
\bm{\Psi}=(\nu,\bm{\mu},\Sigma,{\Lambda})
.

Theorem 5.4.2
.

Let
\bm{Y}\sim\text{SNGIG}_{p,q}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,{\Lambda},\bm{\theta})
. The PDF of
\bm{Y}
is given by

\displaystyle f(\bm{y}|\bm{\Psi})=

\displaystyle 2^{q}\bm{h}_{p}\bigl(\bm{y}\big|\bm{\mu},{\Omega},\tau,\chi,\chi\bigr)\bm{H}_{q}\Bigl(\bm{m}\Bigl[\frac{\chi}{\chi+d(\bm{y})}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{4}}\Big|\bm{0},{\Delta},\tau-\frac{p}{2},\kappa,\kappa\Bigr),

where
\bm{\Psi}=(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\bm{\Lambda},\bm{\theta})
,
\bm{\theta}=(\tau,\chi)^{\top}
,
\kappa=\chi\sqrt{1+d(\bm{y})/\chi}
,
\bm{h}_{p}(.\big|\bm{\mu},{\Omega},\tau,\chi,\chi)
and
\bm{H}_{q}\bigl(\cdot\big|\bm{0},{\Delta},\tau-p/2,\kappa,\kappa\bigr)
are the PDF and CDF of a
p
- and
q
-dimensional symmetric hyperbolic distribution, respectively.

Proof:
See
Murray et al. (2017)
.

Table 5.1:
Family of heavy-tailed skew models

Characteristics

\text{SGGL}_{p,q}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,{\Lambda},\bm{\theta})

\text{SGG}_{p,q}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,{\Lambda},\bm{\theta})

\text{SGGIG}_{p,q}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,{\Lambda},\bm{\theta})

family of
G

Generalized Lindley (
{\cal{GL}}(\omega,\beta,\gamma)
)

{\cal{G}}(\eta,\zeta)

generalized inverse Gaussian (
{\cal{GIG}}(\tau,\chi,\chi)
)

parameter

{\bm{\theta}}=(\omega,\beta,\gamma)^{\top}

{\bm{\theta}}=(\eta,\zeta)^{\top}

{\bm{\theta}}=(\tau,\chi)^{\top}

PDF of
G

f(g|\bm{\theta})=\frac{\beta^{\omega+1}g^{\omega-1}(\omega+\gamma g)}{(\beta+\gamma)\Gamma(\omega+1)}\exp\bigl\{-\beta g\bigr\}

f(g|\bm{\theta})=\frac{\zeta^{\eta}}{\Gamma(\eta)}g^{\eta-1}\exp\{-\zeta g\}

f(g|\bm{\theta})=\frac{g^{\tau-1}}{2{\cal{K}}_{\tau}({\chi})}\exp\bigl\{-\frac{\chi g}{2}-\frac{\chi}{2g}\bigr\}

PDF

f(\bm{y}|\bm{\Psi})=\frac{2^{q}\beta^{1-\frac{p}{2}}\omega^{\frac{p}{2}}}{(\beta+\gamma)}\bm{t}_{p}\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{\omega}{\beta}}(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu})\bigg|\bm{0},{\Omega},2\omega\Bigr)

f(\bm{y}|\bm{\Psi})=2^{q}\bigl(\frac{\eta}{\zeta}\bigr)^{\frac{p}{2}}

f(\bm{y}|\bm{\Psi})={2^{q}}\bm{h}_{p}\Bigl(\bm{y}\big|\bm{\mu},{\Omega},\tau,\chi,\chi\Bigr)

\times\bm{T}_{q}\Bigl(\bm{m}\sqrt{\frac{p+2\omega}{2\beta+d(\bm{y})}}\bigg|\bm{0},{\Delta},p+2\omega\Bigr)

\times\bm{t}_{p}\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{\eta}{\zeta}}(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu})\bigg|\bm{0},{\Omega},2\eta\Bigr)

\times\bm{H}_{q}\Bigl(\bm{m}\Bigl[\frac{\chi}{\chi+d(\bm{y})}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{4}}\Big|\bm{0},\Delta,\tau-\frac{p}{2},\chi\sqrt{1+\frac{d(\bm{y})}{\chi}},

+\frac{2^{q}\gamma\beta^{-\frac{p}{2}}(\omega+1)^{\frac{p}{2}}}{(\beta+\gamma)}\bm{t}_{p}\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{\omega+1}{\beta}}(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu})\bigg|\bm{0},{\Omega},2\omega+2\Bigr)

\times\bm{T}_{q}\Bigl(\bm{m}\sqrt{\frac{p+2\eta}{2\zeta+d(\bm{y})}}\bigg|\bm{0},{\Delta},p+2\eta\Bigr)

\chi\sqrt{1+\frac{d(\bm{y})}{\chi}}\biggr)

\times\bm{T}_{q}\Bigl(\bm{m}\sqrt{\frac{p+2\omega+2}{2\beta+d(\bm{y})}}\bigg|\bm{0},{\Delta},p+2\omega+2\Bigr)

Representation

\bm{Y}\mathop{=}\limits^{d}\bm{\mu}+\frac{{{\Lambda}}|\bm{Z}_{0}|}{\sqrt{GL}}+{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\bm{Z}_{1}}{\sqrt{GL}}

\bm{Y}\mathop{=}\limits^{d}\bm{\mu}+\frac{{{\Lambda}}|\bm{Z}_{0}|}{\sqrt{G}}+{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\bm{Z}_{1}}{\sqrt{G}}

\bm{Y}\mathop{=}\limits^{d}\bm{\mu}+\frac{{\Lambda}}{\sqrt{GIG}}\big|\bm{Z}_{0}\big|+{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\bm{Z}_{1}}{\sqrt{GIG}}

Full conditional
G|(\bm{Y}=\bm{y}_{i},\cdots)

Implementing the Gibbs sampling for making Bayesian inference about parameters of the heavy-tailed skew models represented earlier in (
5.35
) involves sampling from the full conditionals of variables
G
and
\bm{U}
. Herein, we represent the full conditionals
{G}|(\bm{Y}=\bm{y}_{i},\bm{U}=\bm{u}_{i},\bm{\Psi})
and
\bm{U}|(\bm{Y}=\bm{y}_{i},{G}={g}_{i},\bm{\Psi})
, and describe how to sample from it under three scenarios shown in Table
5.1
.

i.

Scenario 1 (mixing random variable is
{\cal{GL}}(\omega,\beta,\gamma)
):

By definition we have

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(g_{i}\big|\bm{y}_{i},\bm{u}_{i},\bm{\Psi}\bigr)=

\displaystyle\frac{f\bigl(\bm{y}_{i},\bm{u}_{i},g_{i}\big|\bm{\Psi}\bigr)}{f\bigl(\bm{y}_{i},\bm{u}_{i}\big|\bm{\Psi}\bigr)}

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle f\bigl(\bm{y}_{i},\bm{u}_{i},g_{i}\big|\bm{\Psi}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}\big|\bm{u}_{i},g_{i}\big|\bm{\Psi}\bigr)f\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}\big|g_{i}\bigr)f\bigl(g_{i}\big|\bm{\theta}\bigr)

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle\frac{\beta^{\omega+1}}{(\beta+\gamma)\Gamma(\omega+1)}\Bigl[h(g_{i})\Bigr]^{\frac{p+q}{2}}\Bigl[g_{i}^{\omega-1}(\omega+\gamma g_{i})\Bigr]\exp\Bigl\{-\beta g_{i}

\displaystyle-\frac{h(g_{i})}{2}\Bigl[\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-\bm{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-\bm{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)+\bm{u}^{\top}_{i}\bm{u}_{i}\Bigr]\Bigr\}.

(5.39)

Setting
h(g_{i})=g_{i}
in the RHS of (
5.39
), it turns out that

\displaystyle\pi(g_{i}|\bm{y}_{i},\bm{u}_{i},\bm{\Psi})\propto

\displaystyle g_{i}^{\frac{p+q}{2}+\omega-1}(\omega+\gamma g_{i})\exp\Bigl\{-g_{i}\Bigl[\beta+\frac{\bm{u}^{\top}_{i}\bm{u}_{i}}{2}+\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)^{\top}

\displaystyle\Sigma^{-1}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)/2\Bigr]\Bigr\}.

(5.40)

Comparing the RHS of (
5.40
) with the PDF
f(g|\bm{\theta})
given in the first column of Table
5.1
. Then more algebra shows that

\displaystyle G|\bigl(\bm{Y}=\bm{y}_{i},\bm{U}=\bm{u}_{i},\bm{\Psi}\bigr)\sim{\cal{GL}}\bigl(\omega^{*},\beta^{*},\gamma^{*}\bigr),

(5.41)

where
\omega^{*}=(p+q)/2+\omega
,
\beta^{*}=\beta+\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-\bm{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)/2+\bm{u}^{\top}_{i}\bm{u}_{i}/2
, and
\gamma^{*}=\gamma/\omega
.

ii.

Scenario 2 (mixing random variable is
{\cal{G}}(\eta,\zeta)
):
Following the same argument as in Scenario 1 when
\gamma=0
,
\omega=\eta
, and
\beta=\zeta
, then it turns out from RHS of (
5.41
) that

\displaystyle G|\bigl(\bm{Y}=\bm{y}_{i},\bm{U}=\bm{u}_{i},\bm{\Psi}\bigr)\sim{\cal{G}}\bigl(\eta^{*},\zeta^{*}\bigr),

(5.42)

where
\eta^{*}=(p+q)/2+\eta
and
\zeta^{*}=2\zeta+\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)/2+\bm{u}^{\top}_{i}\bm{u}_{i}/2
.

iii.

Scenario 3 (mixing random variable is
{\cal{GIG}}(\tau,\chi,\chi)
):
If we let
f\bigl(g_{i}\big|,\bm{\theta}\bigr)
in the RHS of (
5.36
) to be PDF of GIG distribution whose form is given in third column of Table
5.1
, we have

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(g_{i}\big|\bm{y}_{i},\bm{u}_{i},\bm{\Psi}\big)\propto

\displaystyle\biggl[\frac{g_{i}^{\tau-1}}{2{\cal{K}}_{\tau}({\chi})}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{\chi g_{i}}{2}-\frac{\chi}{2g_{i}}\Bigr\}\biggr]\bigl[h(g_{i})\bigr]^{\frac{p+q}{2}}\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{h(g_{i})}{2}

\displaystyle\times\Bigl[\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-\bm{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-\bm{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)+\bm{u}^{\top}_{i}\bm{I}_{q}\bm{u}_{i}\Bigr]\Bigr\},

(5.43)

If in the RHS of (
5.43
) we let
h(g_{i})=g_{i}
, then it turns out that

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(g_{i}\big|\bm{y}_{i},\bm{u}_{i},\bm{\Psi}\big)\propto

\displaystyle g_{i}^{\frac{p+q}{2}+\tau-1}\exp\Bigl\{-\epsilon\frac{g_{i}}{2}-\frac{\chi}{2g_{i}}\Bigr\}.

(5.44)

Evidently,

\displaystyle{G}|\bigl(\bm{Y}=\bm{y}_{i},\bm{U}=\bm{u}_{i},\bm{\Psi}\bigr)\sim{\cal{GIG}}\Bigl(\frac{p}{2}+\frac{q}{2}+\tau,\epsilon,\chi\Bigr),

where
\epsilon=\bigl[\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)+\bm{u}^{\top}_{i}\bm{u}_{i}+\chi\bigr]
.

Full conditional
\bm{U}|(\bm{Y}=\bm{y}_{i},\cdots)

By definition we have

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}\big|\bm{y}_{i},g_{i},\bm{\Psi}\bigr)=

\displaystyle\frac{f\bigl(\bm{y}_{i},\bm{u}_{i},g_{i}\big|\bm{\Psi}\bigr)}{f\bigl(\bm{y}_{i},{g}_{i}\big|\bm{\Psi}\bigr)}\times\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}^{q+}}(\bm{u}_{i})

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle f\bigl(\bm{y}_{i},\bm{u}_{i},g_{i}\big|\bm{\Psi}\bigr)\times\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}^{q+}}(\bm{u}_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}\big|\bm{u}_{i},g_{i}\big|\bm{\Psi}\bigr)f\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}\big|g_{i}\bigr)f\bigl(g_{i}\big|\bm{\theta}\bigr)\times\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}^{q+}}(\bm{u}_{i})

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle f\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}\big|\bm{u}_{i},g_{i}\big|\bm{\Psi}\bigr)f\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}\big|g_{i}\bigr)\times\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}^{q+}}(\bm{u}_{i})

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle-\exp\Bigl\{\frac{h(g_{i})}{2}\Bigl[\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-\bm{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-\bm{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)

\displaystyle+\bm{u}^{\top}_{i}\bm{u}_{i}\Bigr]\Bigr\}\times\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}^{q+}}(\bm{u}_{i}),

where
\mathbb{I}_{{\cal{S}}}(x)
is defined in (
3-17
). We use the fact that

\displaystyle\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-\bm{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-\bm{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)+\bm{u}^{\top}_{i}\bm{u}_{i}

\displaystyle=d\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}\bigr)+\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}-\bm{m}\bigr)^{\top}\Delta^{-1}\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}-\bm{m}\bigr),

(5.45)

where
d\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}\bigr)
,
\bm{m}
, and
\Delta
are defined in Theorem
5.4.1
. Hence,

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}\big|\bm{y}_{i},g_{i},\bm{\Psi}\bigr)\propto

\displaystyle\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{h(g_{i})}{2}\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}-\bm{m}\bigr)^{\top}\Delta^{-1}\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}-\bm{m}\bigr)\Bigr\}\times\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}^{q+}}(\bm{u}_{i}).

(5.46)

This means that

\displaystyle\bm{U}|\bigl(\bm{Y}=\bm{y}_{i},G={g}_{i},\bm{\Psi}\bigr)\sim{\cal{TN}}_{q}\Bigl(\bm{m},\frac{\Delta}{h(g_{i})}\bm{0},\bm{\infty}\Bigr),

(5.47)

where
{\cal{TN}}_{q}(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
refers to the family of truncated
q
-dimensional Gaussian distributions introduced in Sub-section
3.11
.

Bayesian inference for skew Student’s
t
distribution

As it is seen from second column of Table
5.1
, the skew Gaussian gamma distribution in which
G\sim{\cal{G}}(\alpha,\beta)
specializes, for
\alpha=\beta=\nu/2
, to skew Student’s
t
distribution with
\nu
degrees of freedom for which a Bayesian paradigm has been developed by
(
Parisi and Liseo, 2018
)
. Herein, we compute the Bayesian estimators for parameters of
\text{SGG}_{p,q}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\bm{\Lambda},\bm{\theta})
in which
\bm{\theta}=(\alpha,\beta)^{\top}
. To this end, first, we simulate from full conditionals
g_{i}\big|\bigl(\bm{\mu},{\Sigma},\nu,\bm{u}_{i},{\bm{y}}_{i}\bigr)
and
\bm{u}_{i}\big|\bigl(\bm{\mu},{\Sigma},\nu,{{g}}_{i},{\bm{y}}_{i}\bigr)
(for
i=1\cdots,n
) in order to construct the complete data
\{(\bm{y}_{1},\bm{u}_{1},g_{1}),\cdots,(\bm{y}_{n},\bm{u}_{n},g_{n})\}
. It follows from the RHS of (
5.42
) that

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(g_{i}\big|\bm{y}_{i},\bm{u}_{i},\bm{\Psi}\bigr)={\cal{G}}(\alpha^{*},\beta^{*}),

(5.48)

where
\alpha^{*}=(p+q)/2+\alpha
and
\beta^{*}=2\beta+\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-\bm{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-\bm{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)/2+\bm{u}^{\top}_{i}\bm{u}_{i}/2
. Moreover, using
h(g_{i})=g_{i}
, it turns out from (
5.47
) that

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\bm{u}_{i}\Big|\bm{y}_{i},g_{i},\bm{\Psi}\bigr)={\cal{TN}}_{q}\Bigl(\bm{m}_{i},\frac{{\Delta}}{{g_{i}}},\bm{0},\bm{\infty}\Bigr),

(5.49)

where
\bm{m}_{i}={\Lambda}^{\top}\Omega^{-1}(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu})
,
{\Delta}=\bm{I}_{q}-{{\Lambda}}^{\top}{\Omega}^{-1}{{\Lambda}}
, and
{\Omega}={\Sigma}+{{\Lambda}}{{\Lambda}}^{\top}
.
Constructing the complete data, we proceed to generate from the remainder full conditionals

\bm{\mu}\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\bm{\mu})}\bigr)
,

\Sigma\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-{\Sigma})}\bigr)
,

{\Lambda}\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-{\Lambda})}\bigr)
,

\alpha\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-{\alpha})}\bigr)
, and

\beta\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-{\beta})}\bigr)
in which
{\underline{\bm{y}}}=(\bm{y}^{\top}_{1},\cdots,\bm{y}^{\top}_{n})^{\top}
,
\underline{\bm{u}}=(\bm{u}^{\top}_{1},\cdots,\bm{u}^{\top}_{n})^{\top}
, and
{\bm{y}}=({g}_{1},\cdots,{g}_{n})^{\top}
. First, we notice that

\displaystyle\pi(\bm{\Psi}|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}})

\displaystyle\propto L_{c}(\bm{\Psi}|\underline{\bm{y}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}})\pi(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=L_{c}(\bm{\Psi}|\underline{\bm{y}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}})\pi(\bm{\mu})\pi(\Sigma)\pi({\Lambda})\pi(\alpha)\pi(\beta),

(5.50)

where, as usual, we assumed that priors are independent and furthermore

\displaystyle\pi(\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{N}}_{p}\bigl({\cal{M}}_{0},{\cal{S}}_{0}\bigr),

\displaystyle\pi(\Sigma)

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{IW}}\bigl({\cal{D}}_{0},\nu_{0}\bigr),

\displaystyle\pi(\Lambda)

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{N}}_{q}\bigl({\cal{L}}_{0},{\cal{P}}_{0}\bigr),

\displaystyle\pi(\alpha)

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{G}}\bigl(a_{0},a_{0}\bigr),

\displaystyle\pi(\beta)

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{G}}\bigl(b_{0},b_{0}\bigr).

As it is seen, for priors of
\alpha
and
\beta
, we assumed a gamma distribution with common shape and rate parameters. The results below give the full conditionals needed for implementing the Bayesian paradigm.

•

full conditional of
\bm{\mu}\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\bm{\mu})}\bigr)
:
By considering a conjugate prior, that is
\pi(\bm{\mu})\sim{\cal{N}}_{p}({\cal{M}}_{0},{\cal{S}}_{0})
, more algebra shows that

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\bm{\mu}\big|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\bm{\mu})}\bigr)={\cal{N}}_{p}\Bigl(\bm{\mu}\Big|{\cal{Q}}_{0}\Bigl[{\cal{S}}^{-1}_{0}{\cal{M}}_{0}+\Sigma^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}(\bm{y}_{i}-{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i})\Bigr],{\cal{Q}}_{0}\Bigr),

where

\displaystyle{\cal{Q}}_{0}=\Bigl[{\cal{S}}^{-1}_{0}+\Sigma^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\Bigr]^{-1}.

•

full conditional of

\Sigma\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-{\Sigma})}\bigr)
:
By considering a conjugate prior, that is
{\Sigma}\sim{\cal{IW}}({\cal{D}}_{0},\nu_{0})
, we can write

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\Sigma\big|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-{\Sigma})}\bigr)={\cal{IW}}\bigl(\cdot\big|{\cal{R}}_{0},\nu_{0}+n\bigr),

where
{\cal{R}}_{0}={\cal{D}}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-{\Lambda}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)^{\top}
.

•

full conditional of

{\Lambda}\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-{{\Lambda}})}\bigr)
:
Although a possible choice for prior of
{\Lambda}
is a matrix normal normal distribution, but we prefer rather to sample from this full conditional in a slightly different method. We perform this by sampling from each column of
{\Lambda}
separately. Let
{{\Lambda}}_{(c)}
denote the
c
-th column (for
c=1,\dots,q
) of matrix
{\Lambda}
and
{\Lambda}_{(-c)}
is the matrix
{\Lambda}
when its
c
-th column is removed. Likewise, assume
u_{i(c)}
denote the
c
-th element of vector
\bm{u}_{i}
and
\bm{u}_{i(-c)}
is vector
\bm{u}_{i}
when its
c
-th element is removed. Suppose
\bm{\xi}_{i}=\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-{{\Lambda}}_{(-c)}\bm{u}_{i(-c)}
, then it can be seen that

\displaystyle\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-{{\Lambda}}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}-{{\Lambda}}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl({\Lambda}_{(c)}{u}_{i(c)}-\bm{\xi}_{i}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bigl({\Lambda}_{(c)}{u}_{i(c)}-\bm{\xi}_{i}\bigr).

Replacing above identity in the RHS of (
5.36
)
and simultaneously assuming a multivariate Gaussian conjugate prior for
{\Lambda}_{(c)}
, that is
{{\Lambda}}_{(c)}\sim{\cal{N}}_{p}\bigl({\cal{L}}_{0(c)},{\cal{P}}_{0(c)}\bigr)
, it turns out that

\displaystyle{\Lambda}_{(c)}\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\Psi_{(-\Lambda_{0(c)})}\bigr)\sim{\cal{N}}_{p}\bigl({\cal{V}},{\cal{W}}\bigr),

where, for
c=1,\dots,q
, we have

\displaystyle{\cal{V}}

\displaystyle={\cal{W}}\Bigl[{\cal{P}}^{-1}_{0(c)}{\cal{L}}_{0(c)}+\Sigma^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}u_{i(c)}\bm{\xi}_{i}\Bigr],

\displaystyle{\cal{W}}

\displaystyle=\Bigl[{\cal{P}}^{-1}_{0(c)}+\Sigma^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}u^{2}_{i(c)}\Bigr]^{-1}.

After simulating sequence
\{\Lambda_{(1)},\Lambda_{(2)},\ldots,\Lambda_{(q)}\}
, the generated skewness matrix
{\Lambda}
is reconstructed as
{\Lambda}=\bigl[\Lambda_{(1)}\big|\Lambda_{(2)}\big|\ldots\big|\Lambda_{(q)}\bigr]
.

•

full conditionals

{\alpha}\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-{\alpha})}\bigr)
and
{\beta}\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-{\alpha})}\bigr)
:
We have

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\bm{\theta})}\bigr)\propto L_{c}(\bm{\Psi}|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},\bm{g})\pi(\bm{\theta}),

(5.51)

By assuming independence between marginals of
\pi(\bm{\theta})
, it follows from RHS of in (
5.51
) that

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\alpha\big|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\alpha)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\propto\prod_{i=1}^{n}f\bigl(g_{i}\big|\bm{\theta}\bigr)\pi(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta^{n\alpha}\alpha_{0}^{\alpha_{0}}}{[\Gamma(\alpha)]^{n}\Gamma(\alpha_{0})}\bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\bigr)^{\alpha-1}\alpha^{\alpha_{0}-1}\exp\bigl\{-\alpha_{0}\alpha\bigr\}.

(5.52)

It can be easily seen that
\pi\bigl(\alpha\big|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\alpha)}\bigr)
is log-concave for
\alpha_{0}\geq 1
, and hence the ARS approach is suggested for sampling from this full conditional. If one would like to employ the MH approach, one choice for candidate may be
{\cal{G}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{0})
. This yields the the acceptance ratio as

\displaystyle p\bigl(\alpha_{k},\alpha_{k+1}\bigr)=\min\Biggl\{1,\penalty\

\displaystyle\Bigl(\frac{\alpha_{k+1}}{\alpha_{k}}\Bigr)^{\alpha_{0}-1}\Bigl[\frac{\Gamma\bigl(\alpha_{k}\bigr)}{\Gamma\bigl(\alpha_{k+1}\bigr)}\Bigr]^{n}\beta^{n(\alpha_{k+1}-\alpha_{k})}

\displaystyle\times\exp\Bigl\{\alpha_{0}(\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{k+1})\Bigr\}\bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\bigr)^{\alpha_{k+1}-\alpha_{k}}\Biggr\}.

(5.53)

Similarly, assuming
\pi(\beta)\sim{\cal{G}}\bigl(\beta_{0},\beta_{0}\bigr)
. We have

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\beta\big|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\beta)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\propto\prod_{i=1}^{n}f\bigl(g_{i}\big|\bm{\theta}\bigr)\pi(\beta)

\displaystyle\propto\beta^{n\alpha+\beta_{0}-1}\exp\Bigl\{-\beta\bigl(\beta_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\bigr)\Bigr\}.

(5.54)

From the RHS of (
5.54
), it turns out that

\beta\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\beta)}\bigr)\sim{\cal{G}}\bigl(\beta_{0}+n\alpha,\beta_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\bigr)
.

In what follows, Algorithm
14
describes how to compute the Bayesian estimator for parameters of
{\text{SGG}}_{p,q}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,{\Lambda},\bm{\theta})
.

Algorithm 14

Bayesian inference for SGG

1:

Read
N
,
M
, and determine quantities
{\cal{M}}_{0}
,
{\cal{S}}_{0}
,
{\cal{D}}_{0}
,
\nu_{0}
,
{\cal{L}}_{0}
,
{\cal{P}}_{0}
,
a_{0}
, and

2:

\bm{\theta}_{0}=(a_{0},b_{0})^{\top}
;

3:

Set
t\leftarrow 0
;

4:

Set
\bm{\mu}^{(0)}\leftarrow{\cal{M}}^{0}
,
\Sigma^{(0)}\leftarrow 1/n\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}^{(0)}\bigr)\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}^{(0)}\bigr)^{\top}
,
{\nu}^{(0)}=2
,

5:

and set
\bm{\Psi}^{(0)}=\bigl(\bm{\mu}^{(0)},\Sigma^{(0)},{\Lambda}^{(0)},\bm{\theta}^{(0)}\bigr)
;

6:

while

t\leq N

do

7:

Set
i=1
;

8:

while

i\leq n

do

9:

Simulate
g_{i}
from
{\cal{G}}(a,b)
where
a=\alpha^{(t)}+(p+q)/2

and

b=

10:

\Bigl[2\beta^{(t)}+\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}^{(t)}-{\Lambda}^{(t)}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)^{\top}\bigl[\Sigma^{(t)}\bigr]^{-1}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}^{(t)}-{\Lambda}^{(t)}\bm{u}_{i}\bigr)\Bigr]/2+\bm{u}^{\top}_{i}\bm{u}_{i}/2
;

11:

Simulate
\bm{u}_{i}
from
{\cal{HN}}_{q}\bigl(\bm{m}^{(t)}_{i},\frac{{\Delta}^{(t)}}{{g_{i}}}\bigr)
where

\bm{m}^{(t)}_{i}=\bigl[{\Lambda}^{(t)}\bigr]^{\top}\bigl[\Omega^{(t)}\bigr]^{-1}(\bm{y}_{i}-

12:

\bm{\mu})
with
{\Omega}^{(t)}={\Sigma}^{(t)}+{{\Lambda}^{(t)}}\bigl[{{\Lambda}^{(t)}}\bigr]^{\top}
and
{\Delta}^{(t)}=\bm{I}_{q}-\bigl[{{\Lambda}^{(t)}}\bigr]^{\top}\bigl[{\Omega^{(t)}}\bigr]^{-1}{{\Lambda}^{(t)}}
;

13:

Set
i\leftarrow i+1
;

14:

end

15:

Generate
\bm{\mu}^{(t+1)}\sim{\cal{N}}_{p}\Bigl({\cal{Q}}_{0}\Bigl[{\cal{S}}^{-1}_{0}{\cal{M}}_{0}+\bigl[\Sigma^{(t)}\bigr]^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}(\bm{y}_{i}-{\Lambda}^{(t)}\bm{u}_{i})\Bigr],{\cal{Q}}_{0}\Bigr)

16:

where
{\cal{Q}}_{0}=\Bigl[{\cal{S}}^{-1}_{0}+\bigl[\Sigma^{(t)}\bigr]^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\Bigr]^{-1}
;

17:

Generate
\Sigma^{(t+1)}
from

{\cal{IW}}\bigl({\cal{R}}_{0},\nu_{0}+n\bigr)
where
{\cal{R}}_{0}={\cal{D}}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-

18:

\bm{\mu}^{(t+1)}\bigr)\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}^{(t+1)}\bigr)^{\top}
;

19:

Set
j=1
;

20:

while

j\leq q

do

21:

Set
{\cal{W}}^{-1}=\bigl[{\cal{P}}^{-1}_{0(j)}+\bigl[\Sigma^{-1}\bigr]^{(t+1)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}u^{2}_{i(j)}\bigr]
,

{\cal{V}}={\cal{W}}\bigl[{\cal{P}}^{-1}_{0(j)}{\cal{L}}_{0(j)}+

22:

23:

\bigl[\Sigma^{(t+1)}\bigr]^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}u_{i(j)}{\bm{\xi}_{i}}\bigr]
, and
\bm{\xi}_{i}=\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}^{(t+1)}-{{\Lambda}}^{(t)}_{(-j)}\bm{u}_{i(-j)}
;

24:

Generate
{{\Lambda}}_{(j)}\sim{\cal{N}}_{p}\bigl({\cal{V}},{\cal{W}}\bigr)
;

25:

end

26:

Construct
{\Lambda}^{(t+1)}=\bigl[{\Lambda}_{(1)}\big|{\Lambda}_{(2)}\big|\ldots\big|{\Lambda}_{(q)}\bigr]
;

27:

Use the ARS-within-Gibbs sampling technique for simulating

\alpha^{(t+1)}
while

28:

\log\pi\bigl(\alpha\big|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\alpha)}\bigr)\propto n\alpha\log\beta-n\log\Gamma(\alpha)+(\alpha-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log g_{i}+

29:

(\alpha_{0}-1)\log\alpha-\alpha_{0}\alpha

30:

Simulate
\beta^{(t+1)}
from full conditional
\beta\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\beta)}\bigr)\sim{\cal{G}}\bigl(\beta_{0}+

31:

n\alpha^{(t+1)},\beta_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\bigr)
;

32:

end

33:

Set
\bm{\Psi}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\bigl(\bm{\mu}^{(t+1)},\Sigma^{(t+1)},{\Lambda}^{(t+1)},\bm{\theta}^{(t+1)}\bigr)
and
t\leftarrow t+1
;

34:

end

35:

Sequence
\bigl\{\bm{\Psi}^{(M)},\bm{\Psi}^{(M+1)},\cdots,\bm{\Psi}^{(N)}\bigr\}
is a sample of size
N-M+1
from

36:

posterior
\pi(\bm{\Psi}|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{u}},{\bm{g}})
;

37:

The Bayesian estimator of
\bm{\Psi}
is given by
\frac{1}{N-M+1}\sum_{t=1}^{N-M+1}\bm{\Psi}^{(t)}
.

5-37

Herein, we suppose a sample of
n=500
observations are drawn from

\bm{Y}\sim{\text{SGG}}_{2,2}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,\bm{\Lambda},\bm{\theta})
with parameters

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}=\biggl[\begin{matrix}2\\
-2\end{matrix}\biggr],\penalty\ \Sigma=\biggl[\begin{matrix}0.4&0\\
0&0.4\\
\end{matrix}\biggr],\penalty\ \bm{\Lambda}=\biggl[\begin{matrix}-2&2\\
3&0\\
\end{matrix}\biggr],\penalty\ \bm{\theta}=\biggl[\begin{matrix}\alpha\\
\beta\end{matrix}\biggr]=\biggl[\begin{matrix}5\\
5\end{matrix}\biggr].

We follow the steps of Algorithm 7 to obtain Bayesian estimator of
\bm{\Psi}
.

The pertaining
R

R

{\cal{M}}_{0}=(0,0)^{\top}
,

,

,

,
\beta_{0}=2
, and
\alpha_{0}=2
. The output of the Gibbs sampler is displayed in Figure

.

Figure 5.7:
The PDF of
\text{SGG}_{2,2}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma,{\Lambda},\bm{\theta})
. (a): true model and (b): true and fitted contour plots.
