5.5
Gibbs sampling in Bayesian paradigm with more than two latent variables: Model-based Clustering

The aim of clustering technique is to divide a non-homogeneous population into
K
numbers of homogeneous sub-populations. Obviously, members of a non-homogeneous population have dissimilarities while the clustered or partitioned sub-populations have more similarities. Herein, the term “similarity” may refer to some measurable attribute or criterion such as Euclidean distance. If two observations are similar, then they are close together (high degree of similarity) and otherwise, two observations are dissimilar (low degree of similarity).

Figure 5.9:
(a): Ten data points that can constitute a cluster, (b):
Ten data points that should be clustered into two groups, (c):
Ten data points in part (a) that can be modelled through a single-component (homogeneous) model with PDF
f\bigl(\bm{y}\bigl|\bm{\Theta}\bigr)
, and (d): Ten data points in part (b) that can be modelled through a two-component (non-homogeneous) mixture model with PDF
0.4f\bigl(\bm{y}\bigl|\bm{\Theta}_{1}\bigr)+0.6f\bigl(\bm{y}\bigl|\bm{\Theta}_{2}\bigr)
.

Figure
5.9
(a) displays a set of ten observations, labeled with numbers
1,2,\cdots,10
, that are similar and so can constitute a cluster. On the other hand, consider the situation in which ten observations are non-homogeneous or dissimilar as shown by Figure
5.9
(b). Herein, ten observations with labels
\{7,6,1,4\}
are similar together, but quite dissimilar with observations whose label are
\{2,8,1,3,9,5\}
. So, observations in Figure
5.9
(b) should be groped into two clusters. We emphasize that the objective of clustering technique is to determine the label of each observation. Furthermore, sometimes the yardstick for measuring the similarity between observations may not be a quantitative tool such as the Euclidean distance. The clustering technique proceeds to divide observations into
K
groups such that the observations within each group have the maximum degree of similarity while the observations between groups have the maximum degree of dissimilarity. Sometimes the clustering technique is known as semi-supervised or unsupervised machine learning since label of observations are not known and the only available information are observed data
McNicholas (2016)
.

The model-based clustering (or non-hierarchical clustering) uses a finite mixture of multivariate statistical distributions of the same family for the purpose of clustering. Hence, the model-based clustering and finite mixture models are often used interchangeably in the literature. As mentioned above, the aim of clustering is to determine the label or the origin of an observation is coming from. It is worth to note that the concepts “origin”, “cluster”, “label”, and “component” may be used interchangeably. For more clarifications, Figure
5.9
(c) shows a set of ten observations with perfect similarity and, within the model-based clustering framework, we assume that these ten observations follow independently the same multivariate distribution. On the other hand, Figure
5.9
(d) shows another set of ten observations with two clusters, and hence within the model-based clustering framework, we prefer to assume these data points are coming from two different multivariate statistical distribution of the same family. In other words, for the first and second clusters we consider two multivariate statistical distributions of the same family whose PDFs are
f\bigl(\bm{y}\bigl|\bm{\Theta}_{1}\bigr)
and
f\bigl(\bm{y}\bigl|\bm{\Theta}_{2}\bigr)
, respectively. The corresponding weights are 0.4 and 0.6 and hence the distribution of ten observations is a mixture of two distributions fitted to two clusters whose PDF is

\displaystyle g(\bm{y}|\bm{\Psi})=0.4f\bigl(\bm{y}\bigl|\bm{\Theta}_{1}\bigr)+0.6f\bigl(\bm{y}\bigl|\bm{\Theta}_{2}\bigr).

(5.55)

In (
5.55
), the vector
\bm{\omega}=(0.40,0.60)^{\top}
, is known as the weight vector that means 40% of observations in Figure
5.9
(d) are coming from the first component and the remaining 60% are coming from the second component. Overall, for
p
-dimensional random vector
\bm{Y}
, the PDF of a
K
-component mixture model is given by

\displaystyle g(\bm{y}|\bm{\Psi})=\sum_{k=1}^{\text{K}}\omega_{k}f\bigl(\bm{y}\bigl|\bm{\Theta}_{k}\bigr),

(5.56)

where
\bm{\Psi}=(\bm{\omega}^{\top},\bm{\Theta}_{1},\cdots,\bm{\Theta}_{\text{K}})
is the parameter space in which
\bm{\omega}=(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{\text{K}})^{\top}
is vector of mixing parameters such that
\sum_{k=1}^{\text{K}}\omega_{k}=1
. In (
5.56
),
f_{\bm{Y}}(\bm{y}|\bm{\Theta}_{k})
is the PDF of
k
-th component and hence the PDF
g(\bm{y}|\bm{\Psi})
is proper since

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}g(\bm{y}|\bm{\Psi})d\bm{y}=\sum_{k=1}^{\text{K}}\omega_{k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}f\bigl(\bm{y}\bigl|\bm{\Theta}_{k}\bigr)d\bm{y}=\sum_{k=1}^{\text{K}}\omega_{k}=1.

The weight parameter
\bm{\omega}
provides the chance of observing sample from components. Sometimes, representation (
5.56
) is called the
finite mixture model
since the quantity
K
or number of clusters is assumed to be finite. In next two subsections, we proceed to discuss about generating realization from a finite mixture model and how to determine quantity
K
.

5.5.1
Generating from Gaussian finite mixture model

For generating
n
realizations from a
K
-component mixture model, we may follow one of the two following approaches. In the first method,
one can generate independently
n_{k}=\lceil n\times\omega_{k}\rceil
(for
k=1,\cdots,{\text{K}}-1
) realizations from PDF
f\bigl(\bm{y}\big|\bm{\Theta}_{k}\bigr)
in which
\lceil n\times\omega_{k}\rceil
denotes the smallest integer value equal or greater than
n\times\omega_{k}
, and finally
n_{\text{K}}=n-\sum_{k=1}^{{\text{K}}-1}n_{k}
realizations are drawn independently from PDF
f\bigl(\bm{y}\big|\bm{\Theta}_{\text{K}}\bigr)
.
The following pseudo code describes how to draw a sample of size
n
from a
K
-component Gaussian mixture model under the second method.

Algorithm 15

Simulating from
K
-component Gaussian mixture model.

1:

Read
n
,
K
(number of components), and
\bm{\Psi}=(\bm{\omega},\bm{\Theta}_{1},\cdots,\bm{\Theta}_{\text{K}})
;

2:

Set
\bm{Y}
a vector of length
n
;

3:

Set
i=1
;

4:

while

i\leq n

do

5:

Sample
\bm{u}\sim{\cal{MUL}}(1,\bm{\omega})
;

6:

Set
k_{max}=\bigl\{k|\bm{u}[k]=1\bigr\}
where
\bm{u}[k]
is the
k
th element of vector
\bm{u}
for

7:

k=1,\cdots,{\text{K}}
;

8:

Sample
\bm{y}
from PDF
f\bigl(\cdot\bigl|\bm{\Theta}_{k_{max}}\bigr)

9:

Set
\bm{Y}[i]\leftarrow\bm{y}
;

10:

Set
i\leftarrow i+1
;

11:

end

12:

Accept
\bm{Y}
as sample of size
n
from
K
-component mixture model.

5-38

Suppose we are interested in generating a sample of
n=300
observations from
observations are coming from a three-component bivariate Gaussian mixture model with PDF given by

\displaystyle g(\bm{y}|\bm{\Psi})=\frac{1}{3}{\cal{N}}_{2}\bigl(\bm{\mu}_{1},\Sigma_{1}\bigr)+\frac{1}{3}{\cal{N}}_{2}\bigl(\bm{\mu}_{2},\Sigma_{2}\bigr)+\frac{1}{3}{\cal{N}}_{2}\bigl(\bm{\mu}_{3},\Sigma_{3}\bigr),

(5.57)

where
\bm{\Psi}=\bigl(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3},\Theta_{1},\Theta_{2},\Theta_{3}\bigr)
is whole parameter vector whose elements are

\displaystyle\Theta_{1}=\omega_{1}=\frac{1}{3},\bm{\mu}_{1}=(-3,-3)^{\top},\Sigma_{1}=\biggl[\begin{matrix}1&0.5\\
0.5&1\end{matrix}\biggr],

\displaystyle\Theta_{2}=\omega_{2}=\frac{1}{3},\bm{\mu}_{2}=(0,0)^{\top},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Sigma_{2}=\biggl[\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{matrix}\biggr],

\displaystyle\Theta_{3}=\omega_{3}=\frac{1}{3},\bm{\mu}_{3}=(3,3)^{\top},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Sigma_{3}=\biggl[\begin{matrix}1&-0.25\\
-0.25&1\end{matrix}\biggr].

(5.58)

Based on Algorithm
15
, we use the following
R
code to generate from three-component bivariate Gaussian mixture model with whole parameter given as above. Figure
5.10
(a) displays the corresponding scatterplot.

5.5.2
Model selection

The true value of
K
is not known in practice. This quantity can be determined through the Bayesian information criterion (BIC)
(
Schwarz et al., 1978
)
. The process of finding best
K
is known as the
model selection
in the literature. Based on
n
realizations
\bm{y}_{1},\cdots,\bm{y}_{n}
that are assumed to follow independently a distribution with PDF
g(\bm{y}|\bm{\Psi})
, the BIC is defined as

\displaystyle\text{BIC}=2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\sum_{k=1}^{{\text{K}}}\widehat{\omega_{k}}f\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}\big|\widehat{\bm{\Theta}_{k}}\bigr)-N\log n,

(5.59)

where
\widehat{{\omega}_{k}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\Theta}_{k}}
(for
k=1,\cdots,{{\text{K}}}
) account for the ML estimators of
{{\omega}}_{k}
and
{\bm{\Theta}}_{k}
, respectively. Furthermore, constant
N
denotes number of model’s free parameters that must be estimated. The BIC is an effective criterion for model selection in Gaussian mixture models, see
Fraley and Raftery (2002)
;
Mcnicholas and Murphy (2008)
, and has been used frequently for other model-based clustering, see
Keribin (2000)
;
Fraley and Raftery (1998)
;
Fraley and Raftery (2002)
;
Mcnicholas and Murphy (2008)
;
McNicholas and Murphy (2010)
. In practice, for determining
K
, we compute BIC defined by (
5.59
) when data points are clustered through other clustering approaches such as k-mean
(
Hartigan and Wong, 1979
)
or other approaches of clustering, for
k=1,2,\cdots
. We choose the mixture model for which BIC is the largest. For instance, suppose that we do not know the true value of
K
when 300 observations are coming from a three-component bivariate Gaussian mixture model with true parameters given by (
5.58
). As expected, when
{{\text{K}}}=3
the BIC gets its maximum value, see Figure
5.10
(b). The
R
function
BIC
has been developed for determining the true value of
K
for a
K
-component Gaussian mixture model.

Figure 5.10:
(a): Scatterplot of 300 data points drawn from
\bm{Y}=(Y_{1},Y_{2})^{\top}
following a three-component Gaussian mixture model and (b): Computed BIC versus number of clusters.

As noted earlier, determining the label of each observation is the main task of each clustering technique. To this end, we may use the Bayesian (Gibbs sampling) or the expectation-maximization (EM)
(
Dempster et al., 1977
)
algorithm. If the former applied, depending on the model structure, the Gibbs sampling involves one, two, or more latent variables.

5.5.3
Finite mixture of Gaussian distributions

Evidently the most commonly used statistical distribution for the purpose of model-based clustering is Gaussian mixture model. Suppose
p
-dimensional random vector
\bm{Y}
follows a
K
-component Gaussian mixture model. It is clear that

\displaystyle\bm{Y}\big|H_{ik}=1

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{N}}_{p}\bigl(\bm{\mu}_{k},\Sigma_{k}\bigr),

\displaystyle\bm{H}_{i}

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{MUL}}\bigl(\bm{\omega}\big|1\bigr).

Since
\bm{H}_{i}
is latent variable, hence we encounter with a incomplete data problem. Here
\{\bm{y}_{1},\cdots,\bm{y}_{n}\}
is the sequence of observed data and
\bm{H}_{i}=(H_{i1},\cdots,H_{i\text{K}})^{\top}
is the missing component’s label. Herein
{H}_{ik}=1
takes on values zero and one. If
{H}_{ik}=1
, then
\bm{y}_{i}
comes from
k
-th component, otherwise
H_{i1}=0
. Hence, the complete data PDF can be represented as

\displaystyle g_{c}({\underline{\bm{y}}}|\bm{\Psi})=\prod^{n}_{i=1}g\bigl(\bm{y}_{i},\bm{H}_{i}\big|\bm{\Psi}\bigr),

(5.60)

where
{\underline{\bm{y}}}=\{\bm{y}_{1},\cdots,\bm{y}_{n}\}
and

\displaystyle g\bigl(\bm{y}_{i},\bm{H}_{i}\big|\bm{\Psi}\bigr)

\displaystyle=g\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}\big|\bm{H}_{i},\bm{\Psi}\bigr)\times{\cal{MUL}}\bigl(\bm{\omega}\big|1\bigr)

\displaystyle=\biggl[{\cal{N}}_{p}\Bigl(\bm{y}_{i}\Big|\bm{\mu}_{k},\Sigma_{k}\Bigr)\biggr]^{H_{ik}}\times\omega_{1}^{H_{i1}}\times\cdots\times\omega_{{\text{K}}}^{H_{i{\text{K}}}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{{\text{K}}}\biggl[\omega_{k}{\cal{N}}_{p}\Bigl(\bm{y}_{i}\Big|\bm{\mu}_{k},\Sigma_{k}\Bigr)\biggr]^{H_{ik}}.

(5.61)

Replacing the RHS of (
5.61
) into the RHS of (
5.60
), the PDF of complete-data is

\displaystyle g_{c}({\underline{\bm{y}}}|\bm{\Psi})=\prod^{n}_{i=1}\prod_{k=1}^{{\text{K}}}\biggl[\omega_{k}{\cal{N}}_{p}\Bigl(\bm{y}_{i}\Big|\bm{\mu}_{k},\Sigma_{k}\Bigr)\biggr]^{H_{ik}}.

(5.62)

Thus, the complete-data likelihood function is

\displaystyle L_{c}(\bm{\Psi}|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{H}})=

\displaystyle(2\pi)^{-\frac{np}{2}}\times\omega_{k}^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{ik}}\times\cdots\times\omega_{{\text{K}}}^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{i{\text{K}}}}\times\big|\Sigma_{k}\big|^{-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{{\text{K}}}H_{ik}}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{{\text{K}}}H_{ik}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{k}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}_{k}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{k}\bigr)\Bigr\},

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle\omega_{1}^{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\omega_{{\text{K}}}^{n_{\text{K}}}\times\big|\Sigma_{1}\big|^{-\frac{n_{1}}{2}}\times\cdots\times\big|\Sigma_{{\text{K}}}\big|^{-\frac{n_{{\text{K}}}}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{{\text{K}}}H_{ik}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{k}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}_{k}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{k}\bigr)\Bigr\},

(5.63)

where
\underline{\bm{H}}=\{\bm{H}_{1},\cdots,\bm{H}_{n}\}
in which
\bm{H}_{i}=(H_{i1},\cdots,H_{i\text{K}})^{\top}
and
n_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{ik}
provided that
n=\sum_{k=1}^{{\text{K}}}n_{k}
. Given
i
th observed data
\bm{y}_{i}
, for computing PDF of
\bm{H}_{i}|\bm{Y}=\bm{y}_{i}
, it follows from (
5.61
) that

\displaystyle L_{c}\bigl(\bm{\Psi}\big|{\bm{y}}_{i},{\bm{H}}_{i}\bigr)\propto

\displaystyle g\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}\big|\bm{H}_{i},\bm{\Psi}\bigr)\times{\cal{MUL}}\bigl(\bm{\omega}\big|1\bigr).

Obviously,

\displaystyle\bm{H}_{i}\big|\bm{\Psi},\bm{Y}=\bm{y}_{i}\sim{\cal{MUL}}\bigl(\bm{\tau}_{i}\big|1\bigr),

(5.64)

where
\bm{\tau}_{i}=(\tau_{i1},\cdots,\tau_{i\text{K}})^{\top}
denotes the
i
th row of
n\times{\text{K}}
matrix
\bm{\tau}
whose
(i,k)
th element is

\displaystyle{\tau}_{i,k}=\frac{\omega_{k}{\cal{N}}_{p}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}\big|\bm{\mu}_{k},\Sigma_{k}\bigr)}{\sum_{k=1}^{\text{K}}\omega_{k}{\cal{N}}_{p}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}\big|\bm{\mu}_{k},\Sigma_{k}\bigr)}.

(5.65)

Once we have generated the entire sample (for
i=1,\cdots,n
) from full conditional
\bm{H}_{i}\big|\bigl(\bm{\Psi},{\bm{y}}_{i}\bigr)
, we proceed to generate from full conditionals

\bm{\mu}_{k}\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{H}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\bm{\mu}_{k})}\bigr)
and

\Sigma_{k}\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{H}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-{\Sigma}_{k})}\bigr)
(for
k=1,\cdots,{\text{K}}
). To this end, first we have

\displaystyle\pi(\bm{\Psi}|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{H}})

\displaystyle\propto L_{c}(\bm{\Psi}|\underline{\bm{y}},\underline{\bm{H}})\times\pi\bigl(\bm{\Psi}_{1}\bigr)\times\cdots\times\pi\bigl(\bm{\Psi}_{{\text{K}}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=L_{c}(\bm{\Psi}|\underline{\bm{y}},\underline{\bm{H}})\times\pi\bigl(\bm{\omega}\bigr)\times\pi\bigl(\bm{\mu}_{1}\bigr)\times\cdots\times\pi\bigl(\bm{\mu}_{{\text{K}}}\bigr)\times\pi\bigl(\Sigma_{1}\bigr)\times\cdots\times\pi\bigl(\Sigma_{{\text{K}}}\bigr).

(5.66)

Substituting
L_{c}(\bm{\Psi}|\underline{\bm{y}},\underline{\bm{H}})
given by (
5.63
) into the RHSS of (
5.66
) yields

\displaystyle\pi(\bm{\Psi}|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{H}})\propto

\displaystyle\times\pi\bigl(\bm{\omega}\bigr)\times\pi\bigl(\bm{\mu}_{1}\bigr)\times\cdots\times\pi\bigl(\bm{\mu}_{{\text{K}}}\bigr)\times\pi\bigl(\Sigma_{1}\bigr)\times\cdots\times\pi\bigl(\Sigma_{{\text{K}}}\bigr)\times

\displaystyle\omega_{1}^{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\omega_{{\text{K}}}^{n_{\text{K}}}\times\big|\Sigma_{1}\big|^{-\frac{n_{1}}{2}}\times\cdots\times\big|\Sigma_{{\text{K}}}\big|^{-\frac{n_{{\text{K}}}}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{{\text{K}}}H_{ik}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{k}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}_{k}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{k}\bigr)\Bigr\}.

(5.67)

We consider, for
k=1,\cdots,{\text{K}}
, independent conjugate priors as follows.

\displaystyle\pi(\bm{\omega})

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{DIR}}\bigl(\bm{\rho}\bigr),

(5.68)

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\bm{\mu}_{k}\bigr)

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{N}}_{p}\bigl({\cal{M}}_{k},{\cal{S}}_{k}\bigr),

(5.69)

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\Sigma_{k}\bigr)

\displaystyle\sim{\cal{IW}}\bigl({\cal{D}}_{k},\nu_{k}\bigr),

(5.70)

where
{\cal{DIR}}\bigl(\cdot\bigr)
is defined in (
). In what follows, we give the full conditionals needed for implementing the Gibbs sampling.

•

full conditional of
\bm{\omega}\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{H}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\bm{\omega})}\bigr)
:
Substituting prior
\pi(\bm{\omega})
given by (
5.67
) into the RHS of (
5.66
) yields

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\bm{\omega}\big|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{H}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\bm{\mu})}\bigr)

\displaystyle\propto{\cal{DIR}}\bigl(\bm{\omega}\big|\bm{\rho}\bigr)\times\omega_{1}^{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\omega_{{\text{K}}}^{n_{\text{K}}}={\cal{DIR}}\bigl(\bm{\omega}\big|\bm{\rho}_{*}\bigr),

where

\displaystyle\bm{\rho}_{*}=\Bigl(\rho_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{i1},\cdots,\rho_{{\text{K}}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{i{\text{K}}}\Bigr)^{\top}.

•

full conditional of
\bm{\mu}_{k}\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{H}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\bm{\mu}_{k})}\bigr)
:
Substitute the prior
\pi\bigl(\bm{\mu}_{k}\bigr)
given by (
5.68
) into the RHS of (
5.66
). Then more algebra shows

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\bm{\mu}\big|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{H}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\bm{\mu}_{k})}\bigr)={\cal{N}}_{p}\Bigl(\bm{\mu}\Big|{\cal{Q}}_{k}\Bigl[{\cal{S}}^{-1}_{k}{\cal{M}}_{k}+\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{ik}\bm{y}_{i}\Bigr],{\cal{Q}}_{k}\Bigr),

where

\displaystyle{\cal{Q}}_{k}=\Bigl[{\cal{S}}^{-1}_{k}+\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\times\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{ik}\Bigr]^{-1}.

•

full conditional of

{\Sigma}_{k}\big|\bigl({\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{H}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-\Sigma_{k})}\bigr)
:
Substitute the prior
\pi\bigl({\Sigma}_{k}\bigr)
given by (
5.69
) into the RHS of (
5.66
). Then more algebra shows

\displaystyle\pi\bigl({\Sigma}\big|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{H}},\bm{\Psi}_{(-{\Sigma}_{k})}\bigr)\sim{\cal{IW}}\bigl({\cal{R}}_{k},\nu_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{ik}\bigr),

where
{\cal{R}}_{k}={\cal{D}}_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{ik}\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{k}\bigr)\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{k}\bigr)^{\top}
.

The following pseudo code gives the details for implementing the model-based clustering for finite mixture of Gaussian distributions.

Algorithm 16

Bayesian inference for finite mixture of Gaussian distributions

1:

Read
N
,
M
, and determine quantities
K
,
{\omega}_{k}
,
{\cal{M}}_{k}
,
{\cal{S}}_{k}
,
{\cal{D}}_{k}
,
\nu_{k}
, for
k=1\cdots,{\text{K}}

2:

Set
t=0
;

3:

Determine initial values
\bm{\omega}^{(0)}
,
\bm{\mu}^{(0)}
,
\Sigma^{(0)}
, and set
\bm{\Psi}^{(0)}=\bigl(\bm{\omega}^{(0)}
,
\bm{\mu}^{(0)},\Sigma^{(0)}\bigr)
;

4:

while

t\leq N

do

\triangleright
start Gibbs sampling

5:

Set
i=1
;

6:

while

i\leq n

do

\triangleright
start for sampling form missing labels

7:

Simulate the
i
row of
n\times{\text{K}}
matrix
\bm{H}
as
\bm{H}_{i}\sim{\cal{MUL}}\bigl(\cdot\big|\bm{\tau}_{i}\bigr)
where

8:

\bm{\tau}_{i}=(\tau_{i1},\ldots,\tau_{i\text{K}})^{\top}
is the
i
th row of matrix
\bm{\tau}
is given by (
5.65
);

9:

Set
i=i+1
;

10:

end

11:

Set, for
k=1,\cdots,{\text{K}}
,
n_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{i,k}
where
H_{i,k}
denotes the
(i,k)
th

12:

element of
\bm{H}
;

13:

Simulate
\bm{\omega}\sim{\cal{DIR}}\bigl(1|\rho_{*}\bigr)
where
\rho_{*}=(\rho_{1}+n_{1},\cdots,\rho_{{\text{K}}}+n_{{\text{K}}})^{\top}
;

14:

Set
k=1
;

15:

while

k\leq K

do

\triangleright
start sampling from full conditionals of
\bm{\mu}_{k}
, and
\Sigma_{k}

16:

Generate
\bm{\mu}_{k}^{(t+1)}\sim{\cal{N}}_{p}\Bigl({\cal{Q}}_{k}\Bigl[{\cal{S}}^{-1}_{k}{\cal{M}}_{k}+\bigl[\Sigma_{k}^{(t)}\bigr]^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{i,k}\times\bm{y}_{i}\Bigr],{\cal{Q}}_{k}\Bigr)

17:

where
{\cal{Q}}_{0}=\Bigl[{\cal{S}}^{-1}_{0}+\bigl[\Sigma_{k}^{(t)}\bigr]^{-1}\times n_{k}\Bigr]^{-1}
;

18:

Generate
\Sigma_{k}^{(t+1)}
from

{\cal{IW}}\bigl({\cal{R}}_{k},\nu_{k}+n_{k}\bigr)
where
{\cal{R}}_{k}={\cal{D}}_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}H_{i,k}\times

19:

\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{k}^{(t+1)}\bigr)\bigl(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\mu}_{k}^{(t+1)}\bigr)^{\top}
;

20:

Set
k=k+1
;

21:

end

22:

Set
\bm{\Psi}^{(t+1)}=\bigl(\bm{\omega}^{(t+1)},\bm{\mu}_{1}^{(t+1)}\cdots,\bm{\mu}_{{\text{K}}}^{(t+1)},\Sigma_{1}^{(t+1)},\cdots,\Sigma_{{\text{K}}}^{(t+1)}\bigr)
and
t=t+1
;

23:

end

24:

Sequence
\bigl\{\bm{\Psi}^{(M+1)},\cdots,\bm{\Psi}^{(N)}\bigr\}
is a sample of size
N-M
from

25:

posterior
\pi\bigl(\bm{\Psi}|{\underline{\bm{y}}},\underline{\bm{H}}\bigr)
;

26:

The Bayesian estimator of
\bm{\Psi}
is given by
\frac{1}{N-M}\sum_{t=1}^{N-M}\bm{\Psi}^{(t)}
.

While Algorithm
16
works reasonably well, its performance is subject to outliers. If clusters have large variances, then the Gibbs sampling may fail to allocate true label to each cluster. This mainly because this fact that Gaussian distribution has light tails and the PDF decays exponentially for observation are far from the center of distribution. For instance, consider a two-component bivariate mixture model with equal weights
\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}=0.5
while the second cluster has large locations and variances. It is easy to check that the elements of second column of matrix
\bm{\tau}
in (
5.65
) are very small and hence,
P(H_{i,2}=1)\rightarrow 1
for
i=1,\cdots,n
. Hence, all observations are allocated to the first cluster. One way to tackle this issue is standardization of observations before clustering
(
Tortora et al., 2021
)
. The following example gives more detailed information about this case.

5-39

Herein, we are interested in clustering the famous Old Faithful Geyser Data (known as faithful data). This faithful data consists of 272 observations on two variables
duration of the eruption
in minutes and
waiting time between eruptions
for the Old Faithful geyser in Yellowstone National Park, Wyoming, USA,
(
Azzalini and Bowman, 1990
)
. Figure
5.11
(a) displays the scatterplot of this dataset. Our investigation shows that the BIC of -833.7747 suggests that
{\text{K}}=2
for which the k-mean algorithm pre-clustering results shows that the variances of second variable in both clusters are significantly large as 31.66 and 34.75. Using the
R
code after standardization given by

R> data(faithful)
R> Y <- matrix( cbind(faithful$eruptions, faithful$waiting),
+Ψ nrow = 272, ncol = 2)
R> Z <- apply( Y, 2, function(x)( x - mean(x) )/sd(x) )
R> bic <- BIC(Z)
R> out_kmeans <- kmeans(Z, bic$K)
R> label <- out_kmeans$cluster
R> sapply(1:K, function(x) var( Z[label == x, ] ))

then, it can be seen that variances of second variable in both clusters are 0.1876 and 0.1874, correspondingly. The R package
mclust

(
Scrucca et al., 2016
)
applies the EM algorithm for model-based clustering based on the finite mixture of Gaussian distributions. We notice that the clustering results based on both the Gibbs sampling proposed here and EM algorithm proposed in
(
Scrucca et al., 2016
)
are the same. The most commonly used criterion for measuring the degree of agreement between clustering approaches is the adjusted Rand index (ARI) that varies from 0 (for zero agreement in clustering) to 1 (for perfect agreement in clustering)
(
Hubert and Arabie, 1985
)
. Herein, we use the ARI to compare the Bayesian paradigm and EM algorithm for clustering the faithful data. To this end, among several
R
packages that can compute the ARI, we call the package
MixGHD
. Fortunately, the agreement between two above approaches in clustering faithful data is perfect, that is ARI=1. Figure
5.11
(b) and (c) display the contour and scatterplot fitted to standardized faithful data. The
R
function
FGM
given by the following can be used for applying the model-based clustering to the Gaussian finite mixture model.

⬇

5.6
Simulating from truncated Gaussian distribution

A mentioned in Section
3.9
, the truncated distribution is widely used in practice. Generating from truncated multivariate Gaussian distribution may arise in various contexts sch as probit regression
(
Albert and Chib, 1993
)
, likelihood estimation for max-stable processes
(
Genton et al., 2011
)
, fitting mixed effects models with censored data
(
Grün and Hornik, 2012
)
, model-based clustering
(
Maleki et al., 2019
)
, regression analysis
(
Rodriguez-Yam et al., 2004
)
, logistic regression with covariates subject to measurement error and left censoring
(
Teimouri and Sinha, 2024
)
. For more detailed information on application of truncated multivariate Gaussian distribution, we the refer to
Botev (2017)
and references therein. Section
3.9
gives the first and second moments of a Gaussian distribution under rectangular constraint. Suppose
p
-dimensional random vector
\bm{X}=(X_{1},\cdots,X_{p})^{\top}
follows a truncated Gaussian distribution on region with positive Lebesgue measure
\bm{R}
denoted as

\displaystyle{\cal{TN}}_{\bm{R}}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma),\penalty\ \penalty\ \bm{R}=\bigl\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\big|\bm{a}\leq A\bm{x}\leq\bm{b}\bigr\},

(5.71)

where
\bm{a}=(a_{1},\cdots,a_{p})^{\top}
and
\bm{b}=(b_{1},\cdots,b_{p})^{\top}
are vectors of finite or infinite constants. Moreover, matrix
A
is a
p\times p
full rank matrix that constructs a set of
p
independent linear constraints. One can investigate easily, as pointed out by
(
Geweke, 1991
)
, that the marginals of
\bm{X}
with distribution given by (
5.71
) are not truncated Gaussian, but the full conditionals, that is distribution of
X_{i}
(for
i=1,\cdots,p
)
conditional
on all of other members of
\bm{X}
are truncated Gaussian. To verify this claim, we shall confine ourselves here to suppose
p=2
and
A=\bm{I}_{p}
in (
5.71
). This means that

\displaystyle{\cal{TN}}_{\bm{R}}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma),\penalty\ \penalty\ \bm{R}=\bigl\{\bm{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\big|a_{1}\leq x_{1}\leq b_{1},a_{2}\leq x_{2}\leq b_{2}\bigr\},

(5.72)

where
\bm{\mu}=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})^{\top}
and
\Sigma=\bigl[(\sigma_{11},\sigma_{12})^{\top},(\sigma_{21},\sigma_{22})^{\top}\bigr]
. For computing the marginal distribution of (
5.72
), e.g.
X_{1}
, whose PDF is denoted by
{\cal{TN}}_{R_{1}}(x_{1}|\bm{\mu},\Sigma)
, we have

\displaystyle{\cal{TN}}_{R_{1}}(x_{1}|\bm{\mu},\Sigma)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\cal{P}}_{G}}\int_{a_{2}}^{b_{2}}\bm{\phi}_{2}\bigl(\bm{x}|\bm{\mu},{\Sigma}\bigr)dx_{2},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\cal{P}}_{G}}{\phi}\bigl(x_{1}\big|\mu_{1},\sigma_{11}\bigr)\int_{a_{2}}^{b_{2}}{\phi}\bigl(x_{2}\big|{\mu}_{2}^{*},\sigma_{22}^{*}\bigr)dx_{2},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\cal{P}}_{G}}{\phi}\bigl(x_{1}\big|\mu_{1},\sigma_{11}\bigr)\Bigl[\Phi\bigl(b_{2}\big|{\mu}_{2}^{*},\sigma_{22}^{*}\bigr)-\Phi\bigl(a_{2}\big|{\mu}_{2}^{*},\sigma_{22}^{*}\bigr)\Bigr],

(5.73)

where
x_{1}\in R_{1}
,

{\mu}_{2}^{*}={\mu}_{2}+\sigma_{12}\sigma_{11}^{-1}(x_{1}-{\mu}_{1})
,
\sigma_{22}^{*}=\sigma_{22}-\sigma_{12}\sigma_{11}^{-1}\sigma_{21}
, and
{\cal{P}}_{G}=\bm{\Phi}_{2}\bigl(\bm{b}\big|\bm{\mu},{\Sigma}\bigr)-\bm{\Phi}_{2}\bigl(\bm{a}\big|\bm{\mu},{\Sigma}\bigr)
. Obviously, since both terms within the square bracket in the RHS of (
5.73
) depend on
x_{1}
through
{\mu}_{2}^{*}
, hence
{\cal{TN}}_{R_{1}}(x_{1}|\bm{\mu},\Sigma)
is no longer the PDF of a Gaussian distribution unless
{\mu}_{2}^{*}
is independent of
x_{1}
that occurs only when
\sigma_{12}=\sigma_{21}=0
. This is while both of the full conditionals
X_{1}|X_{2}
and
X_{2}|X_{1}
correspond to the joint PDF in (
5.72
) are Gaussian. For example,
X_{1}|X_{2}\sim{\cal{TN}}_{R_{1}}\bigl({\mu}_{1}^{*},\sigma_{11}^{*}\bigr)
where
R_{1}=\bigl\{x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}\big|a_{1}\leq x_{1}\leq b_{1}\bigr\}
,
{\mu}_{1}^{*}={\mu}_{1}+\sigma_{12}\sigma_{22}^{-1}(x_{2}-{\mu}_{2})
,
\sigma_{11}^{*}=\sigma_{11}-\sigma_{12}\sigma_{22}^{-1}\sigma_{21}
, and
a_{2}\leq x_{2}\leq b_{2}
. The following proposition generalizes the fact described above for higher dimensions.

Proposition 5.6.1
.

Rodriguez-Yam et al. (2004)

Suppose
\bm{X}\sim{\cal{TN}}_{\bm{R}}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma)
as defined in (
5.71
). Furthermore
\bm{Y}=\bm{c}+B\bm{X}
where
\bm{c}=(c_{1},\cdots,c_{p})^{\top}
is a vector of finite constants and
B
is a
p\times p
full rank matrix. Then,

i.

\bm{Y}\sim{\cal{TN}}_{\bm{T}}(\bm{c}+B\bm{\mu},B\Sigma B^{\top}),\penalty\ \penalty\ \bm{T}=\bigl\{\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\big|\bm{c}+BA^{-1}\bm{a}\leq\bm{y}\leq\bm{c}+BA^{-1}\bm{b}\bigr\}
.

ii.

For a given partition of
\bm{X}
,
\bm{\mu}
, and
\Sigma
given by

\displaystyle\bm{X}=\left[\begin{matrix}\bm{X}_{1}\\
X_{p}\end{matrix}\right],\bm{\mu}=\left[\begin{matrix}\bm{\mu}_{1}\\
\mu_{p}\end{matrix}\right],\Sigma=\left[\begin{matrix}\Sigma_{11}&\Sigma_{1}\\
\Sigma^{\top}_{1}&\sigma_{pp}\\
\end{matrix}\right],

we have

\displaystyle X_{p}\big|\bm{X}_{1}=\bm{x}_{1}\sim{\cal{TN}}_{R_{p}}\bigl({\mu}_{p}^{*},\sigma_{pp}^{*}\bigr),

(5.74)

where

\displaystyle{\mu}_{p}^{*}=

\displaystyle{\mu}_{p}+\Sigma^{\top}_{1}\Sigma_{11}^{-1}\bigl(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{\mu}_{1}\bigr),

(5.75)

\displaystyle\sigma_{pp}^{*}=

\displaystyle\sigma_{pp}-\Sigma^{\top}_{1}\Sigma_{11}^{-1}\Sigma_{1},

(5.76)

\displaystyle R_{p}=

\displaystyle\bigl\{x_{p}\in\mathbb{R}\big|\bigl(\bm{x}_{1},x_{p}\bigr)\in\bm{R}\bigr\}.

In what follows, we review two algorithms for generating from truncated multivariate Gaussian distribution on a set of linearly independent constraints using the Gibbs sampling. The first algorithm is due to
Geweke (1991)
. For a given covariance matrix
\Sigma
and a
p\times p
full rank matrix
A
, suppose
\bm{Z}\sim{\cal{TN}}_{\bm{T}}(\bm{0},A\Sigma A^{\top})
follows a truncated Gaussian distribution on region
\bm{T}=\bigl\{\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\big|\bm{\alpha}\leq\bm{z}\leq\bm{\beta}\bigr\}
with positive Lebesgue measure in which

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha}=

\displaystyle\bm{a}-A\bm{\mu},

(5.77)

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}=

\displaystyle\bm{b}-A\bm{\mu},

(5.78)

are vectors of finite or infinite constants. It follows from Proposition (
5.6.1
) (i) that
\bm{X}=\bm{\mu}+A^{-1}\bm{Z}\sim{\cal{TN}}_{\bm{R}}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma)
where
\bm{R}=\bigl\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\big|\bm{a}\leq A\bm{x}\leq\bm{b}\bigr\}
as (
5.71
).
Let
\bm{z}_{-i}
denote the realization associated with
\bm{Z}_{-i}=\bigl({Z}_{1},\cdots,{Z}_{i-1},{Z}_{i+1},\cdots,{Z}_{p}\bigr)^{\top}
. It follows from Proposition (
5.6.1
) (ii) that

\displaystyle{Z}_{i}\big|\bigl(\bm{Z}_{-i}=\bm{z}_{-i}\bigr)\sim{\cal{TN}}_{T_{i}}\bigl({\mu}_{i}^{*},{\sigma}_{ii}^{*}\bigr),\penalty\ \penalty\ T_{i}=\bigl\{z_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\big|c_{i}\leq z_{i}\leq d_{i}\bigr\},

(5.79)

where elements of vectors
\bm{c}=\bigl(c_{1},\cdots,c_{p}\bigr)^{\top}
and
\bm{d}=\bigl(d_{1},\cdots,d_{p}\bigr)^{\top}
are given by (
5.77
) and (
5.78
), respectively. Furthermore, based on (
5.75
) and (
5.76
), for
i=1,\cdots,p
, we have

\displaystyle{\mu}_{i}^{*}=

\displaystyle V^{\top}_{1}V_{11}^{-1}\bm{z}_{-i},

(5.80)

\displaystyle{\sigma}_{ii}^{*}=

\displaystyle V_{ii}-V^{\top}_{1}V_{11}^{-1}V_{1},

(5.81)

in which
V=A\Sigma A^{\top}
. Algorithm
17
describes the method proposed by
Geweke (1991)
for simulating from truncated Gaussian distribution.

Algorithm 17

Simulating truncated Gaussian distribution: Geweke’s method

1:

Read
\bm{a}
,
\bm{b}
,
\bm{\mu},\Sigma,A
given by (
5.71
), and
N
for number of Gibbs sampler runs;

2:

Set
t=0
and suggest the initial vector
\bm{z}^{(t)}=\bigl(z_{1}^{(t)},\cdots,z_{p}^{(t)}\bigr)^{\top}\in\bm{T}
where
{T}_{i}
(for
i=1,\cdots,p
) is given by (
5.79
);

3:

while

t<N

do

4:

set
i=1
;

5:

while

i\leq p

do

6:

Draw
{z}_{i}^{(t+1)}
form full conditional
{Z}_{i}^{(t+1)}\big|\bigl({Z}_{1}^{(t+1)}={z}_{1}^{(t+1)},\cdots,{Z}_{i-1}^{(t+1)}={z}_{i-1}^{(t+1)},{Z}_{i+1}^{(t)}={z}_{i+1}^{(t)},\cdots,{Z}_{p}^{(t)}={z}_{p}^{(t)}\bigr)
that follows a truncated Gaussian distribution with PDF given by (
5.79
);

7:

set
i=i+1
;

8:

end

9:

\bm{z}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\bm{z}^{(t)}
;

10:

Accept
\bm{x}^{(t+1)}=\bm{\mu}+A^{-1}\bm{z}^{(t+1)}
as a sample of from distribution in (
5.71
);

11:

set
t=t+1
;

12:

end

The second algorithm is due to
Rodriguez-Yam et al. (2004)
. Let
p
-dimensional random vector
\bm{X}=(X_{1},\cdots,X_{p})^{\top}
follows a truncated Gaussian distribution on region
\bm{R}
denoted as

\displaystyle{\cal{TN}}_{\bm{R}}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma),\penalty\ \penalty\ \bm{R}=\bigl\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\big|C\bm{x}\leq\bm{b}\bigr\},

(5.82)

where rows of matrix
B
are not restricted to be linearly independent. If
D
is a square matrix of full rank for which we have
D\Sigma D^{\top}=\bm{I}_{p}
, then for transformation
\bm{Z}=D\bm{X}
it follows from Proposition
5.6.1
(i) that

\displaystyle\bm{Z}\sim{\cal{TN}}_{\bm{T}}\bigl(D\bm{\mu},\bm{I}_{p}\bigr),\penalty\ \penalty\ \bm{T}=\bigl\{\bm{z}=D\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\big|E\bm{z}\leq\bm{b}\bigr\},

(5.83)

where

\displaystyle E=CD^{-1}.

(5.84)

It is not hard to see that the only choice for
D
is inverse of the lower-triangular Cholesky decomposition of
\Sigma
. Using the transformation
\bm{Z}=D\bm{X}
avoids computing
{\mu}_{i}^{*}
and
{\sigma}_{ii}^{*}
given in (
5.79
) when applying Algorithm
17
proposed by
Geweke (1991)
. Suppose
\bm{\alpha}=D\bm{\mu}
,
\bm{Z}_{-i}=\bigl({Z}_{1},\cdots,{Z}_{i-1},{Z}_{i+1},\cdots,{Z}_{p}\bigr)^{\top}
, and
\bm{z}_{-i}
denotes the realization of
\bm{Z}_{-i}
. Based on Proposition
5.6.1
(ii), we have

\displaystyle Z_{i}\big|\bigl(\bm{Z}_{-i}=\bm{z}_{-i}\bigr)\sim{\cal{TN}}_{T_{i}}\bigl(\alpha_{i},1\bigr),\penalty\ \penalty\ T_{i}=\bigl\{z_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\big|E\bm{z}\leq\bm{b}\bigr\},

(5.85)

where
\alpha_{i}
is the
i
th element of vector
\bm{\alpha}
. Let
E_{-i}
denote the matrix
E
given in (
5.84
) when its
i
-th column is removed and
\bm{e}_{i}
is the
i
-th column of matrix
E
. Then, the region
T_{i}
given in (
5.85
) can be represented as

\displaystyle T_{i}=\bigl\{z_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\big|\bm{e}_{i}z_{i}\leq\bm{b}-{E}_{-i}\bm{z}_{-i}\bigr\}.

(5.86)

As pointed out by
Rodriguez-Yam et al. (2004)
, since constraints on
\bm{X}
construct a convex region in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
, hence the region
T_{i}
given by (
5.86
) takes the forms
l_{i}\leq z_{i}\leq u_{i}
,
-\infty\leq z_{i}\leq u_{i}
, or
l_{i}\leq u_{i}\leq\infty
in which
l_{i}
and
u_{i}
are, accordingly, the lower and upper bounds of inequalities obtained from (
5.86
). Algorithm
18
describes how to implement the method proposed by
Rodriguez-Yam et al. (2004)
for simulating from a truncated Gaussian distribution.

Algorithm 18

Simulating truncated Gaussian distribution: Rodriguez-Yam et al. method

1:

Read
\bm{b}
,
\bm{\mu}
,
\Sigma
,
C
given by (
5.82
), and
N
for number of Gibbs sampler runs;

2:

Compute
\bm{\alpha}=D\bm{\mu}
and
E=CD^{-1}
in which
D
is the Cholesky decomposition of
\Sigma
;

3:

Set
t=0
and suggest the initial value
\bm{z}^{(t)}=\bigl(z_{1}^{(t)},\cdots,z_{p}^{(t)}\bigr)^{\top}\in\bm{T}
where
\bm{T}
is given by (
5.86
);

4:

while

t\leq N

do

5:

set
i=1
;

6:

while

i<p

do

7:

Draw
{z}_{i}^{(t+1)}
form full conditional
{Z}_{i}^{(t+1)}\big|\bigl({Z}_{1}^{(t+1)}={z}_{1}^{(t+1)},\cdots,{Z}_{i-1}^{(t+1)}={z}_{i-1}^{(t+1)},{Z}_{i+1}^{(t)}={z}_{i+1}^{(t)},\cdots,{Z}_{p}^{(t)}={z}_{p}^{(t)}\bigr)
that follows a truncated Gaussian distribution with PDF given by (
5.85
);

8:

set
i=i+1
;

9:

end

10:

\bm{Z}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\bm{Z}^{(t)}
;

11:

\bm{X}^{(t+1)}=D^{-1}\bm{Z}^{(t+1)}

12:

set
t=t+1
;

13:

end

In what follows, we are willing to review an example given by
(
Rodriguez-Yam et al., 2004
)
for simulating from a bivariate truncated Gaussian distribution. It is worthwhile to note that the methods proposed by
Geweke (1991)
and
(
Rodriguez-Yam et al., 2004
)
hereafter are denoted as Method 1 and Method 2, respectively.

5-40

Suppose we are interested in simulating from
\bm{X}=(X_{1},X_{2})^{\top}\sim{\cal{TN}}_{\bm{R}}(\bm{\mu},\Sigma)
where
\bm{R}=\bigl\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\big|\bm{x}\geq\bm{0}\bigr\}
,
\bm{\mu}=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})^{\top}
and
\Sigma=\bigl[(1,0.8)^{\top},(0.8,1)^{\top}\bigr]
. We discuss the key features for implementing Algorithm
17
and Algorithm
18
as follows.

•

Method 1
: For implementing the first step of Algorithm
17
, we set

,
\bm{a}=(0,0)^{\top}
,
\bm{b}=(+\infty,+\infty)^{\top}
, and
N=2000
. The vector of initial values in the second step can be
\bm{z}^{(0)}=(1,1)^{\top}
and further, using
\bm{\mu}=(0,0)^{\top}
, it follows from (
5.77
) and (
5.78
) that
T_{1}=T_{2}=[0,\infty)
. Based on (
5.75
) and (
5.76
), the full conditionals in the sixth step of Algorithm
17
are given as follows.

\displaystyle Z_{1}\big|\bigl({\bm{Z}}_{-1}={\bm{z}}_{-1}\bigr)\sim{\cal{TN}}_{T_{1}}\Bigl(\frac{4}{5}z_{2},\frac{9}{25}\Bigr),

\displaystyle Z_{2}\big|\bigl({\bm{Z}}_{-2}={\bm{z}}_{-2}\bigr)\sim{\cal{TN}}_{T_{2}}\Bigl(\frac{4}{5}z_{1},\frac{9}{25}\Bigr).

Method 2
: For implementing the first step of Algorithm
18
, we set
\bm{b}=\bm{0}
,
C=-\bm{I}_{2}
, and
N=2000
. For completing the second step, the Cholesky decomposition
D
, of
\Sigma
and its inverse, are
D=\bigl[(1,4/5)^{\top},(0,3/5)^{\top}\bigr]
and
D^{-1}=\bigl[(1,-4/3)^{\top},(0,5/3)^{\top}\bigr]
, respectively. Hence, for
\bm{\mu}=(0,0)^{\top}
, we have
\bm{\alpha}=D^{-1}\bm{\mu}=(0,0)^{\top}
and
E=CD=\bigl[(-1,-4/5)^{\top},(0,-3/5)^{\top}\bigr]
where
{E}_{-1}=(0,-3/5)^{\top}
and
{E}_{-2}=(-1,-4/5)^{\top}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle T_{1}=\biggl\{z_{1}\in\mathbb{R}\bigg|\left[\begin{matrix}-1\\
-\frac{4}{5}\end{matrix}\right]z_{1}\leq-\left[\begin{matrix}0\\
-\frac{3}{5}\end{matrix}\right]z_{2}\bigg\},

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{z_{1}\in\mathbb{R}\Big|z_{1}\geq 0,z_{1}\geq-\frac{3}{4}z_{2}\Big\},\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle=\Bigl[\max\Bigl\{0,-\frac{3}{4}z_{2}\Bigr\},\infty\Bigr),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

(5.87)

and

\displaystyle T_{2}=\biggl\{z_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\bigg|\left[\begin{matrix}0\\
-\frac{3}{5}\end{matrix}\right]z_{2}\leq-\left[\begin{matrix}-1\\
-\frac{4}{5}\end{matrix}\right]z_{1}\bigg\},

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{z_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\Big|z_{2}\geq-\frac{4}{3}z_{1}\Big\},\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle=\bigl[-\frac{4}{3}z_{1},\infty\bigr).\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

(5.88)

Using (
5.87
), (
5.88
), and
\bm{z}^{(0)}=(1,1)^{\top}
the third step of the Algorithm
18
is complete. Finally, the required full conditionals in the seventh step of this algorithm are given as follows.

\displaystyle Z_{1}\big|\bigl({\bm{Z}}_{-1}={\bm{z}}_{-1}\bigr)\sim{\cal{TN}}_{T_{1}}\bigl(\mu_{1},1\bigr),

\displaystyle Z_{2}\big|\bigl({\bm{Z}}_{-2}={\bm{z}}_{-2}\bigr)\sim{\cal{TN}}_{T_{2}}\Bigl(-\frac{4}{3}\mu_{1}+\frac{5}{3}\mu_{2},1\Bigr).
